The two 1931 Carrel articles remain **bibliographically verified but not yet read in full as primary texts**. New archival access routes have now been located.

### Primary-source identities

- Alexis Carrel, “The New Cytology,” *Science* 73(1890), 297–303 (20 March 1931), DOI 10.1126/science.73.1890.297, PMID 17811580. PubMed record: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17811580/. OpenAlex classifies this record as closed access: https://openalex.org/W2056014132.
- Alexis Carrel, “Physiological Time,” *Science* 74(1929), 618–621 (18 December 1931), DOI 10.1126/science.74.1929.618, PMID 17740867. PubMed record: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17740867/. OpenAlex classifies this record as closed access: https://openalex.org/W4235426938.

### Archive routes

1. **Georgetown University Manuscripts — Alexis Carrel Papers, GTM-530913, Box 14, Folder 7 (“Health”).** The finding-aid item list explicitly names “The New Cytology” (1931), “Inner Time” (circa 1931), and “Physiological Time” (circa 1931), among other mixed materials. Citation: “Health, Box: 14, Folder: 7. Alexis Carrel Papers, GTM-530913. Georgetown University Manuscripts, Booth Family Center for Special Collections.” Finding aid: https://findingaids.library.georgetown.edu/repositories/15/archival_objects/1494026. Most manuscript collections are open to researchers, but restrictions may apply; off-site materials require a minimum of three days for retrieval, and researchers should contact the Booth Family Center in advance at speccoll@georgetown.edu. This is a **catalogue locator**, not evidence that the physical folder has been consulted or that all items are unrestricted.
2. **Bibliothèque de l’Académie nationale de médecine — Fonds Alexis Carrel.** The 2012 inventory identifies “The New Cytology,” *Science*, 20 March 1931, 73(1890), 297–303 as item **Carrel 11.1, no. 68**. Inventory PDF: https://bibliotheque.academie-medecine.fr/wp-content/uploads/2013/06/Carrel_Inventaire-version-d%C3%A9finitive.pdf. The inventory metadata is useful for a second repository locator; it does not provide the paper's text. The exact record was inspected on PDF page 76 (zero-indexed viewer page 75).
3. **Publisher/journal access.** PubMed and OpenAlex confirm both articles' records; OpenAlex currently reports closed access for both. A library subscription or interlibrary/document-delivery route may therefore be needed if the archive copies cannot be consulted.

### Refined reading of Carrel through the secondary source

Rosine Kelz, “Tissue culture and biological time: Alexis Carrel, Henri Bergson and the plasticity of living matter,” *BioSocieties* 17, 442–460 (2022; online 13 March 2021), DOI 10.1057/s41292-020-00224-2, is open access: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1057/s41292-020-00224-2. The “Carrel and physiological time” section (especially notes/passages around pp. 457–458 of the article) reports that Carrel distinguished physiological duration from psychological time and treated physiological time as a material substratum for psychological and social time. It is linked to the organism's relation with its milieu. He describes living bodies as processes in which the past persists and is “entered in the tissues”; dead organs and static histological sections are useful abstractions, not the complete living organism. Kelz quotes Carrel's “Physiological Time” p. 619 and “The New Cytology” p. 298.

Carrel's use of “fourth dimension” is therefore **physiological/processual time within a space-time account**, not a claim that organisms extend along a fourth *spatial* axis. It is nevertheless a useful comparison: Carrel and the user's histological proposal both challenge static anatomical sections as exhaustive descriptions of living structure. Their constructions differ: Carrel emphasizes organism–milieu-dependent physiological duration and tissue process; the user's proposal adds a literal branching physical 4D continuity across apparently separate individuals. No evidence from Carrel is to be treated as proof of that latter topology.

**Historical caution:** Kelz also documents the disputed status of Carrel's celebrated “immortal chick-heart” tissue cultures. The claimed indefinite survival is doubtful; Hayflick's later findings and possible introduction of new viable cells via embryonic extract or contamination are discussed. Do not repeat “immortality” as an established experimental result. Broader racist/eugenic and political elements in Carrel's later work must be contextualized separately and not imputed to the two biological-time articles without direct textual evidence.

**Next Carrel operation:** request/check the Georgetown Box 14 Folder 7 material and/or Academy of Medicine item Carrel 11.1 no. 68; obtain and read the two primary articles with page-level notes; compare Carrel's treatment of temporal persistence, tissue constitution and organism–milieu relation with Woodger and the private histological model.

## 46. Woodger's organism-as-event passage and the processual bridge — 2026-11-24

### Primary book and accessible contents evidence

Joseph Henry Woodger, *Biological Principles: A Critical Study* (1929), London: Routledge & Kegan Paul. The accessible Routledge/Book preview (52 pages) provides the contents but does **not** include the substantive pages 299–301. The relevant contents are visible in the preview's contents page (viewer page 11, zero-indexed page 10):

- Chapter VI, section 6: “The importance of time in biology. ‘Taking time seriously.’ Organism and history,” book p. 299.
- Chapter VI, section 7: “The assimilation of time and space. The organism as an event,” book p. 301.
- Section 8: “Division. Repetition of spatial parts. Types of division. Symmetry,” book p. 303.

Preview: https://api.pageplace.de/preview/DT0400.9781317830962_A23912257/preview-9781317830962_A23912257.pdf. This is a **contents-level primary-book locator**, not a reading of the cited primary pages.

### Page-specific secondary quotation

John Dupré and Daniel J. Nicholson, “A Manifesto for a Processual Philosophy of Biology” (2018), in *Everything Flows: Towards a Processual Philosophy of Biology*, quote Woodger's *Biological Principles* (1929a: 219): “An organism, whatever else it may be, is an event—something happening. It is temporally as well as spatially extended. It has temporal as well as spatial parts.” The full passage uses a pet dog on successive days to explain temporal parts and identity through continuity. The authors also cite Woodger 1929a:299 for his call to consider temporal relations and note that his work attempted to extend Whitehead's views to biology. Accessible text: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/322836168_A_Manifesto_for_a_Processual_Philosophy_of_Biology.

Keep the quoted locus **p. 219** distinct from the contents' later Chapter VI time/event sections at pp. 299–301. These may represent separate discussions. Do not silently relocate the quotation to p. 301. Retrieve the full book and inspect both loci before claiming direct primary-text verification.

### Interpretation and connection to the active model

Woodger provides a particularly useful conceptual bridge between static anatomy and the organism as an extended event. Temporal parts are to the organism's identity as spatial parts are to its anatomy; the living entity is not exhausted by one present-time slice. This fits a processual reading of biological continuity and invites careful analysis of criteria for diachronic identity.

It does **not**, by itself, posit an additional spatial dimension or say that different people's bodies are physically connected as branches of one larger organism. Its temporal extension is compatible with ordinary spacetime and processual biology. The user's proposal adds a stronger and distinct claim: a literal branching physical/histological 4D structure in which apparently separate individuals are connected across time and potentially form distal appendages of a larger organism. Treat Woodger as a formal/conceptual comparator, not as prior evidence for that claim.

### Provisional comparison matrix

| Strand | What is extended / connected? | Meaning of “four-dimensional” | Relation to user's histological model |
|---|---|---|---|
| Carrel | Physiological processes, tissue constitution, organism–milieu relations and persistence of the past in living tissue | Physiological duration / space-time continuum | Shares the critique of static sections; no demonstrated branching inter-individual topology |
| Woodger | The organism as an event with spatial and temporal parts; identity through continuity | Temporal extension of organismal process | Strong conceptual bridge for temporal parts and identity; no extra spatial axis or macro-organism of separate individuals |
| User's histological proposal | Neural-core, layered tissue appendages and their proposed physical continuity/branching across time | Literal physical 4D structure, with a proposed additional spatial-temporal organization | Distinct working hypothesis requiring its own geometrical, anatomical and evidential specification |

**Next Woodger operation:** obtain full text or a library scan of *Biological Principles*; inspect pp. 219 and 299–303 directly; extract Woodger's exact account of time, history, organism-as-event, spatial/temporal part relations, division and symmetry. Then compare his concepts with current process philosophy and the user's model without collapsing their ontological commitments.

**Status:** Carrel and Woodger primary bibliographic/archival locators and secondary analyses are advanced; neither primary text's key passages have yet been fully read in this workshop. No Tock closure or `@` advance.


## 47. Woodger full primary text recovered and inspected — 2026-11-24

The earlier section 46 correctly identified the relevant book and secondary quotation, but its primary-text status is now superseded: the full OCR text for the 1929 book has been located in the Internet Archive and the central passages have now been read directly.

**Primary item:** J. H. Woodger, *Biological Principles: A Critical Study* (first published 1929; scan of the 1948 second impression with 1967 reissue notice), Internet Archive identifier `biologicalprinci0000jhwo`, 538 scanned pages. Item record: https://archive.org/details/biologicalprinci0000jhwo. Full OCR text: https://archive.org/stream/biologicalprinci0000jhwo/biologicalprinci0000jhwo_djvu.txt. The Archive item metadata identifies the 1929 first publication and the scanned edition details. The source read here is the primary book's OCR transcription; OCR can contain errors, so any quotation used in a formal paper should be cross-checked against the corresponding page image.

### Direct primary passages inspected

1. **Book p. 219 — organism as event and temporal/spatial parts.** Woodger analyzes the problem of “the same organism” through a pin-prick example: the organism is changed by the first stimulus, so a repeated stimulus is not applied to exactly the same organism. He then treats the organism as an event extended in space and time, with temporal parts as well as spatial parts. Yesterday's and today's dog are different temporal parts of one continuing dog, just as head and tail are different spatial parts. Identity is grounded in the particular continuity between temporal parts, not their numerical sameness. This is the primary locus quoted by Dupré and Nicholson (2018: Woodger 1929a, p. 219).

2. **Book pp. 299–300 — taking time seriously in biology.** Woodger compares the minimal spatial extension needed for an object to exist with the time required for an organism to manifest its organization. A frog abstracted from its history is no more concrete than a life-history abstracted from the frog. His memorable methodological point is that anatomy studies the organism in “timeless space” and thereby omits the time-dimension. A preserved frog is treated as a cross-section of the living organism's history, not as the complete living organism.

3. **Book pp. 301–302 — the four-dimensional event.** Woodger makes an unusually explicit distinction: assimilating space and time yields the notion of a four-dimensional event, but this does **not** mean time is simply “a fourth dimension of space.” Rather, space and time are both abstractions from a four-dimensional continuum; an event is a limited slab of space-time, and a living organism is such an event. He then analyzes different time-slices of the organism: their character varies with both the interval between slices and their position within the organism's total duration. In metazoa he distinguishes overlapping developmental and behaviour periods. Successive temporal slices differ in serial order; continued serial change is part of what constitutes the organism's persistence, and its cessation marks the cessation of life and the breakdown of organization. This is a direct primary source for temporal organismal extension, not for an additional spatial axis.

4. **Book pp. 303–306 — repetition, division, and biological relations.** Woodger treats development as temporal and spatial differentiation, with irreversible/serial temporal change and increasing elaboration of spatial organization. A pattern can be repeated spatially through division. He distinguishes two outcomes: repeated structures may remain in an organic relation as parts of one whole organism (part-producing division), or become two whole organisms (whole-producing division, such as identical twins). He emphasizes that the first yields a later temporal stage of the same organism now with two spatial parts; the second yields two organisms. He further says that offspring may remain in physiological relation with a parent after separation without remaining in an organic part-whole relation. This is a sharp conceptual distinction between *organic membership* and *physiological relation*.

5. **Book pp. 306–307 — symmetry across space and time.** Woodger treats symmetry as spatial repetition and rhythm as repetition in time, both relevant to biological organization. He distinguishes simultaneous repetition in bilateral/radial symmetry, successive emergence in spiral symmetry, and successive paired repetition in metameric segmentation. A footnote explicitly recommends that symmetry be extended from a four-dimensional point of view, and names D'Arcy Thompson's *Growth and Form*, Jaeger's *Lectures on the Principle of Symmetry*, and Whitehead's work on four-dimensional geometry as relevant reading routes. This is a direct new bridge to biological morphology and mathematical symmetry.

### Why this matters to the private histological proposal

Woodger gives us a historically grounded set of distinctions that are highly useful for clarifying, but do not prove, the user's construction:

- **Temporal persistence:** a persisting organism is a temporally extended event whose different temporal parts are not identical but are related by a continuity rule.
- **Part-whole membership:** not every physiological or causal relation makes something a part of an organism. Woodger distinguishes products that remain organically related parts of one whole from products that are now separate whole organisms but retain physiological relations.
- **Branching and division:** ordinary biological division can increase the number of spatial parts within one temporally extended organism, or produce distinct organisms. These are not interchangeable cases.
- **4D symmetry:** body-plan repetition may be analyzed across time as well as space; Woodger explicitly suggests extending symmetry from a four-dimensional point of view.
- **Histological organization:** Woodger discusses how tissue territories and cellular relations can elaborate beyond properties of individual cells. This may be a useful conventional-biology comparator for the user's insistence on histological relations rather than reducing the model to individual filaments or genetic inheritance.

**Key diagnostic question for our model:** when the user's proposed 4D tendrils connect apparently separate people across time, is that relation intended as (a) ordinary genealogy/physiological relation between distinct organisms, (b) part-producing spatial branching within one larger temporally extended organism, or (c) a third kind of intertemporal organic relation not captured by Woodger's conventional developmental/reproductive categories? The proposal appears to aim beyond (a), but its precise criterion for organic continuity still needs formal specification. Do not decide the answer by importing Woodger's taxonomy as a constraint; use it to reveal exactly what the new proposal adds.

### Conceptual boundary and source status

Woodger's “four-dimensional event” is an organism within a space-time continuum. He explicitly rejects interpreting it as merely time added as a fourth spatial dimension. His account is therefore close to a conventional spacetime/processual ontology and distinct from a model asserting an additional spatial direction. The fact that he also invites four-dimensional symmetry analysis makes the text relevant to our geometry programme, but it does not make his biology an account of a higher-spatial-dimensional superorganism.

**Updated primary-text status:** *Biological Principles* has now been read directly in OCR around pp. 219, 299–306. Page-image verification is still outstanding for any formal quotation. The contents and direct text are consistent about the relevant section locations. Dupré and Nicholson (2018) remains a useful secondary synthesis but is no longer the only route to Woodger's central claims.

**Next:** inspect scan images for pp. 219 and 299–307 if a formal quotation or exact punctuation is required; follow Woodger's explicit four-dimensional symmetry footnote into D'Arcy Thompson, Jaeger and Whitehead; and use the part-producing/whole-producing distinction when refining the user's proposed branching organism. No Tock closure or `@` advance.


## 48. Four-dimensional symmetry, morphology and multi-timescale dimensionality — 2026-11-24

Woodger's newly inspected 1929 footnote (book p. 306) opens a useful route from temporal organismal organization into morphology and mathematical symmetry. He explicitly recommends extending the treatment of symmetry from a four-dimensional point of view, naming D'Arcy Thompson's *Growth and Form*, F. M. Jaeger's *Lectures on the Principle of Symmetry*, and Whitehead's discussions of four-dimensional geometry.

### Primary and near-primary source map

1. **F. M. Jaeger, *Lectures on the Principle of Symmetry and Its Applications in All Natural Sciences* (1917; 2nd augmented ed. 1920).** The Biodiversity Heritage Library identifies a 1917 Toronto-held edition and a 1920 University of California Libraries edition, the latter augmented with 173 diagrams and three portraits. Both are marked not in copyright and have digitized-page/OCR access. The Google Books record for the 1920 edition includes biological observations about symmetry, including comparative animal symmetry and evolutionary changes. BHL metadata: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/bibliography/22096 (1920); https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/bibliography/19096 (1917). Full item record: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/62582. **Status:** located from Woodger's direct footnote and bibliographically inventoried; Jaeger's relevant pages have not yet been read. Do not assume that the book itself develops a 4D symmetry theory simply because Woodger cites it in a four-dimensional-symmetry footnote.

2. **D'Arcy Wentworth Thompson, *On Growth and Form* (1917), Chapter XVII, “On the Theory of Transformations, or the Comparison of Related Forms.”** Project Gutenberg's catalogue identifies the book and the chapter; the original 1917 chapter is a primary source for Thompson's grid-based comparison of related biological forms. https://www.gutenberg.org/files/55264/55264-h/55264-h.htm. The first edition's Chapter XVII begins at p. 719. **Status:** source located, but primary chapter not yet read directly in this pass.

3. **Wallace Arthur, “D’Arcy Thompson’s Morphological Transformations: Issues of Causality and Dimensionality,” *Biological Theory* 20, 119–130 (2025; published online 21 June 2023), DOI 10.1007/s13752-023-00438-0.** Open access: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s13752-023-00438-0. Arthur argues that Thompson's familiar diagrams depict two-dimensional outlines of adult forms, and that even extending these to three spatial dimensions leaves an inadequate temporal account if one tries to interpret them as physical causal transformations. He distinguishes **developmental transformations** within one individual's lifetime from **evolutionary transformations/repatterning** over multiple generations. Drawing on Scott F. Gilbert (2007, p. 371), Arthur describes a five-dimensional evo-devo representation: three spatial dimensions plus two temporal scales (developmental and paleontological/evolutionary). Arthur immediately qualifies that these are strictly two distinct timescales, not literally two independent time dimensions; he nevertheless finds the “five dimensions” language useful for exposing analyses that ignore one of the temporal scales.

4. **Scott F. Gilbert, “Fate Maps, Gene Expression Maps, and the Evidentiary Structure of Evolutionary Developmental Biology” (2007), in *From Embryology to Evo-Devo*, pp. 358–374, esp. p. 371.** The chapter's conclusion calls for a five-dimensional representation in evolutionary developmental biology, placing the familiar four space-time dimensions in the context of paleontological time. An accessible author-uploaded copy/record is indexed at https://www.academia.edu/102758256/Fate_Maps_Gene_Expression_Maps_and_the_Evidentiary_Structure_of_Evolutionary_Developmental_Biology. Arthur's article is an accessible, critically engaged route to this passage.

### Conceptual distinctions to preserve

This research now reveals at least four meanings of dimensionality that can coexist in biological writing without making the same claim:

- **Ontological spatial dimensionality:** an organism or tissue is proposed to extend into an additional spatial direction. This remains the stronger higher-space reading associated with Hinton and the user's own distinct histological hypothesis.
- **Spatiotemporal event dimensionality:** an ordinary organism occupies a four-dimensional space-time continuum (three spatial dimensions plus time); Woodger explicitly frames the organism as a limited event/slab and rejects the careless phrase that time is merely a fourth spatial dimension.
- **Morphological representation dimensionality:** a biological form, projection, grid, map or diagram has a certain number of representational dimensions. A 2D grid transformation of adult body outlines does not contain all dimensions necessary to represent real ontogeny or phylogeny as physical causal processes.
- **Multi-timescale evo-devo dimensionality:** a model must represent both within-lifetime development and across-generation evolutionary change. The “five-dimensional” vocabulary can denote three spatial dimensions plus two temporal *scales*. Arthur explicitly cautions against treating the two temporal scales as two independent physical time dimensions.

### Relevance to the user's histological model

The Woodger–Thompson–Arthur sequence is useful for clarifying what a biologically meaningful 4D representation must contain. Woodger supplies the temporally extended organism and 4D symmetry problem; Thompson supplies mathematical morphology, geometric transformations, and organismal shape comparison; Arthur exposes the danger of treating a static shape transformation as a complete causal account when development and evolution occur on different timescales.

For the user's proposal, this suggests a methodological check: separate the geometry of an individual 4D organismal tendril from (a) its ordinary spatial morphology at a given temporal slice, (b) the developmental history of that tendril, and (c) evolutionary changes in the organization of such tendrils across generations. A model that projects all three into one undifferentiated “time” coordinate risks conflating distinct processes. Conversely, adding an extra spatial dimension must be a separately justified geometric claim, not an inference from the fact that biology needs temporal and morphological coordinates.

### New biological symmetry questions

- How should ordinary bilateral/radial symmetry, serial segmentation, spiral repetition and rhythm be distinguished when represented across time?
- Can 4D symmetry transformations describe the relation between successive spatial arrangements without implying that a literal spatially higher-dimensional organism exists?
- How does Woodger's distinction between part-producing and whole-producing division alter the interpretation of branching biological structures across ontogeny and phylogeny?
- Does Thompson's mathematical morphology offer useful local geometric tools for tissue and body-form comparison while remaining silent about the user's larger 4D continuity hypothesis?
- How should a multi-timescale representation track a persistent organismal pattern while its spatial parts, developmental state and lineage context change?

**Next:** read Woodger's named Jaeger source at the relevant sections; read Thompson's 1917 Chapter XVII directly; inspect Arthur's complete dimensionality argument alongside Gilbert p. 371; compare their use of shape, time, symmetry and causality with the user's histological model. Keep this as current biological/history reconnaissance and not as the beginning of the deferred category-theory or dedicated spiritualist-reading programmes. No Tock closure or `@` advance.


## 49. Jaeger and D'Arcy Thompson: symmetry as biological morphology, not a hidden fourth axis — 2026-11-24

This pass follows the explicit four-dimensional-symmetry footnote in Woodger's *Biological Principles* (1929, p. 306). It establishes what the cited morphology/symmetry sources contribute and what they do not.

### Jaeger: a broad symmetry programme across natural sciences

**Primary bibliographic record:** F. M. Jaeger, *Lectures on the Principle of Symmetry and Its Applications in All Natural Sciences*, first edition 1917; second augmented edition, Amsterdam: Elsevier, 1920, xii + 348 pages, 173 diagrams and three portraits. The 1920 edition is held by University of California Libraries and is catalogued by the Biodiversity Heritage Library as not in copyright: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/bibliography/22096 and https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/62582. The first edition is also catalogued in BHL (University of Toronto holding): https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/58808.

**Contemporary reception:** the 6 June 1918 *Nature* review, “Symmetry in Nature,” describes Jaeger's first four chapters as covering the nature, aesthetic value and laws of symmetry; symmetry as a mathematical problem; reflection/inversion and mirror-image repetition; and deriving possible symmetry types using groups of movements. It reports application to the morphology of crystals, plants and animals, including examples of highly symmetrical radiolaria and other organisms. The review further describes Jaeger's broad treatment of enantiomorphism and optical activity. Indexed text of the 1918 review: https://dbc.wroc.pl/Content/29783/PDF/032288.pdf.

The 14 April 1921 *Nature* review confirms that the second edition is substantially the same as the first, with minor corrections and a few additions: https://www.nature.com/articles/107200e0. Google Books' indexed 1920 text supplies snippets defining symmetry in terms of geometrical regularity, repetition and periodic arrangement (p. 2), and discusses symmetry in living nature and evolutionary change in lower/higher animal groups (pp. 201, 203): https://books.google.com/books?id=o2tkAAAAIAAJ.

**Evidence limit:** Jaeger's primary text has been located and its OCR/download routes identified, but the BHL item-text endpoint returned HTTP 403 in this session; Google Books provides only snippets. Accordingly, do not call Jaeger's book directly read yet. Its title and Woodger's reference do not establish that Jaeger develops a four-dimensional symmetry theory. The secure present conclusion is narrower: Jaeger offers a broad mathematical and natural-scientific study of symmetry, including biological morphology, crystallography and molecular optical activity.

### Thompson: the 1917 transformation chapter read directly

**Primary text:** D'Arcy Wentworth Thompson, *On Growth and Form* (1917), Chapter XVII, “On the Theory of Transformations, or the Comparison of Related Forms,” beginning at first-edition p. 719. The original public-domain text was read in full enough to inspect its opening argument and coordinate-transformation method: https://readingroo.ms/5/5/2/6/55264/55264-h/55264-h.htm. A readable transcription of the same public-domain chapter is available at https://redlectern.com/read/on-growth-and-form/8. Project Gutenberg's contents confirms the first-edition chapter title and page: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/55264/55264-h/55264-h.htm.

**Primary findings (first-edition pagination):**

- **pp. 719–722:** Thompson argues that mathematical description of form can move morphology from static shape toward dynamic relations, where a form can be treated as a diagram of forces in equilibrium and differences between related forms as indicating the magnitude/direction of forces that could produce the change. He places organic form within the wider science of form and rejects a categorical separation between organic and inorganic mathematical form; the complexity of living forms lies in the interacting forces and resistances.
- **pp. 723–725:** the morphologist often compares related forms rather than fully defining each shape. Thompson uses Cartesian coordinates to inscribe an organism or part as a function of x,y, then deforms the coordinate system; the figure follows the transformed grid. This is a mathematical comparison of related outlines, intended to suggest a possible deformation and the forces capable of producing it. Thompson limits useful comparisons to relatively simple transformations between organisms that are manifestly related and belong to the same zoological class.
- **Interpretive boundary:** the grid transformation is a formal representation of shape correspondence. It is not, by itself, a demonstrated causal history or a literal physical path through a fourth dimension. Thompson's confidence that the diagram can help infer force direction/magnitude should be distinguished from proof that the depicted transformation occurred as a physical process.

### Arthur's dimensional-sufficiency critique

Wallace Arthur, “D'Arcy Thompson's Morphological Transformations: Issues of Causality and Dimensionality,” *Biological Theory* 20, 119–130 (2025; published online 21 June 2023), DOI 10.1007/s13752-023-00438-0, open access: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s13752-023-00438-0.

Arthur distinguishes two different things often blurred by the iconic diagrams:

1. **Developmental transformations:** physical shape changes within one individual's lifetime.
2. **Evolutionary transformations / developmental repatterning:** changes across generations in the developmental process or its outcomes.

He argues that Thompson's famous diagrams compare adult forms of different species and do not, as drawn, represent the actual physical causal process that produces those different adults. A three-dimensional spatial view alone is not enough if the relevant temporal axis is underspecified. Drawing on Scott F. Gilbert (2007, p. 371), Arthur uses a five-dimensional evo-devo representation: three spatial dimensions plus two temporal scales, developmental and evolutionary/palaeontological time. Crucially, Arthur explicitly cautions that these are **two temporal scales**, not two physically independent time dimensions. His point is methodological: one cannot explain the evolution of development while ignoring either the within-life developmental process or the across-generations changes to that process.

Arthur also qualifies Thompson's causal confidence: the transformation grids may be insightful abstractions, but the exact forces responsible are not specified, and subsequent analysis of the classic crab example has found oversimplifications and misleading mappings. Thus the diagrams should not be read as a complete mechanistic explanation.

### A useful three-level separation for our work

The Woodger–Jaeger–Thompson–Arthur chain now suggests keeping three different dimensions of biological analysis explicit:

- **Organismal spacetime continuity (Woodger):** a living organism as a temporally extended event, with spatial and temporal parts, serial change and persistence.
- **Morphological symmetry and shape relations (Jaeger/Thompson):** patterns, repetitions, transformations and correspondences between biological forms; a geometric relation is not automatically a trajectory or causal process.
- **Multiple biological timescales (Arthur/Gilbert):** within-organism development versus evolutionary change across generations; these are distinct scales of time, not two new physical axes.

The user's histological 4D proposal should not be reduced to Thompson's shape transformations, nor should a 5D evo-devo representation be mistaken for a fourth spatial dimension. Instead, these readings supply analytic tools and guardrails: distinguish a 3D cross-section at a given time from a temporally extended organism; distinguish shape correspondence from physical continuity; and distinguish continuity within one organism from evolutionary change across generations.

### Immediate implications for the private histological model

1. Treat the proposed neural-core/tissue-sheath appendage as a spatially organized histological form at a given time-slice.
2. Treat its extension and temporal branching as a separate claim about physical continuity through time. It requires an explicit criterion for when a later/earlier region is an organic part of one larger whole rather than merely a related organism.
3. Keep ontogenetic development and phylogenetic/evolutionary change as different timescales. A family-tree diagram may encode reproductive history, but that alone does not establish the user's stronger proposed physical 4D part-whole continuity.
4. Use symmetry (bilateral, radial, serial, spiral, metameric) to characterize relations among parts or temporal slices, not to infer an unseen spatial axis without additional premises.
5. For causal claims, specify the physical process that realizes a proposed transformation or coordinated action; a grid, projection or symmetry relation is a representation until a mechanism is supplied.

**Updated source status:** Thompson's 1917 Chapter XVII has been directly read from the public-domain text; Arthur's full open-access article has been inspected; Jaeger is located and its contemporary review and Google Books snippets inspected, but Jaeger's primary text is not yet fully read. No new primary four-dimensional biological organism claim has been identified here. No Tock closure or `@` advance.


## 50. Jaeger full-text primary-source inspection — symmetry in organismal morphology, movement, regeneration and phyllotaxis — 2026-11-24

The earlier section 49 classified Jaeger as located but not directly read. That status is now superseded in part: the full searchable OCR/text extraction of the 1920 second edition was opened and inspected through the preface and the first six chapters, through approximately printed page 166. The 1917 first edition is also held in the Biodiversity Heritage Library. The 1920 volume is identified as not in copyright by BHL.

**Primary scan consulted:** F. M. Jaeger, *Lectures on the Principle of Symmetry and Its Applications in All Natural Sciences*, 2nd augmented ed. (Amsterdam: Elsevier, 1920), University of California Libraries scan. BHL catalogue/item: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/62582; searchable PDF/text rendition: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/38/Lectures_on_the_principle_of_symmetry_and_its_applications_in_all_natural_sciences_%28IA_lecturesonprinci00jaegrich%29.pdf. The parsed scan provides printed page markers and OCR, but page images could not be rendered: the screenshot service rejected the 57.8 MB PDF as too large; a separate 1918 *Nature* review PDF screenshot attempt was also blocked by the service. Accordingly, primary-text reading below is based on the scan's searchable text, not visual page-image verification. Exact quotations, figures, botanical identifications and technical terms should be checked against page images before formal quotation/publication.

### 1. Jaeger's own methodological caveat — preface, pp. viii–ix

Jaeger says that his purpose is not an exhaustive mathematical treatise, but to show the usefulness of symmetry for morphological description and chemical/physical phenomena. He notes that the biological/morphological examples are **preliminary** and “have no claim to absolute correctness”: as a chemist or physicist, he cannot judge exact morphological classifications from drawings alone unless he has studied those subjects in detail. He explicitly invites biologists to correct or improve them. This is an important self-limitation: Jaeger is offering a mathematical descriptive framework and illustrative examples, not claiming that his biological classifications are definitive.

The 1918 *Nature* review corroborates the breadth of the work: the first four chapters develop the symmetry principle and group-based classification; the biological applications include morphology of crystals, plants and animals; later chapters treat phyllotaxis and Pasteur's law, molecular optical activity, physiological action of enantiomorphous antipodes and asymmetric synthesis. The reviewer calls the latter stereochemical topics among the book's strongest sections.

### 2. Living forms and the historical "higher-to-lower symmetry" claim — §6, printed pp. 55–58

Jaeger classifies many familiar higher animals (including humans, quadrupeds, birds and insects) as bilateral forms possessing one plane of reflection, while listing lower animals such as Blastoidea, corals, radiolarians, Discomedusae and ascidians as often possessing higher rotational symmetry and multiple planes of symmetry.

He then proposes that morphological evolution in living nature appears to proceed from higher toward lower symmetry. Crucially, he does **not** present this as a settled law: he says that the frequent bilateral symmetry of higher animals has not yet been rationally explained. He tentatively associates it with animal locomotion. Sedentary organisms may benefit from presenting repeated body parts to the environment in many directions, whereas freely moving animals may benefit from bilateral organization and mechanical stability. He notes that plant organs are generally more symmetrical than animal organs, while immobile crystals provide the most perfect symmetry.

This is a historical biological hypothesis, not a reliable modern evolutionary generalization. Jaeger's “higher/lower animals” language reflects early twentieth-century taxonomic and evolutionary ranking. Its proposed direction of morphological evolution should be treated critically, not carried forward as a contemporary principle. The more durable observation is that symmetry type is correlated with organismal organization and lifestyle in ways that require explanation.

### 3. Bilateral symmetry, regeneration and directional responses — §6, pp. 57–58

Jaeger connects symmetry to dynamical biological phenomena and cites experimental work by Przibram and Jacques Loeb. He describes cases where bilateral symmetry, disrupted by injury or amputation, is restored, sometimes at the expense of normal development or by degeneration of the remaining organ. He interprets the body as an organization of two symmetrical and mutually independent halves, with regeneration phenomena tending to preserve or restore symmetry.

He also discusses responses to asymmetrically applied stimuli: phototropism, geotropism and chemotropism. Following Loeb, he describes tropisms as functional dissymmetries with directional orientation and, in some cases, restoration of symmetry.

**Use with caution:** these are Jaeger's historical summaries and interpretations of the biological literature available to him, not a modern experimental review. The interesting conceptual distinction is between (a) structural symmetry of the body plan, (b) functional asymmetry generated by a stimulus, and (c) developmental/regenerative processes that may preserve, restore or reorganize structure. These should not be collapsed into one simple "symmetry-maintenance" mechanism.

### 4. Organism as a growing, potentially unlimited system — Chapter VI, §29, pp. 159–166

Jaeger distinguishes the symmetry of an individual organ from the mutual arrangement of an organism's organs. Because a plant or animal continuously increases its volume through growth, he suggests that a living organism can be regarded theoretically as an unlimited system, analogous to a crystal's indefinitely repeated spatial lattice; finite lifespan and other limits arise from contingent or secondary factors.

He then develops plant phyllotaxis as an example of regular spatial arrangement:

- Leaves occur in spiral series around an axis; he distinguishes orthostichies (aligned rows) and parastichies (spiral series).
- A cycle is described by a fraction recording the number of spiral turns and the number of inserted leaves.
- The ratios include (1/2, 1/3, 2/5, 3/8, 5/13, 8/21,ldots), which converge toward the golden-section divergence.
- Jaeger describes the idealized angular divergence as approximately (137^circ 30' 28''), linking it to the arrangement of leaves, sunflower fruits, pineapple bracts and related structures.
- He notes an unresolved question: whether the apparent optimization of exposure to light and air is teleological fiction or reflects real adaptive relations. He cites Wiesner on light absorption.

This is an especially useful bridge to geometry and morphogenesis: Jaeger treats an organismal structure as a repeated spatial organization on a surface, with a generative spiral and lattice-like relations. It is not a fourth spatial dimension or a temporal worldtube model. The apparent continuity across successive leaf insertions is the development of a spatial pattern; it should not be conflated with Woodger's temporal organism or the user's stronger 4D histological continuity proposal.

### 5. Molecular chirality and physiological specificity — Chapters VIII and IX, contents-level status

The second edition's contents explicitly identify a substantial stereochemical/biological programme beginning at printed p. 204: Pasteur's law, fission of racemoids, molecular dissymmetry and optical activity, physiological action of antipodes, enzyme action, racemisation, asymmetric atoms, and chemical-molecular symmetry. Chapter IX (p. 283 onward) treats reactions of optically active and inactive compounds, reaction rates of antipodes, and natural/asymmetric synthesis. The 1918 *Nature* review likewise emphasizes Jaeger's treatment of Pasteur's law and asymmetric synthesis of organic molecules.

However, the searchable PDF text extraction available in this session appears to stop near printed p. 166. The detailed text of Chapters VIII and IX has **not yet been read directly**. Do not record Jaeger's specific argument about enzyme action or physiological differences between antipodes as read or verified. Those contents are a clear next primary-reading target.

### 6. What Jaeger contributes to our active inquiry

Jaeger is now more than a generic symmetry bibliography. He supplies a contemporaneous bridge among:

1. **Morphological symmetry:** group-theoretic description of animal and plant form.
2. **Functional symmetry/dissymmetry:** locomotion, stimulus orientation and tropisms.
3. **Regenerative dynamics:** restoration or reorganization after injury.
4. **Unlimited spatial organization:** phyllotaxis and other repeated patterns on cylindrical/conical surfaces.
5. **Molecular chirality:** Pasteur's law, optical activity and physiological specificity (located in the book's structure; full chapters still pending).

For the user's histological proposal, this suggests a useful separation of questions: what is the spatial symmetry of a tissue/appendage at one time-slice; what functional dissymmetries arise during action; what transformations occur through development/regeneration; and what molecular chiral constraints exist at the cell/molecular scale. None of those, alone or together, establishes an extra spatial axis or a branching inter-individual organism. But they identify the biological organization that any such proposal must describe.

### 7. Jaeger and the 4D question — do not overread Woodger's footnote

Jaeger's inspected chapters provide mathematical symmetry groups and applications to 3D morphology, crystals, living forms and molecular chirality. No explicit theory of four-dimensional biological symmetry has been located in the inspected primary text. Woodger's recommendation to extend symmetry from a four-dimensional point of view is a separate instruction in Woodger's book; Jaeger's treatise is a source on the symmetry principle, not presently evidence that Jaeger himself supplied a 4D organismal account. Until the remaining chapters and the exact wording of Woodger's footnote are verified, keep their roles distinct.

## 51. Jacques Loeb primary-text verification of Jaeger's symmetry–tropism link — 2026-11-24

Jaeger explicitly cites Jacques Loeb's *Dynamik der Lebenserscheinungen* (Leipzig, 1906) for the relation between bilateral symmetry and stimulus-directed movement. The English edition, *The Dynamics of Living Matter* (New York: Columbia University Press / Macmillan, 1906), was opened and its primary text inspected in the searchable scan.

**Primary scan:** https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f8/The_dynamics_of_living_matter_%28IA_dynamicsofliving00loeb%29.pdf  
**BHL catalogue:** https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/118293 (Cornell University Library; public domain). The book's author preface is dated 1 January 1906; it develops an explicitly physico-chemical/experimental conception of living processes.

### 1. Loeb's symmetry principle in his own words — Lecture VIII, pp. 138–140

Loeb says that organisms are generally symmetrical not only morphologically but also chemically or dynamically. Corresponding surface-points on the body possess, in his model, approximately the same qualitative and quantitative chemical substances; their chemical reactions therefore proceed at similar rates. Asymmetric points may have different chemical structures and reaction rates.

When an external field (light, electric current or other influence) strikes the two sides unequally, corresponding sensory/surface points undergo different chemical reactions, producing unequal tension in the muscles or contractile elements. The organism turns until corresponding points on the two sides are struck at the same angle; then the tension becomes equal and the body maintains its orientation.

Loeb supplies evidence cases rather than treating this only as a formal analogy:
- In one-eyed/one-sidedly blinded butterflies (*Vanessa antiopa*), blackening one eye produces circular locomotion in the predicted direction.
- In animals responding to light, one-sided stimulus changes muscle tone; equal stimulation of corresponding sensory points stabilizes the orientation.
- Similar principles are discussed for galvanotropism and chemotropism.

This is the primary mechanism behind Jaeger's brief description of “functional dissymmetries” and tropisms as mechanisms tending to restore definite symmetry conditions. The account is historical and should not be treated as a fully established modern neurophysiological law. Its importance for our genealogy is that Jaeger's reading of Loeb is substantially grounded in Loeb's own mechanistic account.

### 2. Same organism, different life stage and mode of organization — Lecture VII, pp. 124–126

Loeb describes *Eudendrium* as an organism observed first as a free-swimming, ciliated larva and shortly afterwards as an attached, sessile form from which a polyp grows. While free-swimming, larvae orient and swim toward one-sided light; after attachment, the developing polyp undergoes a plant-like heliotropic curvature in response to the same directional illumination. Loeb emphasizes that the same individual can be observed first behaving like a freely moving animal and then like a heliotropic plant.

This is especially useful as an ordinary biological example of a continuous organism changing its morphology, orientation and functional mode across development. It distinguishes a persistent organism from a fixed body-plan snapshot, and it demonstrates that the relevant symmetry axis may be reorganized by life stage and environmental context. It does not imply an extra spatial dimension or a connection between separate individuals.

### 3. Related but separate process: tissue-level tropisms

In Lecture VIII, pp. 157–159, Loeb remarks that tropisms and tropism-like compulsory reactions can contribute to the mutual arrangement of organs and tissues. He describes examples such as chromatophores moving onto blood vessels in a *Fundulus* embryo, and discusses proposed tropistic explanations for mesenchyme-cell migration during sea-urchin gastrulation. These examples are not demonstrations that every tissue arrangement is governed by tropisms; Loeb labels some mechanisms uncertain. But they show that his framework was not restricted to whole-organism locomotion: he considered directional response and cell movement relevant to tissue organization and morphogenesis.

### 4. Regeneration as a separate research route

Loeb's *Dynamics of Living Matter* includes Lecture XI, “On the Dynamics of Regenerative Processes,” from p. 199: heteromorphosis and regeneration in *Tubularia*, *Cerianthus*, planarians, the influence of the central nervous system, correlation in regeneration, environmental conditions, reversible processes, and the distribution of regenerative capacity across the animal kingdom. The contents and primary text are present in the scan, but this regeneration lecture has **not yet been read in detail** during this pass. It is a next source-reading target, particularly relevant to Jaeger's claim that bilateral symmetry may be restored following injury or amputation.

### 5. Relation to the user's histological 4D proposal

The Loeb–Jaeger connection adds an important intermediate level to the model-comparison framework:

- **Static anatomical symmetry:** the spatial arrangement of corresponding tissues/parts.
- **Dynamic physiological symmetry:** corresponding sides or regions have similar chemical states/reaction capacities.
- **Stimulus-induced functional asymmetry:** a directional field produces unequal local reactions and action.
- **Reorientation/restoration:** organismal movement or tissue change can restore equal stimulation or a preferred organization.
- **Developmental reorganization:** the same organism can transition between distinct modes of morphology and behaviour.

For the user's distal-appendage hypothesis, this suggests asking separately whether a proposed coordinated action is due to (a) local stimulus-response asymmetry within one ordinary organism, (b) developmental or physiological coordination across time, or (c) the stronger proposed 4D structural linkage among regions that appear to be distinct organisms. Loeb provides a historical model for (a) and an example of (b); he does not evidence (c).

Any exact quotation for publication must be checked against the original scan page.

## 52. Przibram and Wilson: experimental reversal of lateral claw asymmetry — 2026-11-24

This source pass sharpens the Jaeger–Loeb thread. The relevant phenomenon is **not restoration of equal bilateral symmetry**. In *Alpheus* and several other decapod crustaceans, the asymmetric differentiation of a paired appendage can be transferred from one side of the body to the other after injury. The animal remains heterochelic (one dominant claw and one lesser claw), but which side carries the dominant morphology changes.

### A. Przibram's primary synthesis, 1909

Hans Przibram, *Experimental-Zoologie*, II: *Regeneration: Eine Zusammenfassung der durch Versuche ermittelten Gesetzmässigkeiten tierischer Wieder-Erzeugung (Nachwachsen, Umformung, Missbildung)* (Leipzig/Wien: Franz Deuticke, 1909), VIII+338+[16] pp. Primary scanned text, with the 16 lithographic plates missing from the digital copy:

- Catalogue and holding locator: Polish Academy of Sciences / IBD PAN, call no. 11735/2: https://www.rcin.org.pl/dlibra/publication/150330/edition/120492
- Full searchable scan/OCR PDF: https://rcin.org.pl/Content/120492/PDF/WA488_150330_2011735-2_Przibram_Exp.Zool-Regeneration-BRAK-TABLIC.pdf
- The title page and contents identify it as the second part of Przibram's experimental zoology series. The preface says the synthesis incorporates literature through 1908. The book describes a broad comparative experimental zoology of regeneration across protozoa, coelenterates, echinoderms, worms, arthropods, molluscs, urochordates and vertebrates.

**Introductory taxonomy (printed pp. 1–3; OCR lines 388–453):**
- Przibram distinguishes wound healing from regeneration in the stricter sense, which grows the lost part from the wound surface.
- Some organisms restore form through reduction toward a more embryonic condition and subsequent reorganization. He uses “morphallaxis” for formation of a whole from a small piece by transformation/reorganization.
- He distinguishes **correlation/correlative regulation**, where a regenerative process affects a distant part of the same organism, and **compensation/compensatory regulation**, where a related body part develops more strongly to make up for the loss elsewhere. He gives the example of injury to one antler followed by reduction of the other at the next antler change, and of compensatory enlargement of a remaining claw in crayfish.
- Regeneration is not a uniform drive toward a single ideal form: outcomes depend on organism, part, injury, developmental stage and environmental/physiological conditions.

**Decapod chela asymmetry (printed pp. 101–105; OCR lines 4692–4865):**
- Przibram says that removing the smaller claw of certain decapods had proved a fruitful route for studying the influence of one body side upon the other (p. 101).
- He distinguishes **homoeochely** (right and left chelae alike in size and form) from **heterochely** (asymmetric development of the paired chelae).
- Following Stahr, he abbreviates the massive “Knoten- oder Knackschere” as the **K claw** (knobbly/crushing claw) and the slender “Zähnchen- oder Zwickschere” as the **Z claw** (small/toothed or pinching claw). These are morphological/functional types, not merely big versus small labels in every decapod.
- In suitable heterochelous species, when the K claw is removed, the undamaged contralateral Z claw may develop into a K claw over subsequent molts, while the original K-side regenerates a new Z claw. This is **Scherenumkehr**, claw reversal (pp. 101–102).
- Przibram cites *Alpheus* species and other taxa including *Athanas*, *Carcinus*, *Callianassa*, *Portunus* and *Typton*. Results vary: some species regenerate directly; some reverse; some show transient stages with two Z claws; outcomes can depend on sex, size, age, which claw is removed, and whether both chelae or nerves are operated on.
- Reversal is generally more readily produced in younger animals; in larger animals it can be slower or absent. When both claws are removed, direct regeneration often occurs rather than the same reversal. The exact conditions are not resolved in all cases; Przibram explicitly calls for further experimental analysis (pp. 102–105).
- Przibram discusses effects of nerve sections and differences in regeneration speed. This prevents a simplistic claim that all regeneration is independent of neural input.

**Interpretive point:** what is maintained is not bilateral equality but a differentiated *pair* of appendages. The functional/morphological asymmetry migrates from one side to the other. Repeated removal of the newly dominant claw can reverse the pattern again. This is a particularly clear case of compensatory correlation between paired parts of one animal.

### B. Wilson's original experimental report, 1903: located, not yet read

Edmund B. Wilson, “Notes on the Reversal of Asymmetry in the Regeneration of the Chela in *Alpheus heterochelis*,” *Biological Bulletin* 4 (1902–1903), p. 197 onward. The volume IV table of contents explicitly lists the article at p. 197; a contemporary bibliography gives pp. 197–210, while a later bibliography in Herrick gives pp. 197–214. Treat the end page as **bibliographically inconsistent** until the original article is checked. The complete scanned *Biological Bulletin* volume IV is here: https://file.iflora.cn/fastdfs/group1/M00/63/B3/wKhnoF15ugWASWddATcI2K5PnDc835.pdf. Its searchable text extraction in the present session did not expose Wilson's article body, only the contents entry. Therefore the article is **located but not directly read**.

### C. Contemporary synthesis and independent context: Herrick, 1911

Francis Hobart Herrick, *Natural History of the American Lobster*, U.S. Bureau of Fisheries Bulletin (1911), digitized government publication: https://spo.nmfs.noaa.gov/sites/default/files/pdf-content/fish-bull/fb29.6.pdf. The chapter on the great forceps (especially printed pp. 276–278 in the scan) gives a useful contemporary synthesis of Przibram, Wilson, Zeleny and Emmel:

- Herrick describes the *Alpheus* snapping/hammer claw and smaller opposite claw. After loss of the large claw, the smaller contralateral claw can grow into the snapping type, while the amputated side regenerates the simpler small type.
- He reports that Wilson found sex-dependent differences in the timing/type of the regenerated claw: the male's small chela changed more rapidly because it was further advanced in the direction of the large form.
- Removing the smaller claw, or both claws at the same time, does not produce the same reversal in *Alpheus*.
- Przibram reported the reversal in several additional genera and found that the tendency was strongest in younger stages and declined with age.
- Herrick stresses that this is **not a universal crustacean rule**. In the American lobster (*Homarus*), ordinary regeneration usually replaces a lost claw with one of the same type; only rare or partial changes were known, and the mechanism/extent remained disputed. He notes no evidence for a full contralateral compensation in those cases.

This source is a contemporary natural-history synthesis, not a replacement for Wilson's original experiment. It is particularly useful because it makes species- and stage-dependence explicit rather than presenting one striking case as a universal symmetry law.

### D. Loeb's interpretation and an important neural caveat

Jacques Loeb, *The Organism as a Whole, from a Physicochemical Viewpoint* (1916), in the regeneration discussion, repeats Przibram's *Alpheus* observation and says Przibram found the nervous system unrelated to the claw-reversal phenomenon. Loeb suggests the case as a possible effect of internal secretions or substances in the blood. Searchable scan text: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7e/The_organism_as_a_whole%2C_from_a_physicochemical_viewpoint_%28IA_organismaswholef00loebrich%29.pdf. The PDF itself could not be opened through the web reader in this pass because it exceeded the service's size limit; the quoted paragraph is available in the indexed search result.

This must be reconciled carefully with Przibram's 1909 discussion of nerve-section experiments, where nerve cuts and differential growth rates can affect outcomes. Loeb's statement may concern the direct causal basis of *Alpheus* reversal, not the claim that nerves can never influence regeneration. The current evidence does not justify a blanket “nervous system has no effect on regeneration” statement.

### E. Relation to the symmetry and histological inquiry

This example requires us to keep at least four phenomena distinct:

1. **Bilateral morphological symmetry:** paired structures are similar in size/form.
2. **Stable heterochely:** paired structures are intentionally/characteristically differentiated, one K and one Z.
3. **Compensatory lateral reversal:** after the dominant chela is lost, the contralateral minor chela changes into the dominant form while the amputated side regrows a minor form. The asymmetric pattern is retained but changes sides.
4. **Direct regeneration:** an amputated part is replaced with the same type, without a corresponding transformation on the opposite side.

Przibram/Wilson provide a historical experimental example of strong systemic interdependence between distal, paired appendages within a single animal. The uninjured side is changed by an intervention on its opposite counterpart. This is a useful ordinary-biological comparator for thinking about distal paired structures, correlated development and whole-organism regulation. It does **not** demonstrate an additional spatial axis, nonlocal action between organisms, or the user's proposed physical 4D branching continuity. The mechanisms discussed are regeneration, molt-dependent differentiation and organism-level correlation within a conventional individual crustacean.

For the user's 4D histological proposal, the analogy is useful but bounded: it gives us a known case in which apparently separate body sides are not functionally independent. What remains unique to the proposal is the claim that apparently separate people/organisms can be physically continuous appendages of a larger 4D histological body. That stronger relation needs its own anatomical and geometrical specification.

### F. Next source operations

1. Obtain and read Wilson's original 1903 article directly; the volume scan is known but its OCR parser skipped the relevant article body. Find a BHL/Internet Archive page-level download or another readable scan.
2. Locate Przibram's original 1901 and 1907 experimental studies. His 1909 book is a primary synthesis and includes a detailed account, but it cites these earlier reports as the experimental papers.
3. Read Loeb's Lecture XI (1906) on regeneration in *Tubularia*, *Cerianthus* and planarians, with attention to tissue/organelle formation, correlation and environmental constraints. The contents are available, but this lecture has not yet been read in full.
4. Compare with current experimental/regenerative biology only after the historical claims and their conditions are stable.
