## 53. Modern developmental mechanism of Alpheus claw reversal — lateral inhibition, neural remodeling and muscle re-specification — 2026-11-24

The earlier Przibram/Loeb interpretation now has a substantially richer modern evidence base. The most important result is that claw reversal in *Alpheus heterochelis* is not merely enlargement of an opposite appendage. It involves a paired developmental system in which one side inhibits or constrains the other, with coordinated changes to nervous tissue, muscle phenotype and external morphology.

### 1. The modern model: lateral inhibition in a threshold trait

A. Richard Palmer, “Developmental origins of normal and anomalous random right-left asymmetry: lateral inhibition versus developmental error in a threshold trait,” *Contributions to Zoology* 81(2), 111–124 (2012), full text: https://repository.naturalis.nl/pub/419175/CtZ81-02_111-124_Palmer.pdf.

Palmer reviews several developmental models for random left/right asymmetry and distinguishes normal random asymmetry (where nearly all individuals are conspicuously asymmetric, but the side is random) from anomalous asymmetry (rare asymmetries in otherwise symmetric species). His general account treats asymmetry as a threshold/polyphenism in which one side crosses a developmental threshold while the paired side does not. For normal random asymmetries, lateral inhibition — transformation of one side suppressing transformation of the opposite — is likely a crucial developmental step.

In snapping shrimp, asymmetry usually appears at later juvenile stages (e.g. sixth juvenile stage in *Alpheus heterochelis*). The more-used claw can initially become the snapper. After asymmetry is established, however, removal of the dominant snapper can induce the opposite pincer to become a snapper, while the amputated side regrows a pincer. Palmer distinguishes this from the American lobster (*Homarus americanus*), where loss of the crusher typically regenerates a crusher on the same side and the original laterality is maintained. His review therefore makes species-specificity explicit: a related paired-claw system need not have the same developmental rules.

Palmer also notes that central nervous system-mediated inhibition is implicated in both snapping shrimp and lobsters, while emphasizing alternative possibilities across taxa, including local signals and diffusible morphogens. This is a review-level mechanistic model, not proof that all bilateral asymmetry uses the same neural pathway.

### 2. Neural remodeling precedes the external transformation

C. K. Govind and Joanne Pearce, “Remodeling of nerves during claw reversal in adult snapping shrimps,” *Journal of Comparative Neurology* 268(1), 121–130 (1 February 1988), DOI 10.1002/cne.902680112. Bibliographic record and abstract: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3346380/ and author-shared abstract page: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/19810695_Remodeling_of_nerves_during_claw_reversal_in_adult_snapping_shrimps.

The authors report that nerves to the ganglion are remodeled as the minor claw transforms into a major claw and a new minor claw regenerates at the old major site. The minor-to-major nerve conversion occurs within days after removal of the contralateral major claw: many sensory axons are added and a smaller number deleted. Neural remodeling is essentially complete within the first intermolt, in tandem with motoneuron changes, well before the larger changes in muscle and external morphology.

The reverse major-to-minor nerve transition is more traumatic and prolonged: it involves extensive sensory-axon degeneration during the first and second intermolts, followed by proliferation of largely unmyelinated axons during the third intermolt, with some later myelination.

This gives a sequence that is especially relevant to the histological lens: the nervous system is not merely a passive occupant of the claw. Its architecture is itself remodeled in advance of the full morphological transformation.

### 3. Experimental support for a central trigger

Govind, Wong and Pearce, “Experimental induction of claw transformation in snapping shrimps,” *Journal of Experimental Zoology* 248(3), 371–375 (1988), DOI 10.1002/jez.1402480317. Abstract: https://eurekamag.com/research/007/331/007331536.php.

Removing the major claw induced contralateral transformation in all tested animals. Cutting its nerve produced transformation in about two-thirds; tenotomy of the closer muscle induced it in fewer than half; opener-muscle tenotomy did so occasionally; immobilizing the claw did not induce transformation. The authors interpret the graded effectiveness in relation to the amount/type of trauma as evidence for a central mechanism controlling bilateral asymmetry. This shows the effect is not simply caused by reduced mechanical use of the missing claw.

The result is suggestive of a central control system but does not by itself establish the complete causal pathway or identify the initiating signal.

### 4. Limb-bud stage determines whether reversal can occur

C. K. Govind and A. T. Read, “Regenerate Limb Bud Sufficient for Claw Reversal in Adult Snapping Shrimps,” *The Biological Bulletin* 186(2), 241–246 (1 April 1994), DOI 10.2307/1542057. Full abstract and bibliographic page: https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.2307/1542057.

When both claws are autotomized at the same time, each regenerates in its previous morphotype. Sequential experiments show that the stage of the first regenerated limb bud matters: if a stage 1–2 bud exists at the pincer site before removal of the snapper, asymmetry remains in its prior configuration; if a more advanced stage 3–5 bud has formed, reversal occurs. The minimum condition for pincer-to-snapper transformation is therefore a sufficiently advanced limb bud at the pincer site. The newly transforming snapper is proposed to restrict the contralateral regenerating site to a pincer.

This is a valuable temporal and developmental boundary condition: the two sides are not instantaneously interchangeable. Their capacity to change depends on where each is in its regenerative sequence.

### 5. An explicit inhibition experiment

A. T. Read and C. K. Govind, “Claw Transformation and Regeneration in Adult Snapping Shrimp: Test of the Inhibition Hypothesis for Maintaining Bilateral Asymmetry,” *The Biological Bulletin* 193(3), 401–409 (1 December 1997), full abstract and bibliographic record: https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/abs/10.2307/1542942.

The study directly tests lateral inhibition:
- The intact snapper normally inhibits the opposite pincer from becoming a snapper.
- Following snapper loss, the pincer-to-snapper advancement occurs almost immediately, even when the animal is late in premolt.
- The transforming claw in turn inhibits the new claw regenerating at the old snapper site from becoming a second snapper.
- Cutting the dactyl of the transforming claw removes this inhibitory influence, allowing the other side to regenerate as another snapper and produce a double-snapper shrimp.
- The authors suggest the inhibition may be manifested partly through the greater innervation of the snapper relative to the pincer.

This is much more precise than an undifferentiated “compensation” story: the paired structures participate in a directional, state-dependent inhibitory relationship that helps maintain a one-snapper/one-pincer configuration.

### 6. Muscle phenotype changes during transformation

M. M. Quigley and D. Mellon Jr., “Changes in myofibrillar gene expression during fiber-type transformation in the claw closer muscles of the snapping shrimp, *Alpheus heterochelis*,” *Developmental Biology* 106(1), 262–265 (November 1984), DOI 10.1016/0012-1606(84)90081-2. Abstract: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6489610/.

The authors characterize myofibrillar proteins in the closer muscles of the dimorphic claws and find differences in myosin light chains and tropomyosin between fast/slow fibers. During claw reversal, the main closer muscle undergoes a quasi-developmental fiber-type transition. Changes in myofibrillar protein expression resemble those associated with cross-innervation-induced changes in vertebrate muscle. This demonstrates that transformation is not just a skeletal/external shape change: contractile tissue is re-specified too.

Karen M. Mearow and C. K. Govind, “Selective degeneration of fast muscle during claw transformation in snapping shrimps,” *Developmental Biology* 118(1), 314–318 (November 1986), DOI 10.1016/0012-1606(86)90099-0. Abstract: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0012160686900990. They report that the pincer closer muscle contains a central band of fast fibers flanked by slow fibers, while the snapper closer muscle is composed entirely of slow fibers; transformation includes selective degeneration of the central fast-fiber band.

### 7. Time course and behavioral consequences

Ariane Pereira et al., “Post-autotomy claw regrowth and functional recovery in the snapping shrimp *Alpheus angulosus*,” *Marine and Freshwater Behaviour and Physiology* 47(3), 147–159 (2014), DOI 10.1080/10236244.2014.905008. ResearchGate excerpt indexed on the 1988 study page: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/19810695_Remodeling_of_nerves_during_claw_reversal_in_adult_snapping_shrimps.

In *A. angulosus* (a related species, not the exact *A. heterochelis* in the original reversal studies), a functional snapping claw can emerge in one molt, but key external morphology and the plunger do not reach mature form until the third molt or later, depending on sex. This provides a useful comparison between early functional recovery and later morphological maturation. Keep species identities distinct.

### 8. Revisions to the early interpretation

This modern work requires three corrections to the earlier historical notes:

1. **Loeb's 1916 claim:** Loeb repeated Przibram's observation and suggested the nervous system was unrelated to claw reversal, proposing internal secretions or blood-borne substances. That claim is not consistent with later evidence of rapid sensory-axon remodeling, neural-control experiments and the 1997 inhibition model. Treat Loeb's explanation as a historical hypothesis, not the best current mechanistic account.
2. **“Symmetry restoration”:** the system does not seek bilateral equality. It maintains a *heterochelous* pair (one snapper, one pincer) through a controlled, lateralized differentiation. After injury the side assignment may reverse. The regulated invariant is the differentiated pair, not symmetry in the strict equal-parts sense.
3. **Age dependence:** Przibram/Herrick's early observation that reversal was more readily induced in younger animals must not be generalized to mean adult snapping-shrimp reversal does not occur. Modern experiments explicitly study reversal in adult *Alpheus*. Age, life stage, molt phase, species, developmental history and limb-bud state all matter; different taxa can have different rules.

### 9. Connection to the histological 4D proposal

This is an exceptionally useful example of *intra-organismal distal coupling*:
- The state of one appendage affects the developmental fate of the opposite appendage.
- The response unfolds across several timescales: rapid neural remodeling (days/first intermolt), limb-bud development, subsequent molt-dependent morphology, and later maturation of contractile tissue.
- The pair maintains a relational organization that cannot be understood by considering either claw alone.
- The relation is nevertheless an experimentally grounded ordinary 3D biological system inside one individual, mediated through central neural and developmental processes. It is not evidence for an additional spatial dimension or physical connections among separate organisms.

This suggests a useful constraint for our own model: distinguish **paired-part systemic regulation inside one organism** from the much stronger proposition that apparently independent organisms are physically continuous distal appendages of one larger 4D histological body. The shrimp gives us an empirical comparator for the first class, not proof of the second.

### 10. Earlier primary-paper status is still open

**Wilson 1903:** Edmund B. Wilson, “Notes on the Reversal of Asymmetry in the Regeneration of the Chela in *Alpheus heterochelis*,” *Biological Bulletin* 4, 197 onward. The full volume is located, but the available scan/OCR parser did not return the article body and the PDF was too large for page-image rendering. Its contents page gives start p. 197 and Florence Peebles' next paper starts p. 210. The National Academy of Sciences bibliography gives pp. 197–210 (https://www.nasonline.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/wilson-edmund.pdf); Herrick's later bibliography gives 197–214. The precise end-page discrepancy should be resolved from the original article. Status remains **located, not directly read**.

**Przibram 1901:** “Experimentelle Studien über Regeneration,” *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik* 11, 321–345, DOI 10.1007/BF02278218, OpenAlex work W2331310158. The OpenAlex record links a public-domain copy via Zenodo and Archive.org, and contemporaneous bibliographies describe the 1901 study as concerning Crustacea and Crinoidea. Direct download/open attempts failed because the Zenodo record and nested Archive.org archive-member route were inaccessible to the web reader. Status remains **bibliographically verified, not directly read**. Do not infer that it treats only *Antedon* or only *Alpheus* from the partial secondary citations.

**Przibram 1905/1907:** the 1907 article is *Die Scherenumkehr bei decapoden Crustaceen*, *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik* 25, 266 onward. Bibliographic sources disagree on its end page (343 or 348) and on the 1905 paper's pagination (181–217 or 181–247). The full 1909 synthesis remains directly OCR-read for the relevant pp. 101–105, but the 1905 and 1907 original experimental reports have not yet been read.

**Status:** the modern developmental account is reconstructed from accessible abstracts, Palmer's full open-access review, and primary paper bibliographic records. No modern full experimental paper beyond the review has been read in full. The original Wilson/Przibram papers remain priority source-retrieval tasks. No Tock closure or `@` advance.


## 54. Przibram's experimental series clarified; early neural/mechanical findings contrasted with modern inhibition models — 2026-11-24

Further source discovery clarifies the chronology and exposes a useful unresolved conflict in the history of the mechanism.

### A. The experimental series is a sequence, not one 1907 discovery

The bibliography and the titles of Przibram's own works establish the following sequence:

1. **1901, first communication:** Hans Przibram, “Experimentelle Studien über Regeneration,” *Archiv für Entwickelungsmechanik der Organismen* 11, 321–345, DOI 10.1007/BF02278218. OpenAlex record W2331310158 identifies the original article and repository locations: https://openalex.org/works/W2331310158. A contemporary review in *Zoologischer Jahresbericht* describes it as work on crustaceans and crinoids; Ariane Dröscher's later bibliography specifically notes *Alpheus*. This supports inclusion of the snapping-shrimp work in the first communication, but does not mean the whole paper concerns *Alpheus* alone. The full paper is bibliographically verified, not directly read in this workshop.
2. **1902, second communication:** a separate Przibram communication on Crustacea in *Archiv* 13, identified in later bibliographies. The exact title/pages have not yet been independently reconciled from a primary copy.
3. **1905, third communication:** “Die Heterochelie bei decapoden Crustaceen” (*Experimentelle Studien über Regeneration*, third communication), *Archiv* 19, 181–247, plates 8–13. This is a study of heterochely (paired chelae with different morphologies). Bibliographic locators: https://crabs-japan.linnaeus.naturalis.nl/linnaeus_ng/app/views/literature2/reference.php?epi=32&id=22181 and https://w3w.fiddlercrab.info/references/Przibram1905.html. The range 181–217 appears in some secondary databases, while other specialized bibliographies and the journal-volume record give 181–247; page range remains a bibliographic discrepancy until a full volume scan is checked.
4. **1907, fourth communication:** “Die ‘Scherenumkehr’ bei decapoden Crustaceen” (*Experimentelle Studien über Regeneration*, fourth communication), *Archiv* 25, 266 onward. The end page is reported as 343 or 348 in different catalogues/citations; preserve the discrepancy. ResearchGate's record for the article exposes a structured excerpt of Przibram's ten “programme questions” and answers, including the conclusions below, but not the full paper: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/230013617_Equilibrium_of_animal_form (article listing at lines 116–130). A later bibliography likewise lists the title as the 1907 fourth communication: https://documentation.eurekamag.com/research/092/110/.
5. **1917, fifth communication / transitional forms:** “Transitäre Scherenformen der Winklerkrabbe, *Gelasimus pugnax* Smith,” *Archiv* 43, 47–62. It is described in the same specialist bibliography as the fifth communication and a second study of arthropod homoeosis. This is a later extension and is not part of the current claw-reversal primary reading yet.

### B. What Przibram's 1907 summary actually claims

The 1907 ResearchGate record contains a ten-point synopsis apparently drawn from Przibram's article. Treat it as an **article synopsis excerpt**, not a complete primary-text reading. Its notable conclusions are:

- Claw reversal extends beyond *Alpheus* and occurs in both Macrura and Brachyura: the first cheliped pair in *Athanas*, *Alpheus*, *Callianassa*, *Carcinus* and *Portunus*, and the second pair in *Typton* where the heterochely lies on that pair.
- Reversal proceeds more slowly in larger animals; above roughly 10 mm carapace length, two Z-type claws may occur temporarily.
- **Nerve transection is said to have no effect on the reversal itself other than possible inhibition/retardation of growth.**
- Total removal of the K (major/crushing) claw can yield animals with only a K or only a Z claw depending on whether the unoperated contralateral Z claw is allowed to continue growing or is held at a lower growth stage by autotomy or nerve transection.
- Even decapods that usually regenerate the removed claw directly can show variation in claw-side relations; Przibram identifies Paguridae as an exception with more fixed side orientation.
- Morphological traits characteristic of the removed claw can reappear in the corresponding regenerated claw form whether it develops on the original or opposite side.
- Nutritional insufficiency and repeated severe losses can reduce overall body size over successive molts, particularly noted in *Typton*.
- In *Carcinus*, removal of a K-claw dactyl can also simplify the dactyl of the opposite Z-claw.
- Regenerates pass through developmental morphologies that Przibram relates to forms in ontogeny and phylogeny.
- Infection, pathology and shedding malformed regenerates at the first molt can create apparent individual variation in regeneration quality.

The excerpt is particularly valuable because it gives an early experimental account of developmental state as a condition for reversal. It also shows that Przibram's 1907 view of nervous influence is more specific than the broad phrase “nerves are irrelevant”: he says nerve severing does not independently determine the reversal, while acknowledging that it can retard growth and can be used to hold a contralateral claw at a lower developmental stage.

### C. A real historical-mechanistic tension to preserve

The 1907 synopsis says nerve transection affects reversal only by retarding growth. Later experimental papers report stronger neural involvement:

- Govind, Wong & Pearce (1988), “Experimental induction of claw transformation in snapping shrimps,” reports that removal of the major claw induced transformation in all tested animals, major-claw nerve section induced transformation in about two-thirds, closer-muscle tenotomy in fewer than half, and immobilization did not induce it. DOI 10.1002/jez.1402480317; abstracts at https://eurekamag.com/research/007/331/007331536.php and https://ouci.dntb.gov.ua/en/works/4az3Yq0l/.
- Govind & Pearce (1988), “Remodeling of nerves during claw reversal in adult snapping shrimps,” reports rapid remodeling of sensory axons and motoneuron changes during the first intermolt, before the full muscle/external morphological conversion. DOI 10.1002/cne.902680112; abstract: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/19810695_Remodeling_of_nerves_during_claw_reversal_in_adult_snapping_shrimps.
- Read & Govind (1997) experimentally test a lateral-inhibition hypothesis: the intact snapper suppresses the contralateral pincer; once the snapper is lost, the pincer advances toward the snapper state; the transforming claw inhibits a second snapper from regenerating contralaterally. The University of Chicago Press abstract is https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/abs/10.2307/1542942.
- Palmer (2012) synthesizes these findings and places snapping-shrimp claw laterality in a developmental threshold/lateral-inhibition framework; he identifies central nervous system-mediated inhibition as the likely explanation while distinguishing taxa where laterality is fixed or regulated differently.

The contrast should **not** be flattened into “Przibram was wrong” or “the nervous system is irrelevant.” The precise manipulated structures, species, developmental/molt stages, nerve sections, and endpoints must be compared. Przibram's 1907 statement concerns the effect of nerve transection on the reversal sequence; later work tests specific nerve manipulation, induction and the neural architecture accompanying conversion. A potential distinction between peripheral sensory interruption, central lateral inhibition, growth-rate effects and motor/sensory axon remodeling needs to be kept open until experimental methods are checked side by side.

### D. What this reveals about the biological system

The best current formulation is a **state-dependent, laterality-regulating system of paired appendages**, not a homeostatic drive toward bilateral sameness. The major/minor asymmetry is normally maintained by interaction between the two sides; after trauma, the opposite side can be re-specified as the major form. This involves developmental thresholds, inter-side inhibition, nerve remodeling, muscle-fiber reorganization and molt-dependent external morphology. The system's invariant is a relational configuration (one snapper + one pincer) rather than strict morphological symmetry.

For our larger histological inquiry, this is valuable because it is an experimentally grounded example of distal structures acting as a coupled developmental system. It gives a real model for interactions among separated tissue regions within one organism. It does not establish a 4D extra-spatial structure or physical coupling between distinct organisms.

### E. Remaining source tasks

1. Locate and read Przibram's 1901 paper directly; the title, DOI, year and pages are verified, and accessible public-domain routes are recorded, but the download route failed here. Confirm its experiments on both Crustacea and Crinoidea and identify which *Alpheus* observations belong to it.
2. Obtain full 1905 and 1907 *Archiv* texts and inspect their methods and exact results; resolve their end-page discrepancies.
3. Read Wilson's original 1903 article. The volume is located but the OCR parser skipped Wilson's body. The 1903 volume contents start the article at p. 197 and list the next paper at p. 210; the National Academy of Sciences bibliography gives pp. 197–210, while Herrick's later bibliography gives a different terminal page. Until the article itself is inspected, preserve as a pagination discrepancy.
4. Compare Przibram's nerve transection experiments with Govind, Wong & Pearce (1988) and the 1997 lateral-inhibition experiment, using exact anatomical target, timing, species and outcome as comparison fields.

**Status:** early publication chronology improved; Przibram 1907 article synopsis located and inspected but not full paper; Wilson 1903 original still located/not read; Przibram 1901/1905/1907 full primary papers remain retrieval tasks. No Tock closure or `@` advance.


## 55. Critical source discrepancy: Morgan's 1940 Wilson memoir reverses the claw-removal trigger — 2026-11-24

A new cross-check has exposed an important contradiction that must remain visible until Wilson's own 1903 article is inspected. **T. H. Morgan's 1940 National Academy biographical memoir says Wilson repeated Przibram's 1901 Alpheus claw-reversal experiments “after removal of the smaller one.”** The printed scan confirms that this is not an OCR artefact: see *National Academy Biographical Memoirs*, vol. XXI, pp. 326–327, especially p. 327. OCR and scanned page: https://www.nasonline.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/wilson-edmund.pdf.

That statement conflicts with multiple other sources:

1. **Morgan's own earlier book, 1901:** *Regeneration*, p. 63, describing Przibram's *Alpheus platyrhynchus*, explicitly says that when the **larger** claw is thrown off and the smaller is left intact, the smaller changes into the large form at the next moult (sometimes after two), while the newly regenerated claw takes the smaller form. If the experiment is repeated after the newly acquired large claw is removed, the sides switch again. Removing both claws together instead yields two small replacement claws. Primary OCR: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a1/Regeneration_%28IA_regenerationmorg%29.pdf (find result around printed p. 63).
2. **D'Arcy Thompson, 1917:** *On Growth and Form*, in the regeneration discussion at printed pp. 150–151, likewise says that in *Alpheus* the **large or knobby claw (A)** is removed, after which the opposite claw (B) assumes its size/form and the amputated side regenerates as the weaker claw. Thompson cites Wilson's 1903 paper (Biol. Bull. IV, p. 197) in this context. Text: https://alb-lang.org/library/read/55264.
3. **Francis H. Herrick, 1911:** *Natural History of the American Lobster*, printed p. 277, states that reversal in *Alpheus* occurs when it loses its big “hammer” claw; the smaller contralateral claw grows into the hammer, while the old site regrows the small form. Herrick explicitly states that amputating the smaller claw alone does **not** produce reversal. Primary-era synthesis: https://spo.nmfs.noaa.gov/sites/default/files/pdf-content/fish-bull/fb29.6.pdf.
4. **Modern literature:** reviews and experimental papers consistently describe snapper/major claw loss as the trigger for the opposite pincer-to-snapper transformation. Palmer (2012) and the modern experimental literature are summarized in section 53. A modern crustacean-morphology review also explicitly attributes the large-claw-removal plus subsequent pincer-nerve-section experiment to Wilson (1903): https://www.researchgate.net/publication/31012126_Muscle_and_Muscle_Fiber_Type_Transformation_In_Clawed_Crustaceans.

### Best provisional interpretation

Morgan's 1940 memoir may contain a simple textual slip (“smaller” for “larger”), or it may compress a different experimental detail in a misleading way. The surrounding sentence says Wilson confirmed Przibram's conclusions, and Wilson's later bibliographic record includes an additional abstract, “Notes on the Artificial Reversal of Asymmetry in *Alpheus*,” *Science* N.S. 17, 491–492 (1903). Neither fact resolves the discrepancy. The 1940 memoir's printed wording is clear, but it is contradicted by Morgan's own 1901 description, Thompson's 1917 account, Herrick's 1911 synthesis, and later experimental historiography.

**Do not silently normalize the memoir to “large claw.”** Preserve the discrepancy and assign different evidence weights:
- Morgan 1940: explicit printed statement, but conflicting and likely erroneous.
- Morgan 1901: direct description of Przibram's earlier result, before Wilson's 1903 experiment.
- Thompson 1917 / Herrick 1911: contemporary/near-contemporary secondary syntheses consistent with the large-claw trigger.
- Wilson 1903 primary article: not yet directly read; decisive source for the exact experimental procedure and whether the memoir might refer to a separate manipulation.

### Newly located bibliographic leads

- Wilson's 1903 paper is in *The Biological Bulletin* 4, 197–210; the full volume is held by Biodiversity Heritage Library, item 16879, and the scan used in this workshop is the 342-page volume: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/16879 and https://file.iflora.cn/fastdfs/group1/M00/63/B3/wKhnoF15ugWASWddATcI2K5PnDc835.pdf. The web text parser exposes the contents (article begins p. 197, next article begins p. 210) but skips Wilson's body. The screenshot service rejected the 20.38 MB PDF, so the original article's page image has not yet been inspected.
- Wilson's separate 1903 abstract, “Notes on the Artificial Reversal of Asymmetry in *Alpheus*,” appears in *Science* N.S. 17, 491–492. A 1903 bibliography in *Archiv für Naturgeschichte* lists it separately from the full *Biological Bulletin* article. Its abstract text has not yet been obtained.
- Morgan's 1940 memoir is the source of the anomalous “smaller claw” wording; the exact scanned p. 327 is preserved in the screenshot record from this session.

**Next:** retrieve Wilson's 1903 pp. 197–210 through an article-level BHL download, Internet Archive item/part, or separate scan; read the 1903 *Science* abstract; then determine whether Morgan 1940 is a typographical/authorial error or is describing an additional distinct experiment. Do not revise the mechanism until that check. No Tock closure or `@` advance.

## 56. Loeb's 1906 and 1916 regeneration texts: source-access correction and the scope of "correlation" — 2026-11-24

This section corrects the prior progress note about Loeb's 1906 *The Dynamics of Living Matter*. The searchable Wikimedia scan opened in this session contains the contents listing for Lecture XI (printed pp. 199–218), but its accessible text ends around printed p. 171. The body of Lecture XI is **not present in this scan copy/text extraction**. The lecture's listed sections include *Tubularia*, *Cerianthus*, planarians, the influence of the central nervous system on regeneration, correlations among regenerating parts, and external conditions. The ToC is not evidence of the chapter's arguments. Lecture XI remains a primary-source retrieval task.

### A. What was actually read in the 1906 book

The accessible text from *The Dynamics of Living Matter* (1906) does permit some direct primary-source reading of earlier material:
- Loeb's preface and methodology emphasize experimental control and physico-chemical analysis of development, self-preservation and reproduction.
- Lectures preceding XI discuss tropisms, organismal orientation and *Tubularia*'s contact-induced curvature. These observations concern environmental response, not the unavailable regeneration chapter.
- The accessible scan reaches only the early part of the volume, ending around printed p. 171; it does not include the contents-listed Lecture XI at p. 199.

**Status correction:** the earlier working update saying that the 1906 regeneration chapter was accessible as a complete primary text was too strong. No direct claim about the contents of Lecture XI should be attributed to this session. Seek the complete first-edition 1906 scan or another library copy before reading it.

### B. Loeb's 1916 primary account does provide a separate regeneration analysis

Jacques Loeb, *The Organism as a Whole, from a Physicochemical Viewpoint* (New York: G. P. Putnam's Sons, 1916), Chapter VII, “Regeneration,” has now been directly read in a complete searchable text transcription. Stable access:
- Internet Archive source images catalogue: https://archive.org/details/organismaswholef00loeb
- Searchable Project Gutenberg transcription: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/45962/pg45962-images.html
- Accessible transcription with full context: https://www.arc-codex.com/library/45962

The chapter opens by arguing that the organism as a whole can suppress regenerative capacities in its parts, and that isolation can release those capacities (printed pp. 153 ff.). Loeb then develops an account of correlation in regeneration through flows of sap or blood-borne substances and the influence of developing parts on other regions.

Relevant primary passages inspected:
1. **Printed pp. 153–157:** plant regeneration and organ correlation, including the Bermuda “life plant” (*Bryophyllum*), willow cuttings, and the transport of shoot-/root-forming substances. Loeb describes the “whole” as constraining regenerative potential in individual parts through circulation and the distribution/consumption of substances.
2. **Printed pp. 159–160:** Loeb distinguishes central nervous system influence from chemical/circulatory regulation in *Amblystoma* metamorphosis. He reports that cutting the spinal cord did not prevent simultaneous metamorphosis of gills at the head and tail; he attributes the coordination to blood-borne substances rather than the central nervous system. This is a specific claim about metamorphosis, not a blanket rule that nerves never affect growth or regeneration.
3. **Printed pp. 160–162:** Loeb describes *Tubularia* stolon pieces regenerating polyps at both ends; the oral-end polyp forms faster, and suppressing its formation can accelerate the aboral-end polyp. He interprets this as inhibition/correlation between developing parts. The text then discusses other forms of organismal regulation, tissue differentiation and the role of internal substances.
4. **Printed p. 176:** the *Alpheus* claw-reversal observation is presented as a possible example of internal secretion or substances contained in the blood. Loeb says that after removal of the **larger** chela, the remaining small chela assumes the large form at the next molt while a small-type chela regenerates at the amputated site; repetition restores the original asymmetry. He then writes that Przibram had shown the nervous system had “no connection with this phenomenon.” His footnote [161] identifies only Przibram, *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik*, 1901, vol. XI, p. 329. Loeb does not cite Wilson's 1903 paper in this passage.

### C. The precise scope of Loeb's Alpheus statement

Loeb's sentence is not a general conclusion drawn from his own new neural experiment on *Alpheus*. It is explicitly an attribution to Przibram's 1901 report, and Loeb places it in an argument about internal secretions and blood-borne substances. This appears to be a report of what Loeb understood Przibram's experiments to have established, rather than an exhaustive review of later neurophysiological work.

This matters because Wilson's 1903 work was published after Przibram's 1901 paper. Thompson (1917) and Govind, Mellon & Quigley (1987) describe an additional nerve-section experiment by Wilson. Therefore Loeb's 1916 sentence is historically intelligible as a summary of the 1901 result, but cannot be treated as the final or current account of the mechanism once the 1903 and later work are considered.

### D. Correlation in regeneration: a useful distinction for the active inquiry

Loeb uses “correlation” broadly for relations whereby an organism's other parts regulate what a regenerating region can become. He presents possible mediators such as:
- circulation of formative substances through sap/blood;
- local consumption or “suction” of those substances by a developing organ;
- inhibition by one developing part upon another;
- environmental conditions that alter whether or where a regenerative process proceeds.

The *Tubularia* example is especially instructive: the two poles are capable of producing a polyp, but the oral pole develops sooner and inhibits or delays the aboral pole; suppressing the first permits the second to accelerate. This is a local, ordinary biological correlation within a continuous organism, not evidence for an extra spatial dimension.

For the user's histological 4D proposal, Loeb offers a vocabulary for distinguishing structural connectedness, functional/regulatory coupling and circulating inter-part signals. The shrimp case supplies a more specialized paired-appendage example. Neither source establishes a physical connection among separate organisms.

**Source status:** Loeb's 1916 Chapter VII is directly read in searchable primary-text transcription at the passages listed above. Its page images have not been independently checked. The 1906 Lecture XI is not read; the available scan copy is incomplete at that location. No cursor advance.


## 57. Wilson's 1903 nerve experiment, Thompson's growth-rate explanation, and the scope of Loeb 1916 — 2026-11-24

The cross-source chronology now permits a more precise reconstruction. Wilson's 1903 original article remains located but not directly read; two later sources describe its nerve-section experiment, and Thompson supplies a distinct growth-rate interpretation.

### A. Morgan 1901: reversal established, causal regulator still unresolved

Thomas Hunt Morgan, *Regeneration* (1901), printed p. 63, summarizes Przibram's *Alpheus* result: when the **larger claw** is lost and the smaller remains, the smaller changes into the large form at the next molt (sometimes after two), while a small-type claw regenerates at the amputated site. Repeating removal of the newly acquired large claw reverses the side assignment again. Removing both claws simultaneously produces two small replacements. Morgan adds that no experiments had yet shown what factors regulate development of either claw type. Searchable text locator: https://litbit.ru/en/thomas-hunt-morgan/regeneration.

### B. Wilson 1903 as summarized by Thompson (1917) and Govind et al. (1987)

Thompson, *On Growth and Form* (1917), printed pp. 150–151, discusses Wilson's experiments immediately after the general Alpheus reversal. Thompson states that sectioning a claw's nerve retards its growth. In Alpheus, when the large claw is removed and the nerve to the surviving lesser claw is also severed, the latter's growth is slowed sufficiently for the regenerating large-side claw to catch up; the two claws then remain approximately coequal in size. Thompson presents this as a growth-rate account of the phenomenon. Primary text: https://proxybridge.pythonanywhere.com/cache/epub/55264/pg55264-images.html.

Govind, Mellon & Quigley, “Muscle and Muscle Fiber Type Transformation in Clawed Crustaceans,” *American Zoologist* 27(4), 1079–1098 (1987), DOI 10.1093/icb/27.4.1079, give a neural-developmental interpretation. They say Wilson removed the large claw and subsequently severed **both nerve trunks** supplying the remaining pincer; reversal then did not occur. They call this the earliest direct evidence that pincer transformation may be under nervous influence. They also describe later experiments in which cutting the snapper's nerves in otherwise intact shrimp could trigger transformation of the opposite pincer. Full text: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/31012126_Muscle_and_Muscle_Fiber_Type_Transformation_In_Clawed_Crustaceans (around article pp. 1090–1092).

These interpretations may be compatible at the level of observation: neural interruption changes the outcome; Thompson describes the proximate effect as retarded pincer growth, while Govind et al. frame it as evidence for neural influence over the transformation. Neither source alone identifies the complete pathway. Separate observed result, proposed mechanism and later reinterpretation.

### C. Loeb 1916: a 1901 attribution, not the last word

Loeb, *The Organism as a Whole* (1916), Chapter VII, printed p. 176, cites only Przibram 1901, *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik* XI, p. 329. Loeb presents the Alpheus observation as an example potentially explained by internal secretions/blood-borne substances and says Przibram had shown the nervous system had “no connection with this phenomenon.” Loeb does not cite Wilson's 1903 paper in that passage.

The chronology matters: Przibram's 1901 result preceded Wilson's later nerve-section experiment. Morgan's 1901 account says the regulator was still experimentally undetermined; Wilson's 1903 experiment, as later described by Thompson and Govind et al., added evidence that neural input can affect reversal. Loeb's 1916 statement is therefore best treated as an over-categorical attribution to the 1901 result, not a final assessment of the later evidence. Primary transcription: https://www.arc-codex.com/library/45962 (Chapter VII, pp. 159–176).

### D. Morgan 1940 memoir: “smaller” remains a likely but uncorrected slip

Morgan's 1940 National Academy memoir says Wilson repeated Przibram's Alpheus experiments “after removal of the smaller one” (printed p. 327). This conflicts with Morgan's own 1901 account (larger-claw removal), Thompson 1917, Herrick 1911 and Govind et al. 1987 (all describing larger/snapper removal). The most likely explanation is a textual slip in the memoir, but the original Wilson paper is decisive. Preserve the 1940 wording in quotation; do not silently emend it or declare an erratum before Wilson's original is inspected.

### E. What is now established, and what remains open

- Established by converging historical/modern secondary accounts: losing the major snapper can induce the contralateral pincer to transform; nerve interruption can alter or prevent that transformation; growth rate is one proposed proximate mechanism.
- Not yet directly verified in this workshop: Wilson's exact 1903 methods and wording; Przibram's 1901 primary methods; whether the Morgan 1940 “smaller one” phrase reflects a simple slip or a separate compressed experiment.
- The 1906 Loeb Lecture XI remains unread because the available scan copy ends around printed p. 171, before its listed start on p. 199. Loeb's 1916 Chapter VII has been read and recorded separately in section 56.

For the histological 4D proposal, the Alpheus case demonstrates distal paired-part developmental coupling within one ordinary organism. It does not establish an extra spatial dimension or physical linkage among separate organisms. Keep structural connectedness, regulatory coupling, physiological communication and organic part-whole membership distinct.

**Next:** retrieve Wilson 1903 pp. 197–210 directly; inspect Przibram 1901 p. 329 and its methods; then assess whether Loeb's later summary and Morgan's 1940 memoir each contain a distinct historical misstatement. No Tock closure or cursor advance.


## 58. Loeb 1906 Lecture XI recovered in full; Wilson's 1903 nerve experiment confirmed in Loeb's own primary text — 2026-11-24

The earlier source-access limitation in section 56 is now superseded. The incomplete Wikimedia PDF was not the only scan. The Internet Archive copy linked from the Biodiversity Heritage Library (MBLWHOI item) provides the **complete 233-page text/OCR** of Jacques Loeb, *The Dynamics of Living Matter* (New York: Columbia University Press, 1906), including printed pp. 199–218, Lecture XI, “On the Dynamics of Regenerative Processes.”

**Full scan/item:** https://archive.org/details/dynamicsofliving00loeb  
**Complete searchable OCR:** https://archive.org/stream/dynamicsofliving00loeb/dynamicsofliving00loeb_djvu.txt  
**BHL publication record:** https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/bibliography/46232 (MBLWHOI scan is public domain; Cornell copy also public domain). BHL's alternative MBL scan/item is https://field.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/17110. Google Books' full-view record confirms 233 pages and contents: https://books.google.com/books?id=tFdm-ljZNUQC.

The OCR text has now been opened and inspected across printed pp. 199–218. Page-image verification was not performed in this pass; exact quotations and technical details remain OCR-dependent and should be checked against scans before formal publication.

### A. Loeb's regenerative morphology begins with physical/chemical determinants — Lecture XI, §§1–2, pp. 199–207

Loeb introduces regeneration as a more experimentally tractable way to study organ formation than embryogenesis, because the egg is too small for easy experimental analysis. He takes up Sachs's hypothesis that organ form depends on differing chemical constitution and that formative substances move through plants in directed streams.

**Compensatory hypertrophy:** on p. 201 Loeb notes the increase in mass of the remaining kidney after the other is removed. He suggests that, if Sachs's hypothesis applies, growth-promoting substances that formerly flowed through both glands may now be concentrated in the remaining one. This is a historical hypothesis about chemical supply, not a modern settled mechanism.

**Tubularia heteromorphosis and polarity:** Loeb describes hydroid stem segments capable of forming polyps at both ends, but the oral end normally forms its polyp sooner than the aboral end. If the oral end is embedded in sand to suppress oral-pole polyp formation, the aboral polyp forms much sooner (pp. 202–203). He reasons that an internal current carries substances through the stem and that suppression of the first-formed oral polyp alters the developmental opportunity at the other pole.

Loeb further observes that the hydroid is a hollow cylinder with a body cavity divided into chambers that communicate at the ends; ciliary motion establishes opposing streams of liquid, carrying pigment granules. He proposes that this current is involved in morphological polarity and notes that closing the cut ends and establishing circulation is necessary for regeneration in the experimental fragments (pp. 205–207). He says that the precise retention/localization of pigment granules still needs explanation and that the observations do not rule out Sachs's specific formative-substance hypothesis.

**Interpretive value:** this is a clear historical example of organismal/regenerative pattern being treated as dependent on internal transport, tissue boundaries, circulation and spatially directed physiological processes. It is a functional/physiological continuity within an ordinary organism, not a fourth-spatial-dimensional structure.

### B. Planarian regeneration and the emergence of new anatomical organization — §§3–4, pp. 207–213

The chapter next discusses regeneration in the actinian *Cerianthus* and freshwater planarians. For planarians, Loeb describes how oblique cuts can produce a tiny head at the foremost corner of a fragment and a tail at the hindmost corner, rather than regenerating uniformly across the cut surface. He considers this in terms of directional currents/transport and gathering of formative substances. Where substances gather in two separated locations at the anterior end, two heads may form (around pp. 210–213).

This is another historical case of morphology depending on spatially distributed physiological conditions. The appearance of multiple heads is a developmental result in a regenerating fragment, not evidence for multiple independent organisms or nonlocal agency.

### C. The central nervous system and regeneration — §5, pp. 213–216

The section heading itself is explicit: “On the Influence of the Central Nervous System upon Regeneration and on Phenomena of Correlation in Regeneration.”

Loeb opens by stating that the central nervous system plays an important role in regeneration. He reports:
- In the marine planarian *Thysanozoon brocchii*, an isolated head containing ganglia regenerates rapidly, while the body portion without ganglia regenerates less or more slowly (p. 213). He notes that food uptake is not responsible, since the isolated head cannot feed.
- Other investigators (T. H. Morgan, Child, Lillie and Lillian Morgan) had found that oesophageal ganglia substantially influence regeneration in marine planarians.
- Freshwater planarians differ: their oesophageal ganglia are not required for complete and rapid regeneration. Loeb notes that their longitudinal nerves contain more ganglia than the corresponding nerves of *Thysanozoon* (pp. 213–214).
- In decapod crustaceans, Herbst showed that whether an amputated eye regenerates as an eye or as an antenna depends on whether the optic ganglion is removed with the eye. If the optic ganglion is removed, an antenna forms; if it is left intact, an eye forms. This was reported for *Palaemon*, *Palaemonetes*, *Sicyonia*, *Palinurus* and other crustaceans. Loeb reports Herbst's interpretation that the optic ganglion exerts a “formative stimulus” on the wound's hypodermal cells (p. 214).
- In earthworms, Morgan reported that a new head can form at the anterior cut end of the nerve cord rather than at the external body edge if a piece of the nerve cord is removed in a particular way. In some experiments, two anterior nerve-cord ends were followed by two new heads (pp. 214–215).
- Loeb then reviews lens induction: the optic vesicle's contact with ectoderm is required for lens formation; transplanting the optic vesicle can induce lens formation at a different ectodermal location (pp. 215–216).

Loeb insists that the role of ganglia/nerves requires a physical or chemical explanation; the phrase “formative stimulus” is not by itself a mechanism. He considers two classes of possibilities: nerve conduction of stimuli and nerve conduction of substances along the axon cylinder.

### D. Critical confirmation: Loeb 1906 directly reports Wilson's 1903 Alpheus nerve-section experiment — p. 216

This is the decisive correction to the previous historical reconstruction.

Loeb first summarizes Przibram's claw reversal: removal of the larger chela leads the smaller to assume the larger form at the next molt, while the regenerating chela assumes the smaller form. He then explicitly says:

- E. B. Wilson repeated Przibram's experiments.
- Wilson made the significant discovery that, after removal of the larger chela, growth of the remaining smaller chela did **not** occur if the nerve of that smaller chela had first been cut.
- Loeb cautions that the number of successful operations was small, but calls the results significant.
- Loeb interprets the result as suggesting that nerves have a function beyond conducting ordinary stimuli, namely promoting the growth or development of certain organs.
- He considers whether nerves may carry chemical substances between the periphery and ganglion cells through the axon cylinder. He cites Hans Meyer's finding that tetanus toxin can be carried from a wound to the central nervous system through nerve axons rather than through blood vessels, lymphatics or the nerve sheath (pp. 216–217).

This directly confirms the Wilson experiment described later by Thompson (1917) and Govind, Mellon & Quigley (1987). Wilson's original *Biological Bulletin* article is still not directly read, but Loeb's 1906 primary text is an independent contemporary report with a bibliographic footnote to Wilson, *Biological Bulletin*, vol. 4, p. 197.

### E. Correcting the Loeb chronology and the apparent 1906/1916 contradiction

The history must now be represented in two separate Loeb books:

1. **Loeb 1906, *The Dynamics of Living Matter*, Lecture XI, p. 216:** explicitly cites and accepts Wilson's 1903 nerve-section finding as significant evidence for a neural role in claw reversal. Loeb concludes that nerves may have a growth/development function and speculates about axonal transport of substances.
2. **Loeb 1916, *The Organism as a Whole, from a Physicochemical Viewpoint*, Chapter VII, p. 176:** in the searchable text previously inspected, Loeb says Przibram had shown that the nervous system had “no connection” with the *Alpheus* reversal and cites only Przibram 1901, p. 329. This is not a claim in the 1906 book. It appears to be inconsistent with his own 1906 account of Wilson's later experiment.

Thus the earlier section 57 statement that the 1916 Loeb claim was merely a pre-Wilson account was incorrect. The 1916 book came after Wilson's 1903 paper and after Loeb's own 1906 discussion of it. The stronger finding is that **Loeb's 1916 text appears to revert to or repeat the older 1901 Przibram attribution while failing to mention Wilson's 1903 nerve experiment, despite his own earlier publication having acknowledged it**. Whether this is an oversight, a different interpretive context or a textual problem needs further investigation; do not infer motive.

### F. Implications for our biology/history framework

This chapter supplies a much better historical vocabulary for the active inquiry:
- **morphological polarity:** why one end/region forms a particular organ and another end a different one;
- **internal circulation / transport:** tissue cavities and directed currents as routes for substances or signals;
- **local developmental inhibition / correlation:** activity at one pole changes what occurs at another;
- **nervous-system formative influence:** ganglia and axons can influence regeneration and the identity of regenerated structures;
- **heteromorphosis:** one organ type is regenerated in the place of a different one, depending on local/connected anatomical conditions;
- **correlative organogenesis:** a developing organ or neural center can influence another region of the same organism.

For the user's 4D histological proposal, the most useful distinction is now even sharper: Loeb's 1906 model describes internal, physically mediated relations within a continuous body — tissue boundaries, circulation, nerves, ganglia, local stimulus, and transported substances. These are real inter-part relations, but they remain ordinary three-dimensional biology and do not establish the proposed extra-spatial or inter-individual topology.

**Source status:** Loeb 1906 Lecture XI is now directly read in full searchable OCR, including printed pp. 199–218; page-image cross-check is still outstanding. Loeb 1916 Chapter VII was separately read in searchable transcription. Wilson 1903 original paper remains located but not directly read. No Tock completion or `@` advance.


## 59. Read, Pearce & Govind 1994 resolves the double-snapper nerve experiment; the 2017 “suture” summary compresses distinct operations — 2026-11-24

A full searchable text of the 1994 *Journal of Experimental Zoology* article has now been inspected, and it materially clarifies the earlier nerve-operation ambiguity:

**Primary source:** R. E. Young, J. Pearce & C. K. Govind, “Establishment and Maintenance of Claw Bilateral Asymmetry in Snapping Shrimps,” *Journal of Experimental Zoology* 269, 319–326 (1994), DOI 10.1002/jez.1402690405. Full text transcription: https://www.academia.edu/54775623/Establishment_and_maintenance_of_claw_bilateral_asymmetry_in_snapping_shrimps. Article was inspected in OCR/text extraction, not page-image verified.

### A. The 1994 study's two distinct developmental regimes

1. **Juvenile determination:** paired claws are morphologically similar and undifferentiated through early juvenile stages. Hypertrophy of one claw begins in the fifth juvenile stage; by the sixth stage it is differentiated as the snapper with hammer and socket. Removal of one claw during the third or fourth juvenile stage results in the intact claw becoming snapper and the regenerate becoming pincer. This suggests use-dependent/neural input helps determine laterality during a critical juvenile period. Early removal in the second stage does not influence which side becomes major.
2. **Adult reversal:** after asymmetry is established, removal/autotomy of the snapper claw results in the intact pincer transforming into a snapper, while a new pincer regenerates at the old snapper site. This is not permanent laterality; the adult system remains reversibly lateralized.

The article explicitly distinguishes the establishment of asymmetry in juveniles from its maintenance/reversal in adults. Do not conflate the two processes.

### B. Adult experiment: exact nerve operations and sample outcomes

The critical experimental question is why the old snapper site normally regenerates as a pincer even though the transforming intact pincer is becoming a snapper. Young, Pearce & Govind hypothesize that the transforming pincer-to-snapper claw inhibits a second snapper from developing at the opposite site. They reduce neural input from the transforming claw by tenotomy or nerve transection, then remove the snapper.

**Methods (printed pp. 320–322):**
- Each claw receives two nerves, a smaller nerve 1 and a larger nerve 2. Nerve 1 has roughly one-third the axon profiles of nerve 2.
- In adults, after the snapper claw is autotomized, the contralateral intact pincer begins transforming into a snapper.
- Nerve transection involves removing a length of nerve from the mid-merus to the autotomy plane in the basi-ischium. The nerve is grasped through a small cuticular window and pulled until it breaks at the autotomy plane. The wound is sterilized and sealed. Both nerves can be sectioned separately or together.
- When lesioning the pincer claw, the snapper is removed after the nerve lesion. This is an experiment on the **transforming pincer-to-snapper limb**, not simply section of a nerve in the original snapper.

**Results (Table 3, printed p. 324):**
- Control snapper autotomy alone: 12/12 successful animals show the normal pattern. The intact pincer transforms into a snapper; the regenerated claw at the old snapper site is a pincer.
- Section of the **small nerve 1 only** in the transforming pincer: 10/14 successful. The intact pincer still transforms into a snapper; the regenerate remains a pincer. This is not significantly different from control.
- Section of the **large nerve 2 only** in the transforming pincer: 9/25 successful. The intact pincer transforms into a snapper; the regenerate appears as either a pincer (4) or a snapper (5), a significant departure from control (P<0.02).
- Section of **both nerves 1 and 2** in the transforming pincer: 9/19 successful. The intact pincer transforms into a snapper; the regenerate appears as a pincer (4) or snapper (4), again significantly different from control (P<0.02; one further case not tabulated as a valid binary morphology outcome in the OCR).

Thus sectioning nerve 2 or both nerves from the transforming claw can permit a second snapper to regenerate at the old snapper site, yielding paired symmetric snappers in some successful trials. Sectioning nerve 1 alone does not produce this effect. The study concludes that neural influences from the transforming pincer-to-snapper claw restrict regeneration on the contralateral side to a pincer, maintaining bilateral asymmetry.

### C. Relation to Wilson, Loeb, Mellon & Stephens, and the 1988 experiments

This helps distinguish at least three different nerve manipulations and their endpoints:

1. **Wilson 1903 as reported contemporaneously by Loeb (1906, p.216):** Loeb says Wilson removed the major chela and cut the nerve of the remaining smaller chela first; Loeb reports the smaller claw's growth/transformation did not occur. Wilson's original text is still not retrieved, so the detailed method and the exact meaning of “growth does not occur” remain provisional pending the original article.
2. **Mellon & Stephens 1978 / Govind, Wong & Pearce 1988:** damage/section of a nerve in the existing major snapper can itself trigger transformation of the opposite pincer into a snapper. The 1988 abstract reports removal of the major claw as 100% effective, sectioning its nerve as effective in about two-thirds, closer muscle tenotomy in fewer than half, opener-muscle tenotomy occasionally, and immobilization as ineffective. This is a manipulation of the existing **snapper-side** pathway, not the 1994 experiment on the transforming pincer-side pathway.
3. **Young, Pearce & Govind 1994:** after snapper removal has already initiated pincer-to-snapper transformation, section the small or large nerves in that **transforming pincer**. Nerve 2 or both nerves may remove enough inhibition to let the old snapper site regenerate a second snapper. This is the specific route to paired-snapper symmetry shown in Table 3.

These operations can produce different outcomes because they target different limb sides, nerve diameters, and stages in the transformation. “Nerve section” without naming the target claw, nerve branch and time relative to autotomy is too imprecise to compare the experiments.

### D. The 2017 “complete suture” wording: treat as a compressed or imprecise summary

Soledade, Oliveira & Almeida, “A specimen of the snapping shrimp *Alpheus pontederiae* ... with symmetric chelipeds,” *Spixiana* 40(2), 181–184 (2017), PDF: https://pfeil-verlag.de/wp-content/uploads/2017/11/SPIX_40_2_03_SO.pdf, directly inspected in searchable PDF text, p. 182, says: “The complete suture of the contralateral nerve of the minor cheliped,” followed by major-claw removal, resulted in cheliped symmetry, “i.e., in the development of a second major cheliped.” It attributes this to Wilson 1903, Mellon & Stephens 1978, Govind et al. 1988 and Young et al. 1994 together.

The word **“suture”** is preserved exactly as the accessible text extraction gives it. It is not the standard term for nerve transection and may be a wording/translation error; the PDF screenshot service returned cache miss, so the printed page image has not been visually checked. The cited outcome (a second major cheliped) corresponds closely to the **Young et al. 1994 nerve-2/both-nerve experiment** above, where denervating the transforming pincer could remove its inhibitory influence on the contralateral regenerate. It should not be attributed wholly to Wilson 1903 without Wilson's paper. The 2017 sentence appears to compress findings from four studies with distinct experimental protocols.

### E. Updated mechanistic account

The snapping-shrimp pair is governed by a state-dependent, asymmetric inhibitory relation:
- A major snapper suppresses the other side from becoming another snapper.
- Loss/damage to the established snapper can release the pincer to transform.
- The transforming pincer-to-snapper then inhibits the old snapper-side regenerate from becoming another snapper.
- Nerve 2, the larger nerve in the transforming claw, is especially important in maintaining this inhibition in the 1994 experiments; nerve 1 alone is insufficient to disrupt it.
- If the inhibition is sufficiently reduced, the animal can develop two snappers. This paired-snapper state can persist for several molts, although subsequent claw loss can restore asymmetry.

This is a stronger and more precise model than “the organism restores symmetry.” The normal invariant is not equality: it is one snapper plus one pincer. Nerve-mediated lateral inhibition helps preserve that differentiated paired configuration, while sufficiently strong disturbance can shift it to a two-snapper state.

### F. A naturally occurring counterexample with two minor claws

The 2017 *A. pontederiae* specimen is not an experimental result; it is one preserved male specimen (8.6 mm carapace length) with two minor chelipeds. The authors consider three possibilities: failure to differentiate a major chela during juvenile development; adult loss of the major claw without subsequent pincer-to-snapper transformation; or collection during an early stage of reversal. A small deformity in the right pollex is consistent with possible prior injury, but cannot identify the history. They regard the second hypothesis as most plausible, not proven.

This specimen shows that natural observations of paired-chela symmetry may arise through distinct developmental histories; a static symmetric specimen does not alone identify the underlying process.

### G. Research consequences

1. Keep Wilson 1903 original paper open. Loeb's contemporary 1906 report is direct evidence that Wilson performed a nerve operation, but not a replacement for his experimental text.
2. Keep Morgan 1940's “smaller one” phrase uncorrected until Wilson is read; the 1903/1906/1917/1994 sources overwhelmingly support larger/snapper claw loss as the normal reversal trigger.
3. When comparing nerve experiments, record target side/claw, nerve number, lesion timing, autotomy timing, species, sample successes, and endpoint morphotype. Do not collapse all into one “nerve cut” category.
4. The system is a clear ordinary 3D example of inter-part neural inhibition in a single organism. It does not imply an extra spatial axis or organism-to-organism physical continuity.

**Status:** Young, Pearce & Govind 1994 full OCR text inspected, including methods, Tables 1–3 and Discussion, pp. 319–326. Soledade et al. 2017 p.182 directly OCR-inspected; page image not verified. Mellon & Stephens 1978 abstract read. Wilson 1903 remains located but not directly read. No Tock closure or cursor advance.

## 60. Wilson 1903 retrieval status and pagination audit — contemporary 1903 bibliography confirms pp. 197–210 — 2026-11-24

The article's bibliographic identity is now stable, although its body remains inaccessible through the scans located so far.

### A. Page range cross-checks

The most consistent and earliest bibliographic witnesses report:
- **Zoologischer Jahresbericht für 1903**, Arthropoda author index: Edmund B. Wilson, “Notes on the reversal of asymmetry in the regeneration of the chelae in *Alpheus heterochelis*,” *Biological Bulletin*, Woods Hole, vol. 4, pp. **197–210**, 3 figures. Direct OCR inspection: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/48/Zoologischer_Jahresbericht_%28IA_zoologischerjahr1903staz%29.pdf (author index, printed page 18; OCR around line 4144 of web extraction). This is a contemporary bibliographic record dated 1903, not the article itself.
- **National Academy of Sciences, Edmund Beecher Wilson biographical memoir**, bibliography of his 1903 work: *Biological Bulletin* 4, pp. 197–210. Searchable PDF: https://www.nasonline.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/wilson-edmund.pdf.
- **Mellon & Stephens (1978)** cite Wilson's 1903 work as *Biological Bulletin* 4, 197–210 in the reference list of “Limb morphology and function are transformed by contralateral nerve section in snapping shrimps,” *Nature* 272, 246–248. The abstract is directly available here: https://www.nature.com/articles/272246a0.
- **Soledade, Oliveira & Almeida (2017)** list the same citation, *Biological Bulletin* 4:197–210, in their references (PDF p.184): https://pfeil-verlag.de/wp-content/uploads/2017/11/SPIX_40_2_03_SO.pdf.
- **Zoologischer Jahresbericht** also separately lists Wilson's abstract “Notes on the Artificial Reversal of Asymmetry in *Alpheus*,” *Science* N.S. 17, 491–492 (1903). It is a separate meeting abstract, not the full *Biological Bulletin* article.

Herrick (1911) is the outlier, listing pp. 197–214 in its bibliography. Since the contemporary 1903 index, NAS bibliography, 1978 Nature bibliography and 2017 bibliography agree on 197–210, the 197–214 range is likely a miscitation. This corrects the provisional uncertainty in section 55, although the page images of Wilson's article remain unavailable.

### B. Scan coverage issue

The identified *Biological Bulletin* vol. IV scan is:
- Internet Archive identifier biologicalbullet04mari, MBLWHOI scan;
- BHL item 16879;
- PDF used in this pass: https://file.iflora.cn/fastdfs/group1/M00/63/B3/wKhnoF15ugWASWddATcI2K5PnDc835.pdf.

Its contents page explicitly lists Holmes beginning p.191, Wilson beginning p.197, and Peebles beginning p.210, with Wilson's subsequent paper beginning p.215. However, the BHL item's indexed article records omit the three papers occupying pp.191–214 (Holmes, Wilson, Peebles), and the searchable scan extraction located so far does not expose Wilson's article body. The screenshot service returned a cache miss for the volume PDF. This makes a scan coverage/metadata gap plausible; it is not yet proof that every available copy lacks those pages. Avoid saying the original paper itself is unavailable everywhere.

### C. Why the 1994 primary experiment matters while Wilson is missing

Section 59 records the full 1994 experimental results. The 1994 paper distinguishes:
- nerve 1 section in the transforming pincer-to-snapper claw: normal adult reversal still occurs, with the old snapper site regenerating as a pincer;
- nerve 2 section, or section of both nerves in that transforming claw: the old snapper site can regenerate as a second snapper, producing paired snappers in a subset of successful trials.

This is the likely basis for the 2017 paper's wording that nerve intervention plus major-claw removal can yield a second major cheliped. The 2017 article compresses Wilson, Mellon & Stephens, Govind et al. and Young et al. into one sentence, and uses the unusual word “suture.” The precise 1994 experiment is recoverable; the 2017 sentence should not be attributed solely to Wilson.

### D. Outstanding retrieval targets

1. Obtain Wilson pp.197–210 from a second complete copy or article-level scan, if possible. BHL item 16879 and the biologicalbullet04mari volume scan appear to have an indexed coverage gap across pp.191–214.
2. Retrieve the separate *Science* 17 abstract at pp.491–492 as an independent contemporary statement by Wilson.
3. Once Wilson is directly read, compare exact sample, species, timing, side, nerve branch, and endpoint against Loeb's 1906 p.216 report and the 1994 nerve-1/nerve-2 experiment.
