## 61. Morgan's grafting programme: mixed-lineage regeneration, compound organisms, shared circulation and morphallaxis — 2026-11-24

This section consolidates direct primary-source reading of Thomas Hunt Morgan's 1899 paper and his 1907 synthesis in *Experimental Zoölogy*. It establishes an empirical bridge to the user's questions about physical continuity, histological organization and organismal boundaries, while keeping the experiments' limits explicit.

### A. Morgan 1899: regeneration from a mixed-species tissue interface

**Primary source:** Thomas Hunt Morgan, “Regeneration of Tissue Composed of Parts of Two Species,” *The Biological Bulletin* 1 (1899), 7–14. Original volume PDF: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/53/The_Biological_bulletin_%28IA_biologicalbullet01mari%29.pdf. The article body, printed pp. 7–14, was inspected in searchable OCR/text extraction. The PDF screenshot renderer returned a cache miss, so this is OCR-inspected, not page-image verified.

Morgan opens with Born's amphibian grafts and Joest's earthworm grafts, where the component parts of grafted animals retain their species characteristics. He notes that a regenerating part of a grafted earthworm had retained the identity of the region from which it immediately arose, despite being joined to a much larger component of another species. His own question is narrower and more novel: when a new regenerating tissue contains cells originating from two species, do the cells blend their specific characteristics or retain their separate identities?

**Procedure and material:**
- He used differently pigmented tadpoles identified as *Rana sylvatica* and *Rana palustris*.
- A tail from one species was grafted to the body of the other. As the animals developed, the host-body ectoderm extended over the base of the transplanted tail.
- The composite tail was then amputated near the junction so that the exposed cut surface contained outer ectoderm from one species and inner tissues (muscle, connective tissue, pigment cells, notochord and nerve cord) from the other.
- The regenerating region therefore contained adjacent tissue layers from two species, and the regenerated tail could be followed by the contrasting pigmentation of its ectoderm and mesodermal pigment cells.

**Observed results:**
- In reciprocal grafts, the new tail had the host species' ectoderm over the graft species' darker or lighter internal tissues. Morgan says each component regenerated its own specific tissue, with no blending of the visible characters.
- In oblique cuts where both species' ectoderm was present at the wound surface, the new tail retained the distinct pigmentation patterns along the contact region. The cells remained identifiable as belonging to their respective source tissues.
- Morgan explicitly notes uncertainty: pigmentation was his main practical lineage marker; some mesodermal pigment cells were imperfectly developed, and he occasionally found isolated cells whose identity was doubtful. He says most well-developed cells were clearly like those of the tissue from which the new tail arose.
- He also acknowledges that most larvae in one series died and that regenerated tails departed from normal form, increasing uncertainty. He did not claim a comprehensive cellular or genetic assay.

**What this experiment establishes at the level Morgan could measure:** a regenerating anatomical structure can be composed of cells and tissues derived from two species, physically adjacent and participating in one regenerated appendage, while the distinguishable tissue components retain their lineage-associated pigmentation. It is not a hybrid organism at the cellular/genetic level; the study does not demonstrate cell fusion or genetic exchange. Its inference that other species-specific traits would also persist is broader than the directly measured pigment characters and should remain identified as Morgan's inference.

### B. Morgan 1907: grafting as an experimental investigation of organismal boundaries

**Primary source:** Thomas Hunt Morgan, *Experimental Zoölogy* (New York: Macmillan, 1907), especially Chapter XVIII, “Experiments in Grafting,” printed pp. 285–305, and Chapter XVII, “Growth and Regeneration,” pp. 277–281. Columbia scan and searchable PDF: https://www.columbia.edu/cu/lweb/digital/collections/cul/texts/ldpd_5949112_000/ldpd_5949112_000.pdf. Printed passages were inspected directly through the text layer. The PDF screenshot renderer returned cache miss for page images, so this remains OCR-inspected, not visual-page verified.

Morgan begins by distinguishing grafting plants (often small buds inserted into stocks) from grafting animals, where complete cut surfaces of already formed structures can be brought together, sometimes exposing entire cross-sections of two animals. He groups the questions under (1) which regions can unite, (2) how united parts influence one another, (3) whether grafting can hybridize species, and (4) what embryonic development reveals about self-differentiation and correlated development.

#### B1. Earthworm grafts: permanent physical unions, altered body topology

Morgan summarizes Joest's experiments:
- Parts from different individual earthworms can unite, not merely complementary pieces from one animal.
- Inserting a middle section between anterior and posterior regions produces a permanently elongated “long” worm; removing a middle region and joining the two ends produces a “short” worm. Where union is complete, the junction may show no regeneration.
- Two anterior cut surfaces or two posterior cut surfaces can also be joined. A graft of two tail ends can produce a permanent two-tailed, headless composite, which slowly starves because it has no head/mouth.
- Where nerve cords align and unite, regeneration at the junction may not occur; where one or both nerve cords remain free, one or two heads may form. In an inverted graft, two heads can regenerate on opposite sides of the junction, each connected with one component's ventral nerve cord.
- Different species are harder to unite than members of the same species, but Joest obtained a compound worm from *Lumbricus rubellus* and *Allolobophora terrestris* that lived for eight months.

This is not merely superficial adhesion. In selected cases the components form a persistent composite animal with a shared body plan at the junction. But Morgan's account also shows that anatomical integration can be incomplete, pathological or nonviable (such as the two-tail/no-head composite).

#### B2. Hydra: morphallaxis can create one animal, two animals or an absorbed component

Morgan contrasts many animals whose formed tissues cannot remodel extensively with hydra and planarians, which can reorganize existing tissue to regain a typical body form with relatively little new tissue production. He describes:
- Joining anterior and posterior halves can make one normal hydra.
- Joining two sub-half pieces creates a short hydra that grows into normal proportions.
- Joining two pieces each longer than half creates a long hydra. It may produce a new foot and tentacles at the junction, then constrict into two separate hydras.
- Joining two oral/anterior ends can result in the heads fusing into one body, or in two heads and separation, depending on the relative orientations and subsequent changes.
- Joining two aboral/posterior ends can lead to new feet at the junction and separation into two individuals.
- Lateral grafts may share a trunk, shift position, split off, produce a foot or head at the junction, or be absorbed into the stock.
- A small fragment that cannot detach and cannot successfully join the shared trunk may be absorbed into the larger hydra.

Morgan says these outcomes vary with orientation, length, level and relative size of the pieces. His explanation is based on pressure/tension relationships and cell differentiation, but he explicitly labels the hypothesis provisional.

#### B3. Planarians: graft geometry, neural continuity and regenerative polarity

In planarian grafts, cut surfaces can unite; when union is complete and nerve cords connect, regeneration at the graft line may not occur. If one or both nerve cords remain free, one or two heads may appear. An upside-down graft of anterior pieces can yield two heads at the junction, one on either side, each aligned with a different nerve cord. Unlike hydra, planarians do not show the same routine ability to pinch apart and separate; misplaced parts can be remodeled or absorbed. Short reversed graft fragments may show polarity reversal, although Morgan notes that uninjured very short planarian pieces can also form two heads, making the graft-specific cause uncertain.

These observations make the nervous system and polarity relevant to boundary formation, but do not imply that a nerve cord alone defines organismal identity.

#### B4. Different species in one compound body: shared circulation without homogenized lineage

Morgan gives an especially important earthworm example:
- A compound worm formed from *Lumbricus rubellus* and *Allolobophora terrestris* lived for eight months.
- Blood circulation passed from one component into the other.
- Neither component showed a material influence on the other half's characteristic phenotype.
- In a further test, a new head regenerated from the *Lumbricus rubellus* component of a composite made with *Allolobophora foetida*. Though the regenerating region was nourished by blood from both components, the new head retained the lighter *Lumbricus* coloration and showed no influence of the darker other component.

Morgan's cross-species tadpole grafts similarly show that some combinations persist for weeks or apparently longer, but each region generally maintains the species-specific traits visible in color. Some pairings fail to unite permanently, or die/separate. This is evidence of composite physical and circulatory continuity with persistent local differentiation, not a homogeneous fusion of biological identity.

#### B5. Moth pupa grafts: integumentary union does not guarantee internal-organ continuity

Crampton's grafts of moth pupae can produce adult moths made from parts of different species. Morgan notes that the internal organs do not unite; the skin and integumentary organs do unite. Most components retain their own coloring, although two reported cases suggest possible pigment influence, which Morgan tentatively attributes to hemolymph passing from the larger component into the smaller. This is another reason not to use “graft” or “fusion” as a single undifferentiated category: the degree and type of tissue continuity vary.

### C. Morgan's proposed mechanism: pressure/tension relations are an explicit working hypothesis

In Chapter XVII, Morgan observes that regenerative growth rates differ according to the level at which an organism is cut, and that growth often slows as the typical form or size is approached. He proposes that the existing differentiated material constrains new growth and that the relations among tissues can be understood through pressure/tension and mutual response among cells.

In Chapter XVIII, he extends the idea to grafting: the relation of the united pieces, their orientation and the tension/pressure state at the junction may determine whether they fuse into one form, remain composite, remodel, divide or form additional parts. He explicitly warns against using an unexplained “formative force” as a causal answer. His hypothesis treats tension as a physical stimulus and proposes that cell differentiation can depend on mutual pressure. But he says the latter assumption is difficult to reconcile with current knowledge and is only a provisional or working hypothesis (printed p. 298).

Keep the categories separate:
- **Observed:** graft union, survival, circulation, regeneration, morphallactic remodeling, separation, pigmentation.
- **Morgan's interpretation:** pressure/tension relations help explain developmental adjustment and differentiation.
- **Not established by these experiments:** a universal mechanical law of morphogenesis, or any extra spatial dimension.

### D. A useful conceptual matrix for the broader H(s)H inquiry

These primary sources show that "one organism" cannot be reduced to a single yes/no test. At least six distinct relations can vary independently:

1. **Physical tissue continuity:** do tissues actually unite at the junction, or merely adhere?
2. **Histological coexistence:** can differently derived tissues live side by side in a shared structure?
3. **Circulatory continuity:** is there a shared vascular/blood route between components?
4. **Neural continuity:** do nerve cords connect, remain separate, or drive distinct regenerative organization?
5. **Developmental/regulatory coupling:** does one component alter the differentiation, polarity, growth or remodeling of the other?
6. **Whole-organism integration:** does the composite function and persist as one body, remodel into a typical organism, remain a composite, split into multiple organisms, or fail to survive?

Morgan's examples populate different combinations:
- Cross-species earthworm: persistent physical union + shared circulation + retained local lineage phenotype.
- Tadpole graft: physical union + mixed tissue boundary + lineage-associated pigmentation retained.
- Moth pupa: integumentary union without internal-organ union.
- Hydra: physical fusion followed by morphallaxis, common-body integration, or separation into individuals depending on graft configuration.
- Planaria: fusion and nerve-cord alignment can suppress regeneration at the junction; misalignment/free cords can yield additional heads.

### E. Relevance to the user's 4D histological proposal: analogies and limits

The strongest biological comparator so far is not the snapping-shrimp claw but the grafting literature. These experiments demonstrate that:
- parts from different individuals, and sometimes different species, can become physically continuous;
- circulation can cross the interface without erasing regional tissue identity;
- regenerative growth can create a new histological structure that contains tissues of distinct lineages;
- physical union, physiological coupling, developmental influence and organismal wholeness are separable variables;
- geometry and orientation can alter whether components persist, remodel, split or form new axes.

This gives us real 3D biological cases against which to define the proposed 4D relation. But the grafted pieces are joined in ordinary space and time through surgical contact, tissue healing, circulation, nerves and developmental processes. The experiments do not demonstrate a body whose physically connected parts are separated along an additional spatial or temporal dimension, and do not show that separate people are continuous appendages of a larger organism.

The critical model question remains: what extra relation would the 4D proposal predict that cannot be captured by (i) ordinary physical continuity, (ii) common circulation or neural coupling, (iii) developmental field/induction, (iv) lineage-specific tissue persistence, or (v) ecological/functional integration? The proposal will need a structural or observational discriminator beyond the fact that the components are related, coupled or share a history.

### F. Morgan's 1900 sequel and next operations

Morgan also published “Further Experiments on the Regeneration of Tissue Composed of Parts of Two Species,” *Biological Bulletin* 2 (1900), 111–119. Its identity is confirmed in Morgan's 1960 bibliography and the MBL History of the Marine Biological Laboratory's digitized bibliography, but the complete article body has not yet been retrieved or read. Do not merge it with the 1899 paper; it is a separate follow-up.

Next primary-source tasks:
1. Retrieve and read Morgan's 1900 sequel to determine what additional mixed-lineage regeneration experiments it reports.
2. Obtain Zeleny's full 1905 *Compensatory Regulation* (102 pages), which Morgan cites in the 1907 book as an independent experimental programme on interactions among body parts during regeneration. The 1905 *double chela* case report is a separate publication and must not be conflated with the 102-page monograph.
3. Compare Morgan's historic graft observations with modern literature on chimerism, vascular anastomosis, tissue lineage and developmental integration, using modern terminology without retroactively imposing it on the older authors.
4. Return to Wilson 1903 once a complete scan is available; it remains located but not directly read.

**Source status:** Morgan 1899 article directly OCR-inspected pp. 7–14; Morgan 1907 *Experimental Zoölogy* directly OCR-inspected pp. 277–305; PDF screenshot service returned cache miss for both sources, so no page-image verification. Morgan 1900 sequel bibliographically verified only. Zeleny 1905 *Compensatory Regulation* located bibliographically, not directly read. No Tock completion or cursor advance.


## 62. Botryllus schlosseri: natural vascular fusion, allorecognition and chimeric stem-cell lineages — 2026-11-24

This section adds a modern biological comparator to Morgan's historical grafting experiments. The colonial ascidian *Botryllus schlosseri* can undergo natural vascular fusion with genetically compatible conspecific colonies, producing a shared circulatory system and, depending on subsequent cell competition, a persistent chimera. It is a much more direct example of a biological entity formed through inter-individual fusion than the Alpheus claw system, and it allows physical continuity, lineage persistence and organismal identity to be examined separately.

### A. Anatomical and organismal setup

A *Botryllus* colony contains many asexually budded zooids embedded in a shared tunic and connected by extracorporeal blood vessels terminating in ampullae. When two colonies contact one another at their growing peripheral ampullae, they may either reject one another or fuse their vessels. Fusion establishes a shared vascular circulation; rejection occurs at the contact sites and produces localized points of rejection. A 2025 review describes fusion/rejection as outcomes of colony allorecognition and shows a compatible pair that fuses and later undergoes resorption of one colony's zooids, leaving the remaining colony with two functional zooid systems. Source: Rinkevich, “From seabed to sickbed: lessons gained from allorecognition in marine invertebrates,” *Frontiers in Immunology* 16 (2025), 1563685, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12018476/. Full text inspected in HTML.

This is not ordinary surgical mammalian parabiosis. It is natural vascular fusion in a colonial marine chordate. “Parabiont” may occur in the literature as a label for the fused vascular entity, but “vascularly fused chimera” is clearer for this workshop.

### B. 2013 primary identification of BHF: a compatibility gate for fusion or rejection

**Primary source:** Ayelet Voskoboynik et al., “Identification of a Colonial Chordate Histocompatibility Gene,” *Science* 341(6144) (26 July 2013), DOI 10.1126/science.1238036. Full open text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3810301/.

This paper was read directly in the full PMC HTML text:
- The authors identify Botryllus Histocompatibility Factor (BHF) as a genetic determinant that accurately predicts observed fusion/rejection outcomes in the tested colony pairs.
- Among 23 colonies, ten unique BHF alleles were identified. A separate validation cohort had six blind predictions, all confirmed (6/6).
- BHF was significantly upregulated in challenged colonies approaching fusion/rejection (P=0.009) and was strongly expressed in the vasculature, including ampullae.
- In BHF morpholino knockdown experiments, control colony pairs generally fused or rejected, while BHF-knockdown pairs showed no reaction during the observation period. This supports BHF participation in initiating the histocompatibility response.
- The authors describe the key downstream biological problem: after vascular fusion, stem cells from the two colonies compete to overtake germline and/or somatic lineages. This may eliminate the other colony's genome, or produce a mixed-genotype chimeric colony.

Primary-source passages: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3810301/ (especially the results section around BHF validation, expression and morpholino tests, and the paragraph on post-fusion stem-cell competition). The article directly measures BHF-related prediction, expression and knockdown outcomes; its discussion of the broader competition among stem-cell lineages cites earlier research and should not be presented as though the 2013 experiments themselves conducted all those long-term lineage studies.

### C. 2011 juvenile fusion study: growth benefit and distinct somatic/germline outcomes

**Primary study identified and abstract/methods/results extracted; full primary article not directly opened in this session:**

Meredith A. Carpenter, John H. Powell, Katherine J. Ishizuka, Karla J. Palmeri, Snjezana Rendulic & Anthony W. De Tomaso, “Growth and Long-Term Somatic and Germline Chimerism Following Fusion of Juvenile *Botryllus schlosseri*,” *The Biological Bulletin* (2011), DOI 10.1086/BBLV220N1P57, PMCID PMC4265767. SciSpace record: https://scispace.com/papers/growth-and-long-term-somatic-and-germline-chimerism-1woz3rpbd0?utm_source=chatgpt&utm_medium=tool.

The extracted abstract and article-level structured summary report:
- The study compared naturally formed and controlled-fusion juvenile chimeras, with high- and low-density growth conditions.
- Growth was density dependent. Under high-density conditions the chimeras grew while almost all individual colonies did not; growth improved when individual colonies were moved to low-density conditions.
- The authors assessed genotype contributions in somatic tissues and testes using AFLP markers.
- Somatic and germline outcomes were not necessarily the same: a genotype losing in somatic tissues could still contribute to the germline, or vice versa.
- Most individual testes sampled in chimeras were clonally derived.
- In linear three-part chimeras, the position of a component was associated with the probability of resorption; the middle partner was resorbed less often than expected, while outside partners were resorbed more often.

This is a strong hint that “the chimera's genotype” is not a single scalar property. Somatic tissue composition, germline composition, morphology and growth can have different outcomes. Because this session did not retrieve the full paper text, regard detailed methodological/results claims here as abstract and structured-extraction level until primary full text can be inspected.

### D. 2004 environmental experiment: somatic composition shifts while germline parasitism can remain distinct

**Primary article identified; abstract and structured methods/results extracted, full primary text not directly opened in this session:**

Baruch Rinkevich & Irena Yankelevich, “Environmental split between germ cell parasitism and somatic cell synergism in chimeras of a colonial urochordate,” *Journal of Experimental Biology* 207 (2004), DOI 10.1242/jeb.01184. SciSpace record: https://scispace.com/papers/environmental-split-between-germ-cell-parasitism-and-somatic-492hzcd12p?utm_source=chatgpt&utm_medium=tool. Publisher full-PDF route listed in the record: https://jeb.biologists.org/content/207/20/3531.full.pdf.

The article's abstract reports:
- Colonies can fuse if they share at least one allele at the highly polymorphic fusibility/histocompatibility locus.
- A partner that is morphologically resorbed can still contribute circulating totipotent cells to the surviving partner's soma or germline.
- The two stem-cell lineage outcomes can be dissociated: somatic cell composition shifted with seawater temperature in the tested pairings, while germline parasitism showed little/no corresponding temperature shift.
- The chimera can therefore contain a composite of genotypes through two distinct, partly unlinked interactions: germline parasitism and somatic cell cooperation/competition.

The study directly challenges a simple inference from morphology: a colony that has disappeared as a visible body component may still have contributed cell lineages to the composite. Conversely, visible tissue dominance does not guarantee germline dominance.

### E. Molecular allorecognition update: more than a single-gene story

**2024 primary research:**

Henry Rodriguez-Valbuena et al., “Genetic and functional diversity of allorecognition receptors in the urochordate, *Botryllus schlosseri*,” *Nature Communications* (2024), PMID 39463968; open full-text record: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11507803/.

The indexed full-text results describe:
- The *fuhc* allorecognition locus includes highly polymorphic haplotypes.
- The previously identified receptors *fester* and *uncle fester* are not the whole picture. *Fester* is part of an expanded gene family (more than 37 loci in their analysis).
- The authors identify a diverse additional family of Fester co-receptors (FcoRs), with more than 50 candidate loci in their assembled data; these transmembrane proteins encode ITIM and hemITAM signaling motifs.
- The work supports a model in which activating and inhibitory signaling pathways are integrated to determine fusion or rejection.

The full PMC page could not be opened due a browser access challenge, but indexed full-text snippets expose the abstract and detailed results. Treat this as indexed article-text evidence rather than a fully inspected page-by-page read until full article access succeeds.

**2025 review:**

Anthony W. De Tomaso & Henry Rodriguez-Valbuena, “Histocompatibility in *Botryllus schlosseri* and the origins of adaptive immunity,” *Immunogenetics* 77 (2025), article 22, DOI 10.1007/s00251-025-01379-7. Full open Springer text: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00251-025-01379-7.

The review describes a broader architecture within the *fuhc* locus: multiple candidate ligands, receptors and signaling components, including *fester*, FcoRs, HE3L and a linked region containing BHF and other candidate ligands. It stresses that not every gene's role is resolved. It is a current synthesis and research agenda, not a new primary experiment. The key correction is to avoid describing the whole recognition apparatus as “a single gene.” BHF remains the gene identified in 2013 as a highly accurate predictor of fusion/rejection in the tested sets, while a broader receptor/signaling architecture is being worked out.

### F. New 2025 synthesis of marine allorecognition: individuality is dynamic and state-dependent

Baruch Rinkevich, “From seabed to sickbed: lessons gained from allorecognition in marine invertebrates,” *Frontiers in Immunology* 16 (10 April 2025), 1563685, DOI 10.3389/fimmu.2025.1563685. Full text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12018476/.

This is a perspective/review and proposes an “allorecognition landscape” to think about self/non-self as dynamic across combinations and time. Its useful empirically grounded observations include:
- *Botryllus* allorecognition is not merely a binary response with all colonies permanently classified as compatible or incompatible in every possible context; the review discusses transitive and non-transitive response hierarchies, indifference, reciprocal or unilateral outcomes and genotype-specific interactions.
- Compatible colony fusion can be followed by resorption of one morphologically visible partner.
- A colony can interact with more than one neighbor at once, and recognition is spatially localized to particular contacting ampullae.
- The review reports that colonies can form chimeras after donor tissue is introduced in ways that bypass the usual ampulla-level recognition interface, despite incompatible natural contact at peripheral vessels. This implies that recognition, cell survival and post-entry lineage behavior are separable stages.

The review author's broader claim that immunity evolved primarily to preserve individuality is a theoretical thesis, not an established universal evolutionary explanation. Do not elevate the proposed “allorecognition landscape” metaphor to a proven law. But the empirical point that different stages of contact, recognition, fusion, resorption and stem-cell competition can have different outcomes is highly relevant.

### G. September 2026 BHF population-genetics result

Agniya M. Sokolova, Jacob Douek & Baruch Rinkevich, “Intra-colony divergence and global allele sharing reflect purifying selection and recombination at the *Botryllus* histocompatibility factor locus,” *Immunogenetics* 78(1) (9 September 2026), article 21, DOI 10.1007/s00251-026-01413-2, PMID 42711581. PubMed full abstract: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42711581/.

The study analyzed full-length BHF alleles from 19 laboratory-born and wild colonies:
- Highly divergent alleles can coexist within individual colonies.
- Identical alleles can be shared across continental-scale distances.
- Despite pronounced nucleotide diversity and intragenic recombination, 33 alleles encoded only 17 distinct protein variants.
- The authors did not detect balancing or directional selection in their sample; they report signatures of purifying selection.
- They propose recombination, human-mediated gene flow and linkage to nearby loci under balancing selection as possible contributors to high nucleotide variation, while functional constraints preserve protein-level stability.

This is a recent population-genetic study of the **BHF locus**, not a new experiment on whole-colony fusion outcomes or long-term chimerism. It refines our understanding of the molecular recognition landscape but does not alter the basic empirical distinction among vascular fusion, lineage competition and organ-level integration.

### H. Comparison with Morgan 1899/1907: a continuity matrix

The Morgan graft examples and *Botryllus* together provide a useful series of different integration states:

| Relation | Morgan's historical animal grafts | *Botryllus schlosseri* |
|---|---|---|
| Physical tissue continuity | Cut surfaces unite; some grafts make persistent composite bodies | Natural ampullary fusion creates joined vascular tissue |
| Shared circulation | Cross-species earthworm grafts can share blood circulation | Common vascular network and circulation after compatible fusion |
| Regional lineage persistence | Mixed-species regenerates retain source-associated pigmentation | Mixed genetic cell lineages can persist after fusion |
| Neural continuity | Earthworm/planarian nerve-cord alignment affects regenerative pattern | Not the defining fusion criterion; hematopoietic/stem-cell and vascular systems are central |
| Developmental coupling | Fusion may remodel, split, absorb tissue or form new parts | Colony-level resorption and stem-cell migration/competition alter long-term composition |
| Somatic vs germline outcome | Not separately genotyped with modern lineage markers | Somatic and germline contributions can diverge |
| Recognition gate | Surgical contact bypasses natural compatibility; outcome depends on tissue/organism | Ampulla-level allorecognition can direct fusion or rejection |

The table is a conceptual comparison, not a claim that the organisms use homologous mechanisms.

### I. What this adds to our model, and what it does not prove

*Botryllus* demonstrates that natural biological fusion can make a multi-genotype vascularly integrated entity. It further shows that:
- morphological disappearance/resorption of one colony does not necessarily mean all of its cells or genetic contributions disappear;
- vascular continuity is not the same as homogeneous tissue identity;
- somatic and germline contributions can follow different histories;
- the compatibility mechanism itself is a spatially localized recognition process, and successful fusion is followed by a distinct competition phase;
- an organism-level composite may have a temporal identity that outlasts the visible morphology of one component.

This is a strong 3D biological counterexample to any simplistic rule that one body must correspond to one genome, one lineage, one visible morphology or one uninterrupted founder individual. It does **not** show the user's proposed extra-spatial 4D continuity among separate people, nor a life-tree structure linking individuals across historical time. The *Botryllus* relations are physically mediated through vessels, mobile cells, stem-cell competition and the ordinary time-course of development.

### J. Updated conceptual distinctions

The active biology taxonomy should now explicitly distinguish:
1. **Vascular union:** connection of fluid-transport systems.
2. **Tissue continuity:** actual integration of tissue layers or anatomical structures.
3. **Cellular chimerism:** persistent coexistence of genetically distinct cell lineages.
4. **Somatic lineage dominance:** which genotype occupies the non-reproductive body tissues.
5. **Germline contribution:** which genotype contributes reproductive cells.
6. **Morphological persistence/resorption:** whether the original visible colony structures remain.
7. **Allorecognition:** the mechanism that permits or rejects initial contact/fusion.
8. **Whole-colony integration:** long-term functional integration despite multi-genotype ancestry.

These dimensions can covary, but the *Botryllus* literature demonstrates that they can also come apart.

**Source-grade status:** 2013 Voskoboynik et al. primary article directly read in full PMC HTML. 2025 Rinkevich perspective directly read in full PMC HTML. 2025 De Tomaso/Rodriguez-Valbuena review read through Springer full text and excerpts. 2026 Sokolova et al. abstract read via PubMed. 2024 Rodriguez-Valbuena et al. article supported by indexed full-text snippets, with page-by-page direct access blocked. 2011 Carpenter et al. and 2004 Rinkevich/Yankelevich supported by abstracts plus structured article extraction, not full texts. No Tock completion or cursor advance.


## 63. Charles Zeleny: double-chela duplication, compensatory regulation and quantitative regeneration — 2026-11-24

This pass distinguishes three separate items that have sometimes been conflated: (1) Zeleny's 1905 single-case paper on a duplicated fiddler-crab claw; (2) his 102-page 1905 *Compensatory Regulation* paper; and (3) his later quantitative studies of regeneration rate. The first has been directly read; the second is located but not yet directly read; the third is directly inspectable in the 1916 monograph and a 1912 student thesis conducted under Zeleny's supervision.

### A. Zeleny 1905: regeneration of a true double chela

**Primary source directly OCR-inspected:** Charles Zeleny, “The Regeneration of a Double Chela in the Fiddler-Crab (*Gelasimus pugilator*) in Place of a Normal Single One,” *The Biological Bulletin* 9 (August 1905), 152–155. Complete searchable issue scan: https://file.iflora.cn/fastdfs/group2/M00/64/B0/wKhnol15uhmAGYBUAVWWdlgzX6g273.pdf. Web text locations: printed pp. 152–155 (PDF lines 2388–2453). Screenshot/page-image attempts failed with cache miss, so this is OCR-inspected but not image-verified.

**Procedure:**
- One male *Gelasimus pugilator* originally had a normal small right chela and a normal large left chela.
- On 2 July 1902, both chela nerves were injured with a needle, producing immediate autotomy of both appendages at the breaking joint.
- The crab was isolated and fed mussel (*Mytilus*).
- It molted on 4 August, 33 days after operation. Both regenerated chelae were substantial; the right/small-side regenerate was observed to be double.
- It was followed for 17 days after molt, 50 days after the operation.

**Anatomical result:**
- The first three podomeres were single and typical.
- The fourth podomere (propodite) divided terminally, with two separate indices and two associated dactylopodites.
- The split extended deeply into the propodite, especially on the index side; the two pinchers were near mirror images with slight differences in size/proportions.
- In life, both portions functioned as pinchers.
- Zeleny classed it as a “true double appendage” rather than an accessory projection that was itself bifid.

**Zeleny's hypothesis, explicitly tentative:** he suggests that after the regenerating bud had become specified, a mechanical force partly divided it along the plane of the future chela, leaving two nearly equal structures able to form complete pincers. He cannot identify the agency responsible for the division and proposes future experiments to test whether regeneration buds can be divided in a manner analogous to Spemann's constriction experiments on amphibian eggs.

**Important distinction:** the doubled chela is a true duplicated appendage, but the proposed mechanical splitting of a specified bud is an interpretation, not an observed mechanism. The paper reports one anomalous successful animal. Its footnote says the operation belonged to the larger compensatory-regulation experimental series then in press; it is not itself a summary of that programme.

### B. Zeleny 1905: *Compensatory Regulation* is a separate 102-page study

**Bibliographic identity verified, full primary text not yet retrieved:**
- Charles Zeleny, “Compensatory Regulation,” *Journal of Experimental Zoölogy* 2(1), 1–102, 27 text figures, April 1905, DOI 10.1002/jez.1400020102.
- Contemporary institutional bibliography: *Year-book of the Brooklyn Institute of Arts and Sciences* (1904–1905), p.265, lists the title, venue, volume/pages, 27 figures and April 1905 publication.
- The complete *Journal of Experimental Zoology*, vol. 2 (1905), is catalogued by Biodiversity Heritage Library, item 86598, University of Toronto/MBL copies: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/86598. Google Books/Play catalogue for the same volume: https://play.google.com/store/books/details/Ross_Granville_Harrison_Journal_of_Experimental_Zo?id=y7gBAAAAYAAJ.
- A specialist fiddler-crab bibliography identifies *Gelasimus pugilator* in Zeleny's article at printed pp. 5, 81–85, 88, 96 and 98–100: https://wwwf.fiddlercrab.info/references/Zeleny1905.2.html.
- A specialist regeneration bibliography describes the work as regulation and interaction among parts of the fiddler crab, carried out at Woods Hole and Naples under the direction of Davenport, Wilson and Morgan: https://www.academia.edu/5520307/Regeneration_research_at_the_Stazione_Zoologica_Anton_Dohrn_di_Napoli_a_bibliography_1873-1970s.

The BHL/Google Books volume is located, but the BHL item-level text reader and full-text route have returned cache/access errors in this session. The article is **not directly read**. Do not infer detailed experiments solely from the single-chela paper's footnote or later citations.

**Bibliographic correction:** some later sources, notably a Herrick/Bureau of Fisheries bibliography, misattribute *Compensatory Regulation* to *Biological Bulletin* vol. 11, pp. 1–102. The verified original citation is *Journal of Experimental Zoölogy* vol. 2, no. 1, pp. 1–102. The separate single double-chela case report is *Biological Bulletin* vol. 9, pp. 152–155.

### C. Zeleny 1916: controlled quantitative programme on the rate of regeneration

**Primary source directly OCR-inspected:** Charles Zeleny, *Studies on the Factors Controlling the Rate of Regeneration*, *Illinois Biological Monographs* 3(1) (1916), 1–170. Full scan: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1e/Illinois_biological_monographs_%28IA_illinoisbiologic03univ%29.pdf. Relevant text includes the introduction, Parts I, III and V, and data summaries on printed pp. 7–10, 61 onward and 136–155. Screenshot attempts on selected PDF pages failed with cache miss; record as OCR-inspected, not page-image verified.

This later monograph is not a substitute for the 1905 *Compensatory Regulation* paper. It is an expanded quantitative programme on how several independent factors affect regeneration rate:
- whether regeneration begins from newly produced versus older tissue;
- the number of successive removals;
- the anatomical level of the cut;
- where the animal is in the regeneration cycle;
- the degree of simultaneous injury.

The author emphasizes controlling age, nutritional state, temperature, size, molt timing and level of amputation. He also says that individual variation and unmeasured factors cannot be completely eliminated. In some comparisons he delegates measurements to observers who do not know the expected result to reduce measurement bias.

#### C1. Degree of injury: salamander foreleg experiment, 1913

In *Amblystoma punctatum*, Zeleny compares three injury conditions:
1. right foreleg alone removed;
2. both forelegs removed;
3. both forelegs plus one-half of the tail removed.

In the main series, young from the same egg lot were selected to make individuals as similar as possible. The regenerate lengths of forelegs were recorded at 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 16 and 19 days. The study used five replicate sets; typically one individual from each injury condition was killed and measured at each timepoint.

Zeleny reports that simultaneous removal of additional parts did not retard the regenerating foreleg. In most timepoint comparisons, the foreleg with additional injury regenerated more than the single-injury condition; adding tail removal did not clearly accelerate it further than the second foreleg removal. The effect was variable across days and the groups converged toward the later measurements.

**Descriptive calculation from Zeleny's reported mean lengths (mm):**

| Days | Right foreleg alone | Both forelegs | Both forelegs + half-tail |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 | 0.13 | 0.16 | 0.15 |
| 4 | 0.22 | 0.36 | 0.29 |
| 6 | 0.42 | 0.53 | 0.55 |
| 8 | 0.66 | 0.83 | 0.73 |
| 10 | 0.91 | 1.34 | 1.24 |
| 12 | 1.48 | 1.60 | 1.61 |
| 14 | 1.98 | 2.19 | 2.29 |
| 16 | 3.02 | 3.01 | 3.08 |
| 19 | 3.84 | 3.64 | 3.90 |

Calculated directly from those nine reported means:
- Both-forelegs condition exceeds the single-foreleg condition at 7 of 9 timepoints; the mean ratio across the nine separately reported timepoint means is 1.221 (about +22.1%). The two exceptions are the 16-day point (about −0.3%) and 19-day point (about −5.2%).
- Both-forelegs-plus-half-tail exceeds the single-foreleg condition at all 9 timepoints; the mean ratio across timepoints is 1.170 (about +17.0%).
- These are descriptive averages of ratios across timepoints, not estimates of a single biological treatment effect. The timepoints use different sacrificed animals, and the available summary does not include standard errors or inferential tests for all these ratios. Do not interpret the mean ratios as a pooled effect size.

Zeleny's own interpretation is cautious: additional injury, especially of a corresponding organ, may accelerate regeneration within a certain range; excessive injury may exceed an optimum and then reduce regeneration. He stresses that some differences are small and may lie within probable measurement error.

#### C2. The relationship between tissue stage and regeneration rate

In the regeneration-cycle chapter, Zeleny reports an initial slow phase during cell migration, a faster phase associated with cell multiplication and limited differentiation, then a declining rate associated with visible cellular differentiation. He says that there is likely a causal relationship but that available knowledge does not yet allow him to explain the control mechanism.

This provides a useful historical quantitative analogue for a biological process in which tissue structure and regenerative velocity change together over time. It does not show that one universal compensatory force regulates all the parts.

### D. Zeleny's 1912 supervised thesis: independent quantitative replication in crayfish

**Primary thesis directly OCR-inspected:** Arthur Thompson Evans, *Effect of Degree of Injury upon Rate of Regeneration and Rate of Molting in the Crayfish, Cambarus blandingi* (University of Illinois BA thesis, 1912), 16 pages, University of Illinois repository:
https://www.ideals.illinois.edu/items/52284/bitstreams/151105/data.pdf.

The thesis title page and approval record identify Zeleny as instructor in charge. Its text explicitly describes the experiment as conducted in the zoological laboratory under Dr. Charles Zeleny's direction.

**Design:**
- Two lots of young crayfish (*Cambarus blandingi*), 33 and 54 animals at start.
- Comparison: removal of the right chela only versus removal of both chelae.
- Conditions controlled as far as possible for size/age, laboratory history, temperature, sex (young specimens), food and operation timing relative to the molt.
- Operations performed on the second day after the first recorded molt.
- The propodite of the large chela was measured to 0.01 mm after a later molt; because some claws had not regrown after one molt, the measure covered two molt periods.
- Individuals that died or molted late were excluded by design; the experiment was terminated when ten matched pairs had molted in each series. A few markedly abnormal regeneration cases were excluded from the summary but remained visible in the underlying record.

**Reported results:**
- Greater injury (both chelae removed) produced 0.30 mm more regenerated length than lesser injury in Series I (+8%) and 0.17 mm more in Series II (+5%).
- Specific amount of regeneration, defined as regenerated length divided by thoracic length, increased by 7% in Series I and nearly 5% in Series II.
- Specific rate, defined as specific amount divided by days regenerating (here measured over two molt periods), increased by 16% in Series I and 22% in Series II.
- Molting occurred 5.5 days earlier in the more severely injured group in Series I and 4.33 days earlier in Series II.

The thesis author proposes several possible explanations rather than a proven mechanism: greater functional demand for food capture and defense after loss of both claws; exercise effects; possible reduced investment in a remaining claw when it can increase efficiency; increased nutrient availability after removal of large, muscular, heavily used chelae; and an associated shortening of molt interval. These should be treated as hypotheses, not as established causal mechanisms.

The thesis itself notes that excessive injury can exceed Zeleny's “optimum limit” and slow regeneration. It criticizes one contrary lobster experiment as potentially exceeding that limit and failing to control food/feeding ability, but this is the student's interpretation. It is useful as a contemporary follow-up in Zeleny's laboratory, not an independently blinded, modern replication.

### E. An older precursor: Zeleny's compensatory regulation of a hydroid operculum (1902)

**Bibliographic identity confirmed, primary article body not yet located:**
Charles Zeleny, “A Case of Compensatory Regulation in the Regeneration of *Hydroides dianthus*,” *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik der Organismen* 13 (1902), 597–609, three figures.
- Contemporary *Zoologischer Jahresbericht für 1902* lists title, journal, volume, pages and three figures: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6d/Zoologischer_Jahresbericht_%28IA_zoologischerjahr1902staz%29.pdf (printed page 16 in the Vermes bibliography, p.597–609).
- Later bibliographies likewise cite the full article; one 1973 paper discusses later research on *Hydroides* opercular regulation and lists the 1902 Zeleny paper.
- The original article itself has not yet been directly read. It is an important next target because it appears to be the earlier source behind Zeleny's use of “compensatory regulation” before the 1905 fiddler-crab programme.

Do not assume the Hydroides model and fiddler-crab model use identical mechanisms. The hydroid paper may concern an operculum/paired opercular structures, whereas the 1905 crab programme concerns chela morphology and the relationship between paired appendages.

### F. What Zeleny contributes to the current histological inquiry

Zeleny's work separates several types of organization:
- **Morphological duplication:** one regenerative bud produces two appendage-like structures.
- **Paired-part compensation:** injury/removal of one structure changes the size, morphology or developmental path of another.
- **Regenerative rate regulation:** additional injury may alter the velocity and timing of regeneration.
- **Molt coupling:** regeneration and molt timing can change together.
- **Developmental-stage dependence:** regenerative velocity varies with the histological/ontogenetic stage of the new tissue.

This is a valuable extension of Morgan and Loeb: biological interdependence need not only be physical connection or shared circulation; one body part's loss can change the fate or rate of development of another. But “compensatory” does not automatically mean the body returns to a globally optimized state, and “regulatory” does not identify one mechanism. The mechanisms may involve functional demand, neural input, systemic nutrients, growth signals, developmental state and molt timing.

For the user's 4D proposal, this furnishes another conventional biological control class: inter-part regulation over developmental time. It remains ordinary within-organism physiology/development and does not demonstrate extra-spatial physical continuity across distinct individuals.

**Source-grade status:** Zeleny 1905 double-chela paper directly OCR-inspected; Zeleny 1916 monograph directly OCR-inspected at relevant sections and numerical tables; Evans 1912 thesis directly OCR-inspected, with page screenshots attempted but cache-missed. Zeleny's 1905 *Compensatory Regulation* is bibliographically verified and located in BHL/Google Books but not directly read. Zeleny's 1902 *Hydroides* paper is bibliographically verified via contemporary bibliography but not directly read. No Tock completion or cursor advance.

## 64. Zeleny's Hydroides opercular compensation and Okada's experimental dissection of the mechanism — 2026-11-24

The Hydroides branch now has a substantial primary-text basis even though the original Zeleny 1902 and 1911 articles remain incompletely retrieved. The key source is Yô K. Okada's own experimental paper on the same reversible opercular asymmetry in a closely related species.

### A. What Zeleny first reported: reciprocal transformation of paired opercula

**Zeleny bibliographic record:** “A Case of Compensatory Regulation in the Regeneration of *Hydroides dianthus*,” *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik der Organismen* 13, 597–609, 3 figures. Contemporary *Zoologischer Jahresbericht* lists it in the 1902 report; Okada (1933) refers to Zeleny's first observation as 1901, and later reviews also vary between 1901 and 1902. A 1909 French review by Ch. Gravier calls it Zeleny (1902), and Przibram (1909) cites the phenomenon as Zeleny 1902. The safest bibliographic representation is **1901/1902, volume XIII (published/cited across the 1901–1902 boundary), pp.597–609**, pending the original title page. Do not erase this date discrepancy.
- Contemporary bibliography: *Zoologischer Jahresbericht* for 1902, OCR result: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6d/Zoologischer_Jahresbericht_%28IA_zoologischerjahr1902staz%29.pdf.
- Near-contemporary French summary: Ch. Gravier, *Annales des sciences naturelles*, 9th series, vol. IX (1909), printed pp. 145–146; complete searchable text: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/78/Annales_des_sciences_naturelles_%28IA_annalesdesscienc99miln%29.pdf.
- Full later experimental discussion: Yô K. Okada (1933), below.

In *Hydroides* there are two corresponding opercula, each derived from a branchial/gill filament. In a typical asymmetric animal one is a large, functional plug that closes the opening of its calcareous tube, while the other remains small and rudimentary.

Okada's historical summary (1933, printed pp.655–656) attributes these results to Zeleny:
- Remove the functional operculum: the opposite rudimentary operculum begins growing and within weeks can reach the size and function of the one removed.
- A new rudimentary organ develops at the stump on the original side.
- The animal therefore switches from right-handed to left-handed or vice versa; the switch may be reversed again by another operation.
- Remove the rudimentary operculum alone: the functional operculum remains unchanged.
- Remove both at once: sometimes the former rudimentary side becomes functional first, but sometimes two functional opercula develop.
- Zeleny's thoracic cut experiments could yield two functional opercula; Okada suggests that absence or immaturity of the cerebral ganglia while the opercula are forming may explain failure to establish the usual asymmetry, but this is Okada's interpretation, not a proven neural mechanism.

Gravier's 1909 account independently says that cutting the developed operculum at its base produces a small stump without ordinary regeneration, while the opposite rudiment develops into the removed organ's form and size; functional balance is restored. Gravier calls it “régularisation compensatrice.”

### B. Zeleny's 1911 follow-up: cut level affects whether reversal or double function appears

**Primary paper located but not yet directly read:** Charles Zeleny, “Experiments on the Control of Asymmetry in the Development of the Serpulid, *Hydroides dianthus*,” *Journal of Morphology* 22 (1911), 927–944, 7 figures.
- Contemporary 1911 *Zoologischer Jahresbericht* author index confirms title, volume, pages and figures: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/44/Zoologischer_Jahresbericht_%28IA_zoologischerjahr1911staz%29.pdf.
- BHL volume 22 record: item 43304, *Journal of Morphology* (1911), MBLWHOI scan, public domain: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/43304.
- Volume contents independently list Zeleny's paper beginning p.927: https://embryology.med.unsw.edu.au/embryology/index.php?title=Journal_of_Morphology_22_%281911%29.

Okada (1933, pp.657–659) provides a detailed contemporary summary of Zeleny's 1911 cutting-level experiments:
- Removal of only the upper cup of the functional operculum usually does **not** reverse asymmetry.
- Injury below the upper cup, to the main body or stalk, does induce compensatory growth of the opposite rudiment.
- Zeleny found that cuts at the most distal level and the most proximal level tend to reverse asymmetry, whereas intermediate-level cuts more often result in two functional opercula.
- Zeleny's proposed explanation is a difference in the state and timing of the regenerate at the original functional side. A distal cut may leave the basal embryonic tissue relatively undamaged; a proximal cut may injure it directly; both can delay or impair regeneration on the original side while the opposite rudiment gets a developmental head start. Intermediate cuts allow more rapid growth of a regenerate at the original functional site, so it may coexist with the now-developing opposite operculum.
- Zeleny regarded the basal region below the breaking joint as embryonic or developmentally active. Okada tests that anatomical idea and finds the connective tissue there not obviously different from surrounding tissue; he sees somewhat taller and more crowded ectodermal cells but cautions that this alone is insufficient evidence to call it embryonic.

This is not a simple “remove one part, the other grows” rule. The site and depth of injury alter the relative developmental trajectories of both structures and may produce reversal, two functional organs or other outcomes.

### C. Okada 1933: direct experimental work on Hydroides norvegica

**Primary source directly read in full searchable text:** Yô K. Okada, “Remarks on the Reversible Asymmetry in the Opercula of the Polychaete *Hydroides*,” *Journal of the Marine Biological Association of the United Kingdom* 18(2) (January 1933), 655–670, 5 figures. DOI 10.1017/S0025315400043976. Complete PDF and OCR: https://plymsea.ac.uk/id/eprint/799/1/Remarks_on_the_reversible_asymmetry_in_the_opercula_of_the_polychaete_hydroides.pdf. Text lines in the opened full PDF include pp.655–670. Screenshot/page-image service returned cache miss, so this is OCR-inspected, not image-verified. Keep the species distinct: Okada's own experiments are on *Hydroides norvegica* from the Plymouth district, while his historical summary discusses Zeleny's *H. dianthus* and allied species.

Okada first confirms that the animal can switch sides:
- The functional operculum restrains development of its opposite rudiment.
- Its removal allows the opposite rudiment to grow.
- Transverse thoracic cuts can produce two nearly symmetrical rudiments; in most specimens one begins growing faster and becomes the sole functional organ.
- In his series, double functional opercula were relatively uncommon after thoracic cuts alone (a few out of roughly 50 in three repeated series), but could be induced more readily by injuring the growing tip of the rudimentary operculum after removing the functional one.
- If the brain remained attached to the side with the rudimentary operculum after removal of half the head, that rudiment became functional. If the brain was removed together with the functional operculum, the opposite rudiment did not grow until the missing half of the head had regenerated. Okada interprets this as a possible influence of the cerebral ganglia, but the observations do not isolate a specific neural signal.

### D. Operculum histology: a functional branchial filament with a vascular core

Okada describes the functional operculum as comprising a distal crown/cup and a long proximal shaft. It is a modified gill filament:
- Beneath the cuticle is tall epithelium.
- Beneath that is a reticular connective tissue containing a large axial blood vessel.
- The vessel travels up the shaft, branches to the proximal cup, then passes into the upper cup and the central region of radiating spines.
- Each spine receives a direct blood supply, while distal parts of the spines and leaf-like processes lack direct blood supply.
- The opposite rudimentary organ is histologically more embryonic-looking: club-shaped, with plump cells, large nuclei and strong staining. As it differentiates, it develops into shaft and two cups.

This supplies a useful histological anatomy for the functional/rudimentary difference. But Okada cautions against treating the developmental state of the functional operculum's base as proven solely from cell crowding.

### E. Okada's selective injury experiments: the inhibitory property is localized to the intact organ

Okada tests which portions of the functional operculum have to remain intact to restrain the opposite rudiment:
1. Removing all distal spines and half of the proximal cup while leaving the other half and the entire shaft intact still caused the rudimentary organ to become fully functional in about two weeks.
2. Removing one side of each cup, leaving more than half of each, also caused growth of the rudimentary organ.
3. Removing just the distal circlet of spines while retaining a complete proximal cup slowed the opposite rudiment's growth, but it still became functional; the same occurred when one side of the proximal cup was cut while the distal spines remained.
4. Okada concludes that the two cup structures, as complete structures, are needed for full inhibition.
5. Removing the peripheral spines and leaf-like processes outside the direct blood supply had no effect on the regulatory relation.
6. A simple fine-needle puncture, even if deep, had no effect.
7. A deeper wound that destroyed internal organization and caused greenish blood to escape almost invariably allowed the rudimentary organ to grow.

This distinction is important: the relevant perturbation is not merely “damage” or “stimulation of nerves.” The severity and depth of injury, and whether it disrupts the organ's internal constitution, determine the response.

### F. Attempts to distinguish neural reflex, chemical stimulus and local inhibitory action

Okada evaluates three possible explanations:
- **Nervous reflex:** he rejects the ordinary reflex account as inadequate. The effect depends on the degree and kind of local injury, and superficial cuts do not provoke reversal. This does not prove that the nervous system has no developmental role: his own brain-removal observations suggest the brain may be necessary in some contexts. The narrower conclusion is that the opercular compensation is not adequately explained as a simple reflex triggered by wounded nerves.
- **Blood-borne or secreted chemical growth stimulant:** he first ligated the functional operculum's shaft and injured the distal region, expecting ligature to block transport of a putative signal. However, ligation alone also caused growth of the rudimentary organ, confounding interpretation. He then collected internal material from 20 wounded opercula, diluted/filtered it and injected it via the thoracic vein into ten intact animals. None showed a reversal or altered growth even after more than two weeks. This is a negative result for his injected-substance hypothesis, not proof that no chemical mediation is possible.
- **Local loss of inhibition:** Okada's preferred working conclusion is that injury directly destroys the intact functional operculum's inhibitory influence, allowing the opposite rudiment to develop. He says that the exact physical/chemical basis is unknown. The inhibitory influence appears to be localized in the operculum, not generally in the gill or rest of the body: cutting off all gill filaments or other body parts without injuring the intact functional operculum does not trigger reversal.

### G. Okada's final model: a local antagonistic pair with developmental threshold and timing

In the discussion and summary (pp.660–669), Okada develops a model in which the two organ rudiments are initially capable of developing on either side. Ordinarily one grows faster, becomes functional and inhibits the other from doing the same. Once the functional organ is injured deeply enough, its inhibitory property is lost; the opposite rudiment begins to grow and takes over. At the original side, a new small rudiment forms, and the now functional opposite organ restrains it. If both sides develop to similar states, the outcome may be two functional or two rudimentary opercula. Okada suggests a threshold process to explain which side first gains dominance, with a general rightward growth tendency in *H. norvegica* but a labile, reversible asymmetry.

The paper's own final summary emphasizes:
- asymmetry is due to a growth difference between two corresponding organs;
- the functional organ holds its mate in check;
- removal/injury of the functional organ releases the rudimentary side;
- the effect is not a straightforward nervous reflex and is not proven to be a chemical growth stimulant;
- inhibition is local to the operculum and depends on intact internal organization;
- the functional operculum has no regenerative capacity, while the rudimentary one retains it;
- with suitable injury/developmental timing, two functional or two rudimentary organs can be produced;
- in *H. norvegica*, the right side usually grows faster, but the asymmetry remains labile.

### H. Comparison with Alpheus: similar relational form, distinct biological mechanism

Both Hydroides and Alpheus exhibit a paired-organ regulatory relationship:
- one member is functionally dominant and the opposite member remains rudimentary or specialized;
- removing or damaging the functional structure changes the developmental fate of the opposite side;
- the new functional organ then restrains the development of the newly regenerated rudiment on its own side;
- outcomes depend on the timing and developmental stage of the two sides.

But the mechanisms are not interchangeable:
- In *Alpheus*, evidence points to nerve-mediated lateral inhibition, neural remodeling, muscle-fiber re-specification and molt-dependent transformation.
- In Okada's *Hydroides*, the immediate inhibitory property is experimentally localized to the internal constitution of the functional operculum itself; deep injury and blood leakage trigger reversal, while superficial injury, simple needle puncture, or cutting other gill structures do not. Neural and chemical roles remain unresolved, and Okada rejects a simple reflex explanation.

The common pattern is relational asymmetry: the developmental state of one member of a pair depends on the state/integrity of the other. The causal implementations differ.

### I. Model relevance to the 4D histological inquiry

Hydroides provides a strong anatomical example of **local paired-organ inhibition** where histological organization matters:
- The functional operculum is itself a differentiated gill filament with epithelial, connective, vascular and cuticular substructures.
- Its internal constitution exerts a regulatory effect on a separate rudimentary organ on the opposite side.
- A wound to selected substructures changes the developmental trajectory of the opposite organ.
- The body can reversibly transfer the functional role from one side to the other, or be experimentally driven into two functional or two rudimentary states.

This is a clear case where organismal organization cannot be understood by looking at either operculum in isolation. But the experiments show local, ordinary physical and developmental regulation in a 3D animal. They do not show an extra spatial dimension or physical continuity among separate organisms.

For the working taxonomy, add:
1. **Inter-organ inhibition:** one developed organ locally restrains a paired rudiment.
2. **Developmental asymmetry with reversibility:** left/right role assignment can be changed experimentally.
3. **Injury-depth sensitivity:** the organ's regulatory influence depends on specific internal structures, not any wound.
4. **Threshold/timing dynamics:** relative growth states determine whether the result is reversal, two functional organs or two rudimentary organs.
5. **Histological localization:** cell organization and vascular anatomy constrain the mechanism, but do not alone identify it.

### J. Source status and next operations

- Zeleny 1901/1902 *Hydroides dianthus* original: bibliographically secured, original pages not yet retrieved. Date varies across sources: 1901 in Okada's citation, 1902 in contemporary annual bibliography and several later sources.
- Zeleny 1911 *Hydroides dianthus* paper, *Journal of Morphology* 22:927–944: BHL volume 22 item 43304 located; content summarized in detail by Okada 1933 but not yet directly read.
- Okada 1933 *Hydroides* paper: full 16-page paper directly OCR-inspected pp.655–670, not page-image verified due screenshot cache miss.
- Gravier 1909 French literature review: directly OCR-inspected; its Zeleny summary supports the functional/rudimentary transformation, but it is secondary.
- Zeleny 1905 102-page *Compensatory Regulation* remains located but unread; do not confuse it with the *Hydroides* paper or the single 1905 double-chela case.

**Next:** obtain the Zeleny 1911 article from BHL volume 22 item 43304 or an alternate full-text source; then return to Zeleny's 1905 102-page *Compensatory Regulation*. The Okada 1933 primary experiment already supplies a well-controlled comparison of the mechanism, so do not wait on those older texts before integrating this case into the biology taxonomy. No Tock closure or cursor advance.

## 65. Schochet's 1973 two-part Hydroides programme: natural life-course reversals and neural involvement — 2026-11-24

The Okada/Zeleny interpretation is now extended by Joy Schochet's two experimental papers on *Hydroides dianthus*. These are especially important because they distinguish (a) naturally occurring reversals across an individual lifespan, (b) local inhibitory effects of the functional operculum, and (c) nervous pathways that may be required to execute or trigger reversal. They qualify Okada's 1933 conclusion that no simple nervous reflex explains the phenomenon.

### A. Part I: opercular ontogeny, distribution and flux

**Primary paper identified; abstract and structured extraction inspected, full article not directly opened in this session:**

Joy Schochet, “Opercular Regulation in the Polychaete *Hydroides dianthus* (Verrill, 1873). I. Opercular Ontogeny, Distribution and Flux,” *The Biological Bulletin* 144 (1973), DOI 10.2307/1540017, open-access BHL part 15261. SciSpace record: https://scispace.com/papers/opercular-regulation-in-the-polychaete-hydroides-dianthus-2dgebrb05g. Full BHL item/part: https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/part/15261.

The abstract reports:
- Although a population maintains roughly equal proportions of right- and left-handed animals, individual *H. dianthus* continually modify their side assignment.
- Two routes can produce reversal. The functional operculum may spontaneously autotomize, allowing the rudimentary partner to enlarge; or the rudimentary operculum may develop independently, temporarily producing two large opercula, after which the older functional organ autotomizes.
- Left- and right-handed individuals change at equal rates, so the population remains balanced despite flux in individual handedness.
- An individual can undergo several reversals during a normal lifespan, beginning in larval stages. The rate gradually declines with age.

This materially changes the biological interpretation: reversal is not merely a laboratory response to an experimental wound. It is part of normal ontogenetic/life-history variation in this species. A population-level 50:50 left/right ratio can therefore coexist with repeated individual-level transitions; static cross-sectional handedness frequencies alone do not reveal the organism's history.

The available abstract is the direct source extract in this session. It does not expose the detailed sample sizes, exact observation periods, or statistical methods. Do not supply those from inference.

### B. Part II: control of opercular regulation

**Primary paper identified; abstract and structured article-level extraction inspected, full text not directly opened:**

Joy Schochet, “Opercular Regulation in the Polychaete *Hydroides dianthus* (Verrill, 1873). II. Control of Opercular Regulation,” *Journal of Experimental Zoology* 184(2) (1973), DOI 10.1002/jez.1401840210. SciSpace record: https://scispace.com/papers/opercular-regulation-in-the-polychaete-hydroides-dianthus-4ktlcj49en. Publisher DOI page: https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/jez.1401840210. This paper is not the same as the *Biological Bulletin* Part I.

The abstract reports the following controlled experiments:
- Amputating the adult functional operculum below the secondary cup causes the opposite rudimentary organ to become functional, while a new rudiment regenerates from the stump of the former functional organ.
- Reversal is proportional to injury severity, with reported order: amputation > crushing > ligation > puncture.
- Removing the rudimentary operculum alone does not affect the functional organ, but in some cases the regenerating rudiment may differentiate into a second functional operculum.
- Removing distal parts of both opercula can produce reversal. Removing both at their bases leads to a functional operculum forming on the side opposite its original position.
- Removing the branchial apparatus together with both opercula tends to result in regeneration on the original side.
- **Neural operations:** decerebration or bisection of the supraoesophageal ganglion does not prevent normal reversal. Severing the external opercular nerves prevents reversal, while sectioning the internal opercular nerve produces reversal in about one-third of the animals.
- Schochet concludes that the opercular regulatory system discriminates among degrees and types of injury, and that the branchiae may participate in opercular relationships.

The extracted structured summary gives a potentially discordant formulation of the nerve experiments in one secondary field (it suggests all animals in a nervous-system interruption group reversed within a week); because the original article body is not yet opened and that is not transparently reconcilable with the abstract's external-vs-internal nerve result, **do not use the all-animals-within-a-week statement**. Retain the abstract's clear summary only and mark detailed sample sizes/timing as unverified.

### C. Reconciling Okada 1933 and Schochet 1973

The findings should not be collapsed into “neural” versus “non-neural”:
- Okada (1933) found that ordinary superficial injury, a simple needle puncture, or removal of structures outside the blood-supplied internal core did not induce reversal. Deep injury to the functional operculum causing internal disruption and blood leakage usually did; he could not demonstrate a transmissible substance by ligation/injection experiments. He concluded that injury destroys an inhibitory property local to the functional operculum and rejected a simple reflex model.
- Schochet (1973 II) reports that the cerebral/supraoesophageal ganglion is not required, but external opercular nerves are necessary for reversal in her operations, while internal-nerve section yields a partial response. This suggests nervous pathways may be required for the operation or execution of the regulatory process without the process being a simple reflex generated by the brain.
- These results are not necessarily contradictory. “Nervous involvement” can mean a local peripheral pathway, maintenance of tissue state, trophic support, effector coordination, or a sensory reflex. The experiments described do not establish which role applies.
- Injury severity matters in both Okada and Schochet. The tissue's internal state, the particular pathway interrupted and whether it remains able to regenerate all condition the outcome.

### D. Updated relational model for the Hydroides opercula

A cautious integrated formulation is:
1. Two corresponding organs develop with different states: one functional, the other rudimentary.
2. The functional organ's intact internal organization participates in suppressing or restraining the opposite rudiment.
3. The effect depends on the depth and kind of injury; superficial damage is not equivalent to internal disruption.
4. Peripheral neural routes influence the ability to reverse, but the supraoesophageal ganglion is not required in the reported Schochet experiments.
5. Developmental timing and relative growth state determine whether reversal, double functionality or other outcomes occur.
6. In ordinary life, the animal may reverse repeatedly; population handedness proportions can remain balanced even as individual animals change.

This is more layered than a simple “one side inhibits the other.” It points to a distributed local control architecture involving organ integrity, tissue state, peripheral nerve pathways, relative growth and developmental history. But the precise molecular mechanism remains unresolved.

### E. Cross-comparison with Alpheus and relevance to the 4D histological question

Hydroides and *Alpheus* share a relational form but not an established identical mechanism:
- Both involve paired structures with unequal differentiation.
- The state of one member of the pair affects development of the other.
- Injury to the dominant structure can transfer functional dominance to the previously subordinate structure.
- The relationship can be disrupted to produce two functional structures.

Differences:
- *Alpheus* reversal is closely tied to a molt-dependent crustacean claw transformation and has strong modern evidence for lateral inhibition, neural remodeling and muscle-fiber re-specification.
- *Hydroides* opercular asymmetry can reverse spontaneously in the normal life course, apparently several times, and Schochet's experimental work implicates peripheral nerve pathways while not requiring the supraoesophageal ganglion.
- The *Hydroides* organs are modified gill filaments with a vascular core, epithelial and connective tissue components, a proximal shaft and distal cup structures.

For the workshop's broader model, this is valuable as a documented **inter-organ relation with reversibility and normal temporal flux**. It shows that the functionally dominant side is a dynamic state, not necessarily a fixed lifetime identity. It remains an ordinary 3D developmental/physiological process; it does not imply extra-spatial continuity or inter-individual structural fusion.
