- Zeleny 1901/1902 *H. dianthus* paper: bibliographically secured; original body still not directly read.
- Zeleny 1911 *H. dianthus* paper: BHL volume 22 item 43304 located; Okada 1933 summarizes relevant experiments; full Zeleny article not directly read.
- Okada 1933 *H. norvegica* study: full text OCR-inspected in section 64, not page-image verified.
- Schochet 1973 Part I: abstract/structured extraction only; full article not directly opened. BHL part 15261 identifies open-access scan.
- Schochet 1973 Part II: abstract/structured extraction only; full article not directly opened; detailed nerve sample counts and methods not verified.

**Next:** retrieve Schochet Part I via BHL item 15261 and inspect its figures/observations on opercular ontogeny and lifetime reversal; retrieve Part II via Wiley/author repository and verify the nerve-section sample counts and exact sequence; then revisit the original Zeleny 1905 *Compensatory Regulation* for the wider paired-organ programme. No Tock completion or cursor advance.


## 66. Morgan 1923/1924: developmental establishment and persistent memory of fiddler-crab handedness — 2026-11-24

The Morgan fiddler-crab branch is now clarified by pairing his two papers with an independent 1977 experimental study, a 2012 developmental synthesis, and later work on post-autotomy claw morphology. The important distinction is between **establishing which side becomes major** and **regenerating the structure after handedness has already been established**.

### A. Morgan 1923: the initial experiment and its acknowledged uncertainty

**Primary citation:** T. H. Morgan, “The Development of Asymmetry in the Fiddler Crab,” *The American Naturalist* 57(650), 269–273 (May 1923), DOI 10.1086/279921. SciSpace article record/abstract: https://scispace.com/papers/the-development-of-asymmetry-in-the-fiddler-crab-26ogkdb4rw.

The full journal page is catalogued by University of Chicago Press, but its article reader/PDF returned access errors in this session. The available SciSpace abstract and structured article extraction have been read; the whole paper has **not** been directly inspected. Accordingly, details below should be cited as abstract/extraction-derived, not full-text reading.

Morgan begins from the known contrast between crustacean groups:
- In *Alpheus* and some other decapods, loss of the large claw can lead the contralateral smaller claw to become large.
- In fiddler crabs, loss of the large claw does not cause the small opposite claw to transform; the large claw is regenerated at the same stump.
- Very young fiddler crabs initially have two small, similar chelipeds. Morgan asks whether removing one during this symmetrical stage will cause the intact one to become large.

**Field observations and experiment:**
- Juveniles were collected at North Falmouth, Massachusetts, on 10 July, 3 August, 2 September and 15 September 1922.
- Developmental stage was classified by carapace width, chela size, shape and bilateral equality.
- Morgan tested removal of one claw in young crabs whose paired claws were initially equivalent, then observed the animals after subsequent molts.
- He also recorded claw laterality in 524 adult males: 241 had a right major claw and 283 a left major claw. This is near a 1:1 split rather than evidence of a fixed population-wide right or left bias.

In one especially relevant group of 18 juveniles with still-symmetrical claws, eight developed the large male-type claw on the intact side after removal of the opposite claw; ten regenerated a small claw. However, their sex could not be identified, and young males may naturally pass through a temporary two-large-claw phase. Morgan explicitly treats the result as suggestive, not yet decisive: the observed transformation might be temporary rather than a permanent setting of handedness.

### B. Morgan 1924: larger follow-up and the symmetric two-small-claw result

**Primary citation:** T. H. Morgan, “The Artificial Induction of Symmetrical Claws in Male Fiddler Crabs,” *The American Naturalist* 58(657), 289–295 (1 July 1924), DOI 10.1086/279981. SciSpace article record/abstract: https://scispace.com/papers/the-artificial-induction-of-symmetrical-claws-in-male-17ovvayj13.

Again, the publisher's free article page was blocked in this session; I inspected its abstract and structured article extraction, not the complete article body. The article abstract says that Morgan repeated the prior work with a larger number of cases and considered the earlier temporary-change concern. Its central claims are explicit:
- Young male fiddler crabs pass through a stage in which they possess two large claws.
- Loss of one of these two large claws during the critical stage determines the later fixed asymmetry: the remaining claw becomes the “fiddle” (major), while the replacement at the lost side becomes the small claw.
- A new result: when **both large claws are removed simultaneously** during the critical stage, two small claws regenerate and remain small through later molts.
- Thus, if the young male does not become unbalanced by the loss of only one claw during this developmental window, it does not establish the usual one-major/one-minor asymmetry.

This clarifies the developmental event far better than the 1923 abstract alone. The side is not predetermined by an inherent right/left program in the individual: the early loss event selects which side retains the future major-claw trajectory. But Morgan's actual 1924 paper is still only abstract/extraction-inspected here; exact sample sizes, molt intervals, and full statistical presentation remain outstanding.

### C. Independent experimental confirmation: Yamaguchi 1977 in Uca lactea

**Primary article and full abstract directly read on the journal page:** Takao Yamaguchi, “Studies on the Handedness of the Fiddler Crab, *Uca lactea*,” *The Biological Bulletin* 152(3), 424–436 (1 June 1977), DOI 10.2307/1540430. Journal page: https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.2307/1540430.

Yamaguchi's abstract reports:
- Early juveniles have two small chelipeds with no morphological difference between the sexes.
- All males above 5.3 mm carapace width exhibited cheliped asymmetry.
- Artificial removal of one cheliped from megalopae or young crabs that still had symmetrical chelipeds induced handedness; the large cheliped always developed on the **intact** side.
- When both chelipeds were removed simultaneously before asymmetry was attained, males failed to develop a giant claw and retained two small chelipeds permanently. The abstract says reproductive function was not detectably abnormal.
- If a male did not lose a cheliped, two giant chelipeds developed.
- Once handedness was established, removal of one or both chelipeds could not reverse it.

This is a very close independent replication of the Morgan developmental logic, in a named species. It also resolves an important timing distinction: removal of both claws **before** handedness is established yields two small claws; removal of both **after** handedness is established preserves the already acquired right/left regenerative target.

### D. What is actually “fixed”: positional target, not an unchanging claw

A crucial interpretive correction: the major appendage itself is not a permanently intact structure. It can be lost and regenerated. What persists is its **side assignment** or the organism's developmental target morphology:
- before asymmetry is established, a single claw loss on one side selects the opposite intact side for future major development;
- after establishment, subsequent removal of the major claw, minor claw or even both claws does not switch the handedness to the other side;
- the regenerates follow the already established asymmetric pattern.

Thus the state is not “the right claw is always large” or “the particular major claw never changes.” The state is an enduring relational rule for future paired-appendage differentiation.

### E. Palmer 2012: threshold-trait model and the role of lateral inhibition

A. Richard Palmer, “Developmental origins of normal and anomalous random right-left asymmetry: lateral inhibition versus developmental error in a threshold trait,” *Contributions to Zoology* 81(2), 111–124 (2012). Full-text PDF directly opened and inspected: https://repository.naturalis.nl/pub/419175/CtZ81-02_111-124_Palmer.pdf.

Palmer distinguishes normal random asymmetry (most individuals are asymmetric, but the side is random) from anomalous random asymmetry (a minority of individuals become asymmetric). His review describes fiddler-crab development as unusual relative to lobsters and snapping shrimp:
- Both claws begin transforming toward major signalling claws in very young crabs (approximately 4–5 mm carapace width in the cited work).
- One of the two large chelae is then autotomized at random; a small claw regenerates in its place.
- Once the side is established, it remains fixed through subsequent molts even after the major claw is lost and regenerated.
- Palmer proposes that **lateral inhibition is not the initiating cause** of asymmetry in fiddler crabs, unlike some other crustaceans; it plays an important role in **maintaining the chosen side** after the first loss event.

This differs from:
- *Homarus* lobster: one claw crosses an activity threshold and begins transformation; it inhibits the other from transforming, and the direction remains fixed. Fiddler crabs may initially have both sides moving toward the major state before one is lost.
- *Alpheus* snapping shrimp: after normal asymmetry is established, removal of the major snapper can induce the opposite pincer to transform into a new snapper, reversing the side assignment.
- *Hydroides* serpulid worms: the functional operculum can be replaced by its formerly rudimentary partner repeatedly during the animal's normal life.

The three paired-organ systems therefore have different transition rules; do not infer one universal mechanism from their shared surface pattern of asymmetrical paired structures.

### F. The “memory” of handedness: an unresolved organism-level regulatory state

The most interesting feature for our larger inquiry is that the established handedness persists even after both chelipeds are removed. The two appendages are no longer present to carry the full positional memory, yet regenerates follow the same right/left target. This suggests that the regulatory state is retained somewhere beyond the missing appendages.

Daniel Lobo, Mauricio Solano, George A. Bubenik & Michael Levin, “A linear-encoding model explains the variability of the target morphology in regeneration,” *Journal of the Royal Society Interface* 11(92) (2014), DOI 10.1098/rsif.2013.0918. Full PMC text directly read: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3899861/.

The authors use the fiddler crab as an example of a persistent, injury-established regenerative target. They suggest that the handedness encoding must lie somewhere in the organism rather than in the lost major claw or the absent appendages, because even after later simultaneous removal of both claws the same pattern is rebuilt. They propose a conceptual “linear encoding” of morphology to explain how cells might regenerate a selected target form. Their suggestion that this target-state encoding is distributed through the body is a **model-level hypothesis**, not a directly localized physiological mechanism. They explicitly note that no physiological mechanism controlling and maintaining fiddler-crab handedness is known; a dynamical mathematical model had been proposed (Seno & Shigemoto 2007).

Use “persistent organism-level pattern memory” as a descriptive question, not as an identified anatomical object or proof of a literal body-wide information field.

### G. Current post-autotomy morphology: handedness persistence does not mean perfect morphological restoration

Pablo D. Ribeiro, Nahuel E. Farías, Emiliano H. Ocampo, Jesús D. Nuñez & Tomás A. Luppi, “The influence of the post-autotomy regeneration of a sexual trait and mating tactics in a fiddler crab,” *Current Zoology* 71(1), 54–64, published online 26 April 2024 and in the February 2025 collection, DOI 10.1093/cz/zoae019. Full PMC article read: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11846806/.

This study is on *Leptuca uruguayensis*, and concerns the morphology/function of regenerated adult major claws, not the initial establishment of handedness:
- The authors analyze a population sample of 259 male claws and compare full-size and newly regenerated forms.
- They conduct a laboratory autotomy experiment on 120 animals and a field experiment, following the consequences of claw loss/regeneration.
- The supposed brachychelous (robust) and leptochelous (slender) classes in this species are not cleanly discrete; the population appears to form a morphological continuum.
- Newly regenerated claws are initially small and proportional to body size; after several molts, a field experiment confirms recovery of claw length.
- Nevertheless, size/energy constraints and differential growth mean that muscle-bearing manus robustness may not recover in the same way as total length. Regenerated claws can be less robust at equivalent body size and can trade weapon function against display/ornament function.
- Leptochelous males were at a disadvantage at high population density but not at low density, suggesting that the fitness consequences of a regenerated claw depend on social context.

Other fiddler-crab species differ: some have more discrete brachychelous/leptochelous morphotypes, with regenerated claws remaining slender, while in *L. uruguayensis* morphology is more continuous. Do not generalize a single regeneration outcome to all fiddler-crab species.

### H. Biological comparison: three distinct forms of paired-structure dynamics

| System | Initiation of asymmetry | Response after dominant-structure loss | Time-course |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fiddler crab (*Uca* group) | Juvenile claw loss selects which side retains the major trajectory | Same side regenerates major; handedness persists, even after both claws are later removed | Developmental side-selection followed by stable target-state |
| Snapping shrimp (*Alpheus*) | Juvenile developmental/use-dependent differentiation with neural regulation | Loss of major snapper can let contralateral pincer transform; side can reverse | State-dependent, molt-associated lateral reversal |
| Serpulid *Hydroides* | Relative growth/differentiation of paired opercular rudiments, with inhibition and peripheral pathways | Functional operculum can be replaced by the opposite side; spontaneous and experimentally induced reversals occur | Multiple reversals can occur during the individual lifespan |

The similarity is a relational dependence between paired structures. The differences are in the temporal window, which perturbation sets the state, whether handedness is maintained or transferred, the degree of reversibility and the physiological mechanisms involved.

### I. Implications for the historical 4D/histological inquiry

Morgan and Yamaguchi add a fourth type of biological organization to the comparison:
- **Structural asymmetry** is not necessarily inherited as a fixed left/right trait from the start.
- A contingent developmental event can establish a persistent laterality state.
- Once the state is established, it survives the removal of both appendages and is expressed again during regeneration.
- This persistence suggests an organism-level regulator or target-state memory beyond the appendages themselves, but its location and material basis remain unknown.
- Later claw morphology may still vary with body size, energetic budget, molt history and social environment even while left/right target direction remains stable.

This gives a grounded biological example of persistent organizational memory without requiring continuous persistence of the original physical appendage. However, it remains ordinary within-organism development and regeneration. It does not establish extra-spatial continuity, a 4D branch between different people or organisms, or a biological “worldtree.” It may instead help sharpen what the stronger 4D proposal must claim beyond ordinary persistent patterning: does it posit an actual additional physical connection, or is it a model of how an organism's form and identity remain related through changing tissues and time?

### J. Source status and open issues

- Morgan 1923 article: bibliographically verified, SciSpace abstract and structured extraction inspected; full journal article not opened. The 8/18 result and 524-male count are from extraction, and should be checked against original pages before exact quotation.
- Morgan 1924 article: bibliographically verified, abstract and structured extraction inspected; full article not opened. Exact sample sizes and full experimental table remain outstanding.
- Yamaguchi 1977 *Uca lactea*: journal page and abstract directly opened; full article body not read.
- Palmer 2012: full open PDF text directly opened and relevant sections inspected; PDF page-image rendering has not been verified in this session.
- Lobo et al. 2014: full PMC text directly read; this is a theoretical/model paper, not identification of the physical substrate.
- Ribeiro et al. 2024/2025 *Leptuca uruguayensis*: full PMC text directly read; post-autotomy adult claw morphology, not initial handedness establishment.
- Morgan's exact fiddler-crab species in 1923/1924 remains unspecified in the available abstract; a specialist bibliography suggests *Minuca pugnax*, but do not assign that species without direct article confirmation.

**Next:** retrieve Morgan 1923/1924 full article pages and confirm sample/molt details; then inspect Yamaguchi's full 1977 article for exact sizes, survival and molt counts. After that, return to Zeleny's 1905 102-page *Compensatory Regulation* or Morgan 1900 mixed-lineage regeneration sequel, depending on which source can be directly recovered. No Tock completion or cursor advance.

## 61. Morgan 1907, Experimental Zoology, Chapter XVIII: grafted bodies, polarity and part-whole relations — 2026-11-24

**Primary source inspected:** Thomas Hunt Morgan, Experimental Zoology (New York: Macmillan, 1907), Chapter XVIII, "Experiments in Grafting," printed pp. 285–301. Full Columbia University Library scan/OCR: https://www.columbia.edu/cu/lweb/digital/collections/cul/texts/ldpd_5949112_000/ldpd_5949112_000.pdf. The PDF text was opened and inspected; page-image screenshot returned a cache miss, so this is OCR-based, not image-verified. This is the book chapter, not Morgan's separate 1908 American Naturalist paper.

### A. The distinctions Morgan makes

Morgan divides animal-grafting questions into: which regions can unite; how united parts influence each other; whether grafting can produce hybridization; and what grafting reveals about embryonic development (pp. 285–286). These are separate outcomes. Anatomical union, physiological integration, developmental influence and inheritance are not interchangeable.

### B. Earthworms: permanent unions and duplicated poles

Joest's earthworm grafts use ligatures through the skin; contact surfaces unite within days and the threads are shed. Parts from different individuals can unite as readily as parts from the same worm. Morgan describes a short worm made by removing a middle region and joining the anterior and posterior ends; a long worm made by inserting a middle piece between anterior and posterior sections; and permanent unions of two anterior or two posterior cut surfaces. Two posterior ends joined by their anterior cut faces can make a two-tailed, headless composite that slowly starves (pp. 286–287).

If the median planes or nervous systems do not align, regeneration may occur at the junction. Morgan assigns the nervous system a major role as the centre from which heads or tails form. Misaligned nerve-cord ends may grow toward one another and unite through connective nerve tissue; if too far apart, regeneration is more likely to occur from one or both ends (p. 287).

### C. Tadpoles: ectopic grafts and self-differentiation

Born's experiments joined pieces of very young tadpoles, including tail tissue grafted to non-native body locations. Morgan reports that grafts could remain attached and continue developing their normal structures, without regeneration at the graft boundary even when unlike organs were not matched. He interprets this as self-differentiation: once a region's developmental trajectory has begun, it can continue in a new anatomical location (p. 287). One tail graft was later absorbed around metamorphosis, when the normal tail is also absorbed. This is a stage-specific outcome, not a general persistence rule.

### D. Planarians: neural continuity and mixed-origin heads

Mrs. Morgan's experiments with Phagocata gracilis and Planaria maculata joined cut surfaces from different body levels. If the graft was perfect, equal-sized pieces left no free edges and remained permanently joined without regeneration at the junction. If nerve cords united, regeneration at the graft line generally did not occur; if one or both cords remained free, one or two heads could appear. In inverted anterior-to-anterior unions, two heads sometimes formed at the junction, one above and one below. Each was connected to the ventral nerve cord of one component, while pigment distribution indicated that each new head included material from both worms (pp. 288–289).

This means tissue provenance and neural organization can tell different parts of the story: a new organ can contain material from both components yet be associated with one component's nerve cord.

### E. Hydra: fusion, separation, remodeling and polarity reversal

Morgan emphasizes that hydra and planarians can remodel old tissue into a typical whole with little new tissue, unlike animals that mainly replace lost parts by new growth (pp. 289–290).

Examples:
- An anterior half joined to a posterior half can produce one normal-proportioned hydra.
- Two individually short pieces form a short composite that elongates toward normal proportions.
- Two individually long pieces develop a new foot and tentacles near the junction, then pinch apart into two hydras.
- Anterior-to-anterior unions can fuse lengthwise into one body or develop two sets of tentacles and separate.
- Posterior-to-posterior unions form two new feet and subsequently separate.
- Lateral grafts can share a trunk before splitting, form a graft-derived head, or pinch off locally.
- A graft may be absorbed into the host if it cannot separate or establish a stable shared configuration.

In some very short grafts, joining oral/anterior ends can reverse the graft's polarity: a head may form at its posterior/aboral end. Morgan says the explanation is difficult and needs careful re-examination (pp. 294–296).

Morgan's pressure/tension account is explicitly a provisional working hypothesis. He suggests that relative size, orientation, polarity and contact geometry may influence whether pieces fuse, remodel, separate or reverse polarity (pp. 292–298).

### F. Different-species composites: shared circulation without demonstrated identity blending

Morgan reports that a composite earthworm made from Lumbricus rubellus and Allolobophora terrestris lived eight months. Blood circulated between components, but neither showed material influence from the other. In a second earthworm composite, a regenerated head retained the color characteristic of the species from which it formed, despite receiving nourishment from blood that came from both components.

Different frog species could also be joined, but each region generally retained its own specific characteristics. Morgan describes a particularly intimate reciprocal tail graft between Rana sylvatica and Rana palustris in which outer ectoderm and underlying tissues could have different species origins; subsequent regeneration showed no detectable influence of one component's cells on the other. Some interspecific hydra combinations failed after temporary adhesion; others persisted (pp. 298–301).

Morgan concludes that grafted cells retain species-specific characteristics even when they live side by side in a composite animal. He distinguishes this from fertilization, where hereditary material combines within a cell.

### G. Theoretical and methodological limit

Morgan rejects "formative force" when it merely renames the capacity to change form. His own physical tension/pressure explanation remains provisional: he concedes that the cellular response and especially the differentiation effects of mutual pressure are not understood (pp. 297–298). He also refuses to infer hybridization from grafting or shared circulation alone.

### H. Relevance to the External Dimensional Workshop

The experiments provide a useful taxonomy for claims that apparently separate living structures belong to a larger organism:

1. Contact and tissue adhesion.
2. Stable anatomical continuity.
3. Physiological integration, including shared circulation.
4. Neural continuity or alignment.
5. Developmental coordination, fusion, remodeling or separation.
6. Mixed tissue provenance in a new organ.
7. Transfer (or non-transfer) of organism-specific traits.

These levels must not be collapsed. Morgan demonstrates experimentally constructed composite organisms and inter-part developmental relations in ordinary three-dimensional animals. He does not demonstrate naturally occurring extra-spatial continuity between separate organisms, nor that genealogy itself is a physical anatomical branch. His work does, however, give us concrete tests for what organismal part-whole membership would require: more than contact, more than circulation, and more than shared developmental influence.

**Status:** Morgan 1907, Chapter XVIII, printed pp. 285–301 inspected in searchable OCR; page-image verification not achieved. No cursor advance.

## 62. Rabes 1901: histological and physiological integration in grafted earthworms — 2026-11-24

**Primary source inspected:** Otto Rabes, "Die histologischen und physiologischen Ergebnisse der Verwachsungsversuche an Lumbriciden," published in *Sitzungsberichte der Gesellschaft zur Beförderung der gesammten Naturwissenschaften zu Marburg*, 1901 volume (printed 1902), pp. 105–115. The original report was opened and read in searchable PDF text; screenshot/page-image verification is still outstanding. Full scan: https://www.zobodat.at/pdf/Sber-Ges-Natwiss-Marburg_1901_0001-0206.pdf. This is Rabes's own short meeting-report account of his histological work, not the full 1901 monograph, which is separately catalogued as *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik* 13, 239–352, plates 3–11.

### A. Why Rabes undertook the follow-up

Rabes says Joest's earlier graft results were based mainly on macroscopic inspection and careful observation of the living components. Rabes examined serial histological sections to determine how union occurred and what happened to individual tissues and organs at the joining site. He highlights the advance of conducting these operations in mature, sexually developed earthworms rather than only in embryos or simple coelenterates.

The 1901 report distinguishes same-individual grafts, grafts between individuals, and grafts between species in the broader literature. Heteroplastic composites are visually notable because their component parts retain their different coloration after permanent union.

### B. External wall and musculature

After the pieces are held together with sutures, lymph cells enter the narrow gap, line the two wound surfaces and close the external wound. In favourable cases, Rabes says, closure may occur within about an hour. Epidermal/hypodermal cells from both sides advance over the scar tissue and form a continuous surface seam.

The circular musculature remains contracted longer. Cells from the hypodermis, including mitotically produced cells, enter the scar and form a new zone between the two old circular-muscle ends. New muscle fibrils are visible at roughly day seven and connect the older stumps. Rabes describes this circular muscle as predominantly ectodermal in origin. Longitudinal muscle is formed almost entirely by cells migrating from the old longitudinal musculature. These are his histological interpretations, based on the preparations available in 1901.

### C. Gut continuity

Rabes reports union of the intestinal ends in all experimental series. Epithelial surfaces first adhere; lymph cells around the wound hold the two ends in position. The junction becomes passable to food particles relatively early, and mitoses in intestinal epithelial cells after about twelve days complete the union. He describes the connection as extension/growth of the intestinal ends into one another.

This is direct evidence for digestive continuity across a graft boundary, not just an externally attached composite.

### D. Vascular continuity, including rotated grafts

Rabes reports blood-vessel union in all experimental series:
- In normally oriented grafts, vessel ends unite directly.
- With a 90-degree longitudinal rotation, free vessel ends bend toward each other and form a bayonet-shaped connector.
- With a 180-degree rotation, dorsal vessels may connect directly with ventral vessels despite their different diameters. Because the flow directions would otherwise conflict, segmental collateral vessels linking dorsal and ventral vessels restore a normal circulation path.
- In implanted side pieces, the side-piece dorsal vessel can connect by multiple anastomoses with the host's ventral vessel.
- Blood communication returns early: waves of blood enter lacunae in the scar tissue and then find a path through the lymph-cell network from one vessel end to the other; vessel walls of the connector develop from the scar tissue.

Thus the claim in Morgan 1907 that blood circulated through different earthworm components has a histological basis in Rabes's work. Crucially, it does not imply that component-specific traits or identities were transferred.

### E. Nervous-system continuity is a further, separable achievement

Rabes calls the nervous-cord junction the most interesting result. One day after operation, the ventral nerve-cord stumps end as frayed bundles in the wound. Old nerve fibers then grow actively from both components through the scar, meet and unite, re-establishing a neural connection. Mitoses appear in ganglia near the junction; new ganglion cells form clusters that populate the connective segment between the old nerve-cord stumps. Rabes says the old ventral nerve cord is primarily responsible for establishing the neural continuity.

This union depends on orientation:
- With rotation up to 90 degrees, even when nerve-cord ends are not directly opposite, the free ends bend toward each other and their axons unite in a bayonet-shaped connection.
- With 180-degree rotation, a neural union does not occur because the cord ends are separated by half the body circumference.
- Rabes interprets the directed growth as a form of chemotropism, which Forsmann had called "neurotropism": degenerating nerve material at the cut ends is proposed to attract/regulate the direction of outgrowing axons. He estimates the effective range in these earthworm operations at about a quarter of the body circumference, or four to five times the diameter of the nerve cord.

Rabes describes a visible twitch response after stimulation of the anterior end, even before the giant Leydig fibers have joined. He also describes a reversed direction of conduction in two-tail unions as evidence of a very intimate neural connection between the components. These are historical observations and interpretations; the proposed neurotropic mechanism is not to be treated as settled modern neurobiology.

### F. The decisive distinction: vegetative unity versus full physiological unity

Rabes explicitly separates two levels:
- First, union of gut and blood vessels gives the components shared food and blood exchange. He calls this a "vegetative unity" and describes it as the basic condition for continued existence.
- Then the free nerve ends unite, restoring what he calls the "complete physiological unity of the individual."

A particularly revealing case occurs when a posterior piece is implanted into a normal worm. Only one pair of ventral-cord stumps may unite, creating a normally functioning individual from two sections that have secondarily joined. The third component can remain connected through epidermis and musculature, and be nutritionally/vascularly integrated, but remain nervously isolated: Rabes describes it as an appendage of the new main body. Which cord ends join seems to depend on their spatial proximity.

This shows why "one organism" cannot be reduced to a single binary test. In Rabes's grafts, outer tissue continuity, alimentary continuity, vascular continuity and neural continuity can be partially independent. A component can belong to the composite body in some anatomical and physiological respects while remaining a nervous-system-isolated appendage.

### G. Other findings and longevity

Rabes reports that the exact correspondence of the joined body regions is not essential: sections from different intestinal, vascular and nervous-system levels can unite, as can hypodermis and musculature. Regenerative buds can form at some graft junctions, and the nervous system appears to be an important determinant.

Tiny skin-muscle fragments too small to live alone can survive when joined to larger viable pieces. Rabes reports that they can form large regenerates using material supplied by the larger component. In one case a regenerate normally expected to form a head instead formed a typical posterior end, a heteromorphic regeneration.

At the time of his report, autoplastically and homoplastically joined earthworms had survived for more than five years; the oldest living composite was about five years and eight months old. These are historical longevity observations, not proof of indefinite survival. Later reports by Korschelt/Rabes give still longer survival for some specific grafts; keep those later durations tied to their own records.

### H. Relation to Morgan 1907 and the workshop's organismal-boundary problem

Rabes supplies the missing tissue-level underpinning for Morgan's description of composites:
1. **External continuity:** a continuous epithelial surface and reconstructed muscle wall.
2. **Digestive continuity:** connected intestine and shared passage of food.
3. **Vascular continuity:** anastomosed blood vessels and circulation across component boundaries.
4. **Neural continuity:** axons and ventral nerve cords can regrow and unite, but may fail depending on geometry.
5. **Functional integration:** the first three can sustain a vegetative composite; neural union may restore coordinated physiological function.
6. **Partial membership:** one graft component can remain vascularly/structurally connected yet nervously isolated, acting as an appendage.

This is unusually relevant to the larger histological proposal because it experimentally demonstrates that anatomical and physiological integration has layers, and that a body can contain components with unequal degrees of integration. But the operations are deliberate surgical grafts in one conventional earthworm composite. They do not show natural extra-spatial continuity among organisms or genealogical branching as a literal anatomical structure. They do give us concrete candidate criteria to test rather than relying on analogy.

**Status:** Rabes's 1901 Marburg report, printed pp. 105–115, inspected in searchable OCR; original full article in *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik* 13 (1901), 239–352, still not directly inspected. Page images not verified. No cursor advance.


## 63. Rabes full monograph retrieval audit — expanded 1901 article identified, scan access not yet achieved — 2026-11-24

A bounded retrieval check was made for Otto Rabes's expanded paper, *Transplantationsversuche an Lumbriciden: Histologie und Physiologie der Transplantationen*, *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik der Organismen* 13 (1901), 239–352.

### Verified bibliographic versions

The Index Catalogue of Medical and Veterinary Zoology lists four versions/records:
- 1901 dissertation, 27 pages, Marburg.
- Preliminary article in *Biologisches Centralblatt* 21(20), 15 October 1901, pp. 633–650.
- Expanded article in *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik der Organismen* 13 (1–2), 12 November 1901, pp. 239–352, seven text figures and plates 3–11 (figures 1–77).
- Rabes's presentation summary in *Sitzungsberichte der Gesellschaft zur Beförderung der gesammten Naturwissenschaften zu Marburg* (8), 27 July 1901, pp. 105–115, read 10 July.

The 1901 *Zoologischer Jahresbericht* independently lists the expanded *Archiv* article at volume 13, pp. 239–352, seven figures and plates 3–11, as well as the preliminary *Biologisches Centralblatt* paper. A 1903 curriculum vitae/professional record likewise lists the dissertation, interim article and *Archiv* paper as distinct publications. This confirms that the short 11-page Marburg report read in section 62 is not identical to the full article.

### Access results

- Google Play/Books record identifies *Archiv für Entwicklungsmechanik der Organismen*, volume 13, 1901–02, as a 764-page full-view volume. The host's page was indexed but returned a cache miss when opened; page-level text and images for Rabes were not exposed in this session.
- The Internet Archive/BHL routes tried earlier did not yield the article body. The accessible *Biologisches Centralblatt* volume scan has a contents/index entry for Rabes at p. 633, but the exposed scan-text does not include the article body at those pages; do not treat the index entry as access to the paper.
- The screenshot service was also tried for the Marburg short report (printed pp. 105–115; estimated PDF page index 119) and returned a cache miss. Thus section 62 is accurately marked as OCR/text inspected and not page-image verified.

### Current source-use rule

For now, cite the **Marburg short report** as the directly inspected primary source for tissue, gut, blood-vessel and nerve-cord union, but keep the 352-page *Archiv* article as a higher-resolution primary-source target, not as a text already read. The *Biologisches Centralblatt* 633–650 paper is also separately identified and remains unread. Do not conflate the four versions or assume the expanded article is merely a verbatim reprint of the short report.

## 65. Siphonophore colony histology: natural modular body with continuous gastrovascular space and distributed neural organization — 2026-11-24

**Primary modern research article directly inspected:** Samuel H. Church, Stefan Siebert, Pathikrit Bhattacharyya & Casey W. Dunn, “The histology of *Nanomia bijuga* (Hydrozoa: Siphonophora),” *Journal of Experimental Zoology Part B: Molecular and Developmental Evolution* 324B, 435–449 (2015), DOI 10.1002/jez.b.22629. Open-access full text: https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/jez.b.22629. Full article text inspected through lines/sections on colony formation, histology, stem structure, and neural-cell distribution. Page-image view not needed for this HTML article; associated histological figure captions were inspected textually.

### A. Colony origin and growth architecture

*Nanomia bijuga* begins as a single sexually produced polyp, the protozooid. It develops two localized growth zones along its body column; the protozooid elongates into a stem, and new zooids are added by budding. The mature colony is organized into two main regions:
- **Nectosome:** bears the pneumatophore and nectophores, the propulsion/swimming zooids.
- **Siphosome:** carries feeding, digestive, reproductive and protective zooids arranged as repeated cormidia.

Each zooid is a modified polyp or medusa and is structurally and functionally specialized. Zooid production takes place at the two growth zones and yields regular colony-level organization. The paper emphasizes that zooids are asexually produced and genetically identical, while morphologically distinct and functionally specialized.

This is not an accidental aggregate of independently assembled animals. It is an organized clonal body whose repeated modules arise from one founding individual through controlled developmental budding.

### B. Direct anatomical continuity

The authors state that all zooids attach to the colony stem and that the gastric cavity of the stem is continuous with the gastric cavities of each zooid. This is the physical basis for colony-wide internal continuity: the zooids are attached modules with a shared gastrovascular space, not simply adjacent bodies.

The paper's microanatomy confirms extensive structural differentiation:
- The pneumatophore develops by invagination at the aboral/anterior end of the protozooid and comprises five distinct tissue populations in the mature structure, including external and invaginated epithelial layers, endodermal layers, mesoglea, a chitin-lined gas chamber, and internal aeriform cells.
- Gastozooids are feeding polyps with a specialized hypostome and basigaster, secretory/glandular endoderm, nematocyst-producing ectoderm and absorptive cells.
- Palpons attach to the stem through a peduncle. Their endoderm has specialized funnel cells with microvilli and absorptive cells, supporting particle capture and intracellular digestion.
- Male and female gonodendra are compound reproductive structures attached close to palpon bases; their stalks connect to the siphosomal stem.
- The stem itself has contractile ectodermal cells with myofibrils along radial extensions of mesoglea, an endodermal/gastrodermal lining around the gastric cavity, and a large axon at its dorsal ridge.

### C. Distributed neural organization

The study combines histology with in situ hybridization for a neuropeptide marker, *rfamide*. It finds:
- nerve-cell rings at the bases of zooid peduncles, including gastrozooids and palpons;
- neuronal cell populations around the mouth region of gastrozooids;
- scattered neuronal cells in the siphosomal stem;
- a giant axon running along the stem, dorsal in the siphosome and ventral in the nectosome;
- transverse neural collars/bands posterior to each gastrozooid, possibly associated with circular musculature and stem constriction.

The giant axon's change in dorsal/ventral position between nectosome and siphosome is interpreted as evidence of a 180-degree torsion between the regions during development, consistent with a previous developmental hypothesis. This is a specific anatomical orientation observation, not an extra spatial dimension.

### D. Functional interdependence and modular specialization

The colony has a strong division of labour: zooids specialize in locomotion, feeding, digestion, protection and sexual reproduction. These specialized modules remain integrated through the stem and continuous gastric cavity, while the stem's contractile and neural structures provide a distributed bodily axis.

The analogy to organs is useful but incomplete. The article notes that zooids are evolutionarily homologous to free-living solitary cnidarians, remain multicellular, possess zooid-specific cell types and show regional subfunctionalization. So the zooids are not merely cells or tissues; they are specialized repeated biological modules at a scale that corresponds evolutionarily to solitary animal forms. Developmentally, however, the colony is generated from a single sexually produced founder and its asexual buds.

### E. What this contributes to our organismal-boundary taxonomy

*Nanomia* gives a natural example with several properties that were surgically reconstructed in Morgan/Joest/Rabes worm grafts:
1. physically attached modules;
2. one continuous gastrovascular cavity;
3. distributed neural structures with a giant conducting axon along the stem;
4. contractile tissue in the shared stem;
5. repeated modules with specialized roles;
6. growth zones that add new modules while preserving a stereotyped colony architecture.

Unlike the earthworm grafts, this organization develops naturally and predictably rather than being surgically assembled. Unlike ordinary multicellular human anatomy, its modules are zooids homologous to solitary cnidarian animals. But it is still a clonal colony arising from one embryo; it does not demonstrate an anatomical union between separate adult organisms or a physical connection between members of a genealogy through time.

For the user's 4D histological proposal, this is a valuable comparator for a body organized as a serially developing, modular, extended structure with physically continuous internal pathways. Its growth zone and repeated zooids make the "body as an ongoing generative architecture" idea much more concrete. The unproven step in the user's proposal remains the extension from one organismal lineage/colony to apparently separate individuals or intertemporal branches as literal parts of one larger physical body.

### F. Caution and next source questions

This article focuses on mature-colony histology and RFamide-positive neural populations; it does not experimentally test colony-level neural signal propagation, whole-colony circulation dynamics, or how the colony behaves after loss of selected zooids. The shared gastrovascular cavity and giant axon are anatomical observations; do not infer a complete nervous or circulatory mechanism beyond what the study demonstrates.

Next, compare this clonal-colony condition with a colonial tunicate such as *Botryllus*, whose zooids are physically linked by a common vascular network and may undergo synchronized budding/regression, and with colonial hydroids. The key question is whether those systems arise from one embryo and asexual budding, or whether independent individuals can fuse while retaining partial individuality. Maintain distinctions between clonal development, fusion of genetically different individuals, shared circulation, and shared neural coordination.

**Status:** Church et al. 2015 open-access article directly inspected through relevant sections and figure legends. This is a current histological source; no page-image audit needed because the source was an HTML article with embedded figure assets. No cursor advance.


## 66. Botryllus schlosseri: natural fusion of independently founded colonies, vascular chimerism and germ-line competition — 2026-11-24

**Primary experimental paper directly inspected:** Douglas S. Stoner & Irving L. Weissman, “Somatic and germ cell parasitism in a colonial ascidian: Possible role for a highly polymorphic allorecognition system,” *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences* 93(26), 15254–15259 (24 December 1996), DOI 10.1073/pnas.93.26.15254. Full text/OCR inspected through ResearchGate's author-uploaded article copy: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/14220932_Somatic_and_germ_cell_parasitism_in_a_colonial_ascidian_Possible_role_for_a_highly_polymorphic_allorecognition_system. Independent PubMed record: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8986797/. A 2014 open review by Taketa & De Tomaso provides broader allorecognition and life-history context: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4185259/.

### A. The biological unit before fusion

Each *Botryllus schlosseri* colony begins with a sexually derived tadpole larva that settles and metamorphoses into a founder (oozooid). The founder then buds asexually to produce a colony of genetically identical zooids. Each zooid is an anatomically complex individual with a heart, gut, gonads and nervous system. The colony's zooids are linked by an **extracorporeal vascular network** enclosed in a common tunic; the vascular system ends in peripheral ampullae.

The key point is that the units that fuse at the edge are not merely adjacent zooids within one clonal colony. They can be two independently founded colonies, each with its own clonal history and set of zooids, that grow toward one another across a substrate.

### B. Compatibility gates whether two colonies fuse

When the peripheral ampullae of two colonies contact, one of two outcomes occurs:
- If their genotypes share at least one allele at the highly polymorphic Fu/HC (fusion/histocompatibility) locus, the ampullae can anastomose and establish a shared vascular connection; the result is a vascular chimera.
- If they share no allele, an inflammatory rejection destroys the interacting ampullae and blocks further vascular integration.

The 2014 review describes the reaction as typically occurring within 24–48 hours. The 1996 article says the laboratory colonies generally fused within 2–3 days after placement in contact. The Fu/HC system is a specific tunicate allorecognition system, not an MHC homolog simply transferred from vertebrates; the 2014 review notes that no apparent orthologous conservation of allorecognition mechanisms across taxa has been established.

### C. Laboratory experiment: two separately founded colonies become a vascular chimera

Stoner and Weissman generated nine laboratory chimeras using compatible colony pairs. They divided each colony into subclones, placed compatible partners next to one another on slides, and sampled at one, four and eight weeks; some pairs were surgically separated one week after fusion and sampled later.

Key observations:
- All nine fused pairs showed both partner genotypes in sampled blood and/or tissues within eight weeks.
- By week one, the blood was already chimeric in distinguishable pairs, even before all stable chimeras showed detectable donor DNA in buds.
- In the five pairs that remained stably fused, mixed-genotype bud tissue appeared by week four and persisted through week eight.
- In stable pairs surgically separated one week after fusion, five of five showed donor-derived chimerism in buds at the later sampling point; none of these five had detectable bud chimerism at week one. This is evidence that donor-derived cells can move through the shared circulation and establish themselves in the partner after an initial period of vascular contact, with some lineage consequences persisting after surgical separation.
- Four of nine laboratory pairs underwent a later resorption process, in which all zooids of one component colony died and were absorbed by the surviving partner. Chimerism patterns varied substantially with whether resorption occurred.

### D. Somatic chimerism: shared tissue contribution is real but not unlimited

Genetic typing showed that buds could contain cells from both founding colonies. In some systems, both genotypes were found side by side (sectorial chimerism); in others, mixed genotypes were distributed throughout the sampled chimera. The authors did not find complete replacement of every sampled bud by the partner; they interpret the data as somatic cell parasitism/chimerism that is real but incomplete in the tested cases.

The paper notes that asexually derived buds are not wholly clonal in their cellular origin: bud formation involves several distinct cell types, and the different cell lines can contribute to a new bud. Therefore, the boundary between the two founding genotypes can be present within the same regenerated/reproductive structures.

### E. Germ-line transfer and competition

The most striking result is that circulating germ-line progenitors can cross the vascular connection and colonize gonadal tissues in the partner colony:
- All five stable laboratory chimeras had testes containing a genotype from the partner.
- In some examples, the donor genotype was detected at levels implying that at least 96–98% of the tested tissue DNA came from that donor genotype; the assay's detection threshold was about 2–4% of the alternative genotype.
- Apparent replacement of a partner's gametic tissue could occur within four weeks and persist for at least eight weeks after fusion.
- The authors considered both clonogenic seeding of a testis and active cell-line competition as possible explanations; their study does not fully distinguish these mechanisms.
- Crosses of field-derived chimeras then confirmed that the transferred lineage was reproductively functional. The partner genotype's share of sperm in the testes predicted its contribution to offspring. Germ-cell parasitism was also found in eggs, not only sperm.

In 10 naturally collected field chimeras, both partner genotypes were present in blood in all 10; somatic chimerism was detected in 8 of 10; germ-line transfer was detected in 8 of 10; and clear germ-cell parasitism was detected in 4 of 10. In two of those four field examples, nearly the entire sperm output had been usurped by one chimeric partner.

The authors call this germ-cell or somatic-cell parasitism because a donor cell lineage can obtain a reproductive advantage using the somatic support of the recipient colony. A colony's visible somatic appearance may therefore fail to reveal which lineage contributes most of its gametes.

### F. The effect of rejection: boundaries are maintained in most incompatible contacts

To compare fused chimeras with adjacent unfused colonies, the authors sampled 15 pairs of colonies in physical contact but not fused. No somatic or germ-line admixture was detected in 13 of 15; one pair showed evidence of both GCP and SCP, and one showed low-level SCP. The authors estimated a lower rate of parasitism in unfused pairs (6.7%) than in fused chimeras (40%). They caution that the field pairs were retrospectively classified as putatively rejecting: because rejection signs can disappear, they could not prove prospectively that every unfused pair had actually undergone rejection rather than never reacting.

### G. The key distinction for the organismal-boundary model

This system contains several different biological identities at once:
1. **Founding-colony identity:** two colonies with independent sexually founded histories can contact.
2. **Vascular identity:** if compatible, they form a connected vascular chimera and share circulating blood cells.
3. **Somatic lineage identity:** cells from each founder can contribute to the composite's buds and tissues.
4. **Germ-line identity:** donor germ-line progenitors can enter and occupy reproductive niches in the partner.
5. **Visible colony identity:** the exterior can appear as a single fused or chimeric structure even while its genetic lineages remain multiple and compete.
6. **Resorption:** in some combinations one colony's zooids die and are absorbed, while donor cells or lineages can persist in the remaining composite.

This is a much closer natural comparator to the workshop's question than a clonal siphonophore: **separately founded organisms can establish a shared vascular system and become a physiological chimera, with living cell lineages crossing the original boundary.** The relation is not merely social or ecological; it is anatomical, circulatory, somatic and reproductive.

But the limits are equally important:
- Fusion is conditional on a specific histocompatibility match, not universal.
- The fusion joins two colony-level clonal bodies through peripheral vascular anastomoses; it does not establish that all component zooids or tissues become one undifferentiated organism.
- Multiple genotypes and competing cell lines persist, and one partner can parasitize another's germ line.
- This is ordinary three-dimensional tissue and vascular biology. It does not show genealogical connections extending physically through time, an extra spatial dimension, or human individuals as distal components of one larger body.

### H. Implications for the next biological comparisons

*Botryllus* suggests a more precise set of experimental questions for any proposed larger organism:
- Is there a physical anastomosis across the boundary?
- Does a shared internal transport system form?
- Which cell lineages cross, and by what route?
- Do cells enter somatic tissues, germ lines, or both?
- Does the original boundary persist, remodel or disappear?
- Can the composite be surgically separated without losing transferred lineages?
- Is the relationship stable, transient, competitive, or destructive?

The user's hypothesis requires evidence at least comparable to this level before ordinary genealogical or developmental relationships can be called literal anatomical continuity.

**Status:** Stoner & Weissman 1996 primary paper directly inspected in full searchable text through the ResearchGate author-uploaded copy, including methods, results, tables and conclusions. Taketa & De Tomaso 2014 review inspected for allorecognition and life-history context. No page-image check was needed for the HTML/OCR text interface used. No cursor advance.


## 67. Grandmaternal microchimerism in human cord blood — 2021 three-generation HLA study — 2026-11-24

**Primary study inspected:** Karlmark et al., “Grandmaternal cells in cord blood,” *eBioMedicine* 74 (2021), 103721, DOI 10.1016/j.ebiom.2021.103721, PMID 34844192, PMCID PMC8720789. Open full text: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2352396421005156; PubMed record: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34844192/. Full article text, methods, results, tables and discussion were inspected through the indexed full-text rendition. Direct PDF page-image verification was not performed.

### A. Design and detection

The authors collected umbilical cord blood from healthy primigravid women and obtained three-generation HLA typing for the maternal grandmother, mother and infant. Of 29 available three-generation families, 28 samples met the criteria for informative HLA-based detection of grandmaternal-specific material. They selected non-inherited HLA alleles present in the grandmother but not shared with the mother or child. HLA-A, -B and -DRB1 typing made it possible to distinguish grandmother-origin genetic material from the ordinary maternal-origin microchimerism signal.

They used HLA-specific quantitative PCR on cord-blood whole-blood DNA and sorted cell fractions, including peripheral-blood mononuclear cells, T cells, B cells, granulocytes and hematopoietic progenitor/stem-cell fractions. Sorted fractions were reported as greater than 95% pure. Results were expressed as genome equivalents of microchimeric donor cells per million host-cell genome equivalents. The qPCR detection method was highly sensitive; ten replicate wells increased combined theoretical sensitivity to 0.0005% under the stated DNA input conditions.

### B. Main result

Grandmaternal-specific signal was found in **5 of 28 informative cord-blood samples (18%)**. By comparison, ordinary maternal-origin microchimerism was found in 57% of the same samples. In whole blood and PBMCs, the grandmaternal signal quantities were about 100 times lower than the maternal signal.

One sample had a relatively high grandmaternal signal in the CD34+ hematopoietic progenitor-cell fraction despite no detectable signal in whole blood or PBMCs. However, the DNA yield from this rare cell fraction was low (about 5,500 genome equivalents tested), so the authors caution that its normalized concentration could be overestimated. The study did not show that grandmaternal material is evenly distributed across cell classes.

### C. Source discrimination and limitations

The study excluded one initially positive sample because the grandmother's and mother's non-inherited HLA alleles were indistinguishable, so the source could not be assigned. Three of the five retained positive samples had paternal HLA typing available; in those three, the relevant grandmaternal allele did not match the child's non-inherited paternal allele, which the authors used to rule out a vanished dizygotic twin as the source. Fathers were unavailable for the other two positive cases, so that particular alternative could not be excluded in the same way for all five.

The authors discuss the possibility of prenatal procedures or unusual pregnancy factors influencing transfer. In a small sample, two of four positive cases with available risk data had elevated aneuploidy-screening risk and invasive trophoblastic procedures, compared with none of 19 negative cases (reported Fisher exact p=0.024). This is exploratory, small-n evidence, not proof that such procedures cause transfer. They also report a lower HLA-compatibility pattern in families with positive samples, but their own discussion cautions that the small number of positive samples limits inference.

Most importantly, the assay measures **grandmother-specific DNA** in whole blood and purified blood-cell fractions. It is consistent with grandmother-origin microchimeric cells in the fetal circulation and is the authors' interpretation, but it is not direct microscopy of intact living donor cells, a single-cell genotype demonstration, longitudinal tracking of cell persistence after birth, or proof of functional engraftment in a tissue. The paper itself says it remains unknown whether the detected grandmaternal material is passive or immunologically active.

### D. What it establishes for the workshop

This is a genuine third-generation genetic signal in a human biological compartment: maternal-grandmother-specific HLA material is detectable in a subset of newborn cord-blood samples, beyond the mother's own non-inherited alleles. It provides evidence consistent with transfer of a lineage across two successive maternal pregnancy interfaces.

It does **not** establish a continuous macroscopic anatomical structure connecting grandmother, mother and child; does not show a shared vascular system across the three bodies; does not demonstrate tissue-level integration or a continuing functional role in the child; and does not support an additional spatial dimension. Its significance is narrower and still striking: genetic material associated with the grandmother can appear in the grandchild's prenatal circulation.

### E. Related evidence and next discriminating question

The 2011 study “Pregnancy, Microchimerism, and the Maternal Grandmother” examined maternal-grandmother-origin microchimerism in adult pregnant women, a possible intermediate host reservoir. The 2021 cord-blood study then detects grandmaternal-specific signal in the next generation. The two findings together make a plausible pathway, but they do not prove a continuous chain of viable cells from grandmother through mother to fetus in the same family over time.

Next, inspect the 2011 primary paper and then studies of maternal-origin microchimerism in adult offspring and fetal-origin microchimerism in mothers, distinguishing qPCR detection from direct cell localization, engraftment and function. Do not infer macroscopic continuity from cellular chimerism.

**Status:** Karlmark et al. 2021 full text, methods, results and discussion inspected through the indexed full text. Page-image verification not performed. This is one small study (28 informative samples, five positive); it requires independent replication and stronger cell-level confirmation. No cursor advance.


## 68. Gammill et al. 2011: maternal-grandmother-origin microchimerism in pregnant women — 2026-11-24

**Primary study inspected:** Hilary S. Gammill et al., “Pregnancy, Microchimerism, and the Maternal Grandmother,” *PLOS ONE* 6(8) (30 August 2011), e24101, DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0024101. Open full text: https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0024101. Full article, methods, tables and results inspected through the PLOS full-text HTML. Page-image check was not needed for HTML text.

### A. What the study measured

The study asks whether cells a woman acquired in utero from her own mother remain detectable in her peripheral blood during the woman's later pregnancy. The authors call the pregnant woman the proband and her own mother the mother-of-the-proband (MP). Family members were HLA genotyped at DRB1, DQA1 and DQB1 to identify an allele unique to the grandmother, then HLA-specific quantitative PCR was used on DNA extracted from the proband's peripheral-blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs).

The longitudinal healthy-pregnancy group included 27 women with uncomplicated singleton pregnancies, selected from a larger recruitment cohort. A separate comparison included 20 women with preeclampsia and 20 matched healthy controls (matched by parity and gestational age).

### B. Results

Grandmother-origin microchimerism was detected in the PBMCs of women with normal uncomplicated pregnancies, with detection increasing across gestation and peaking in the third trimester. For each advancing trimester, the modeled probability of detection increased 12.7-fold (95% CI 3.2–50.3; p<0.001). Mean concentrations also rose from no detectable signal pre-pregnancy/first trimester to higher levels in the second and third trimesters, then fell postpartum.

In the preeclampsia comparison, none of the 20 women with preeclampsia had detectable MP microchimerism, compared with 30% of the 20 matched healthy controls (p=0.03). The authors interpret this as a possible marker of maternal adaptation to pregnancy, not proof that these cells cause a healthy outcome.

### C. Limits

The assay detects grandmother-specific HLA DNA in extracted PBMC material. It is evidence consistent with maternal-grandmother-origin microchimeric cells circulating in the pregnant woman, but the study does not image individual donor cells or prove their viability, tissue integration, or function. It is not a direct experiment on the transfer of these cells from the pregnant woman into her fetus.

The longitudinal healthy cohort is small (27); the preeclampsia comparison is also small (20+20). The 12.7-fold trimester estimate has a wide confidence interval. The association with preeclampsia is observational and should not be described as causal. PLOS reports that the women with normal pregnancies were followed longitudinally; the preeclampsia group was sampled at diagnosis and compared with matched healthy subjects.

### D. How it joins the 2021 third-generation cord-blood study

The two papers measured different links:
- **Gammill et al. 2011:** a woman carries detectable HLA DNA consistent with cells derived from her own mother, and this signal becomes more detectable during her pregnancy.
- **Karlmark et al. 2021:** grandmother-specific HLA DNA is detected in a subset of cord-blood samples from the woman's child (5/28 informative samples).

Together they support a plausible grandmother → mother → fetus route for microchimeric material. They are not one longitudinally followed family chain and therefore do not directly track the same grandmother-derived cells through the mother's circulation and into the fetus. They also do not establish that the cells persist in the child's tissues after birth or contribute to organ function.

For the workshop, this is a real multigenerational cell-lineage phenomenon at the microscopic/cellular scale. It is a much narrower proposition than a macroscopic anatomical body spanning grandmother, mother and child. The evidence concerns rare cell populations and donor-specific genetic material, not a shared circulatory system among the three people.

**Status:** Gammill et al. 2011 full-text article inspected, including methods and results; page-image verification not needed for HTML. Combined with Karlmark et al. 2021, this gives a plausible but not directly tracked transgenerational microchimerism route. No cursor advance.


## 69. Human fetal microchimerism in healed maternal caesarean scars — cell localization and phenotype — 2026-11-24

**Primary study inspected:** Uzma Mahmood & Keelin O'Donoghue, “Microchimeric fetal cells play a role in maternal wound healing after pregnancy,” *Chimerism* 5(2) (2014), 40–52, DOI 10.4161/chim.28746, PMID 24717775, PMCID PMC4199806. Open full text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4199806/. Article methods, results, figure legends and discussion were inspected in the indexed full-text rendition.

### A. Design

The study examined 70 skin biopsies: 31 samples of normal/unwounded skin and 39 caesarean-section scars. Women with prior blood transfusions, stem-cell transplantation or organ transplantation were excluded. The research team used XY-FISH (X/Y chromosome fluorescence in situ hybridization) to locate male cells in tissue sections, additional Y-FISH and nested SRY PCR to confirm male genetic material, and immunostaining on the same sections to identify cell phenotype and extracellular-matrix markers.

The biological logic is that a male fetus can transfer cells into the mother. However, the authors correctly call the cells "male presumed-fetal" in their discussion: a Y chromosome establishes male genetic origin, not in every case the identity of a specific pregnancy.

### B. Findings in the scar

Male cells were identified in healed caesarean scars, particularly in women whose first male child had been delivered by caesarean section. The study reported 90 male cells in 115 tissue sections, with rare isolated cells (the abstract reports 0–9 cells per section). The male cells were located predominantly in the epidermis; some were also seen in the dermis. The comparison skin samples from women whose first male child had been delivered vaginally did not show the same scar-associated pattern.

Combined XY-FISH and immunostaining found that the epidermal male cells expressed pan-cytokeratin, identifying them phenotypically as keratinocytes. The study also found male cells expressing collagen I, collagen III, fibronectin and transforming growth factor beta markers (including TGF-β3). Figure legends show same-section co-localization of chromosome signal and tissue marker staining.

Y-FISH using an alternative probe and nested PCR for SRY were used as additional confirmation of male genetic material in the scar samples. The study therefore goes beyond bulk qPCR detection: it locates individual male-origin nuclei in maternal tissue and assesses their phenotype.

### C. What is and is not established

The data support the presence of rare male-origin cells in healed maternal caesarean scars and provide histological evidence that some have an epidermal keratinocyte phenotype. This is consistent with persistent fetal-origin cells becoming incorporated into maternal tissue and possibly participating in wound repair.

The study does **not** demonstrate that these cells caused improved scar healing, that they materially changed wound mechanics, or that they were necessary for repair. The authors infer possible recruitment/proliferation in response to injury, but the design is observational and does not manipulate or selectively remove the cells. Expression of collagen/TGF-β markers is suggestive of a repair-associated phenotype, not functional proof of a causal role.

Y-chromosome identification is not equivalent to unique pregnancy-source tracing. Women with prior miscarriages could carry male-origin cells from a pregnancy loss; the authors found male cells in some scar samples from women without sons but with previous miscarriages of unknown fetal sex. Although transplantation and transfusion were excluded, the study does not genetically distinguish a particular son from every other possible male pregnancy source in each case.

### D. Relevance to the multi-generational organismal question

This gives a human example of a cell population crossing the fetal–maternal boundary and later being found embedded in a differentiated maternal tissue. Unlike the 2011 and 2021 HLA-qPCR studies, this study uses microscopy and phenotype markers to show a tissue-level location and cell identity.

Still, its scale and evidential scope are specific:
- a few rare cells are found in a localized maternal scar;
- the cells are presumed fetal based on male origin and reproductive history;
- some express host-tissue-associated markers;
- no continuous organ, vascular system or gross anatomical structure links mother and child;
- no chain is traced from a grandmother through mother into child.

This is an example of **cellular incorporation across an individual boundary**, not whole-organism fusion or an anatomical body extending across generations.

### E. Place in the growing taxonomy

The microchimerism branch now distinguishes:
1. maternal-grandmother-origin DNA in a pregnant woman's PBMCs (Gammill et al. 2011, ledger §68);
2. grandmother-specific DNA in a subset of newborn cord-blood samples (Karlmark et al. 2021, ledger §67);
3. male presumed-fetal cells in maternal tissues, with histological evidence of differentiated cell phenotypes (Mahmood & O'Donoghue 2014, this section).

These studies measure different compartments and different generational directions. They should not be merged into a single directly traced lineage. The next useful comparison is a study that demonstrates fetal-derived cells in maternal marrow or multiple organs, ideally combining Y-FISH with tissue/cell markers and a well-defined obstetric history.

**Status:** Mahmood & O'Donoghue 2014 full indexed article text, methods, results and figure legends inspected. Direct high-resolution image validation of the FISH panels was not performed during this pass. Observational evidence supports tissue localization and marker expression, not causal wound-repair function. No cursor advance.


## 70. O'Donoghue et al. 2004: fetal-origin cells in maternal marrow and bone — focused source note

**Primary paper:** Keelin O'Donoghue et al., “Microchimerism in female bone marrow and bone decades after fetal mesenchymal stem-cell trafficking in pregnancy,” *The Lancet* 364(9429), 179–182 (July 2004), DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(04)16631-2. PubMed abstract: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15246731/.

The abstract reports male cells identified by XY-FISH in marrow-derived mesenchymal stem-cell preparations and rib sections from all women in the male-pregnancy group, versus none of the controls (9/9 vs 0/5; p=0.0005). The authors infer that fetal cells transferred into maternal blood can engraft maternal marrow and persist long term.

**Evidence boundary:** this session inspected the PubMed abstract, not the complete Lancet paper or its full methods/results. Y-chromosome detection supports male-origin material but, by itself, does not uniquely identify which pregnancy supplied each cell. The sample is small, and the abstract's interpretation of stem-cell identity and lifelong persistence should be treated as the authors' inference, not independently established here. The paper supports possible long-term cellular persistence in maternal marrow/bone; it does not show whole-organism continuity or a shared anatomical system between mother and child.

**Status:** bibliographic record and abstract inspected; full text not inspected. This is a bounded source note, not a synthesis of the entire microchimerism literature.

The adjacent ND Research Higher Dimensional Biology.txt files are condensed user-message exports, not substitutes for the full research conversation.

- HsH/DEVELOPMENT_FULL_CONVOS/SAT_CONVOS_23/Reboot Higher Dimensional Research (1).txt: consulted for broader mathematical/nLab/braid context only. Its endpoint is not the current biology Tock. Provenance-oriented discussion is background, not an active workstream.

### Findings carried forward

1. The subject has several distinct meanings that must remain separate: literal additional spatial dimensionality of living matter; higher-spatial vitalist or psychical vehicles; biological effective/fractal network dimensionality; and organisms treated as extended through time/lifetime. The latter two are not, by themselves, claims of an extra spatial axis.
2. Hinton is the strongest direct historical anchor so far. His 1904 treatment connects four-dimensional spatial geometry to organic right/left symmetry and possible microscopic movement/folding of living constituents. He explicitly treats the argument as inconclusive, does not claim to explain life by four-dimensional motion, and raises germ-structure and heredity difficulties. Keep his author's claims distinct from the workshop's SAT/H(s)H reading.
3. Whately Smith is a different historical strand. His 1920 account uses vitality, nervous-system and sensory language but proposes a speculative higher-dimensional vehicle associated with psychical survival; he expressly does not treat the 3D body as a section of a 4D whole. Do not merge his view with Hinton's.
4. Abbott's Flatland is a biological/world-building analogy, useful for dimensional perception and organismal-world construction, not a direct scientific proposal about actual 4D organisms.
5. The dimensional-menagerie literature is a separate, productive historical substream: biological examples (flat-dwellers, worms, etc.) are used to reason about dimensional perception and geometry. Trace the primary writers cited by Blacklock rather than treating the review abstract as the whole evidence.
6. Organism-as-process / physiological-time material adds another distinct comparison. Carrel and Woodger are relevant to temporally extended living organization, but this is not automatically the same as an extra spatial dimension or the user's histological 4D construction.
7. The biological record is now substantial, not empty. Existing ledger work covers symmetry and regeneration (including Alpheus and Hydroides), grafting and composite bodies, histological/vascular/neural continuity, siphonophore modularity, Botryllus fusion/chimerism, and human microchimerism. These are comparison cases for organismal boundaries, continuity and organization; none should be promoted into proof of extra spatial dimensionality.
8. The user's own 4D histological construction is a distinct direct-user model. Its proposed physical tissue contiguity, layered neural/muscular/skeletal/skin structure and temporal branching must not be conflated with historical authors or ordinary genetic inheritance. The 28 related image assets have only been metadata-inventoried; visual reconstruction and turn-to-diagram mapping remain open.
9. The dimensional-normalization paper remains a method companion (d), not the ontology and not a substitute for the biology/history synthesis. Its integration into the biological question remains unfinished.
10. The geometry-first category-theory introduction exists, but the substantive k reading programme is still future work. The dedicated spiritualist reading (s) remains deferred until after that programme; relevant historical references may still be read within h where needed.

The current job is to finish a bounded general reconnaissance, not to restart the ledger's already-developed biological case studies. Execute the remaining overview work in this order, adjusting only when a source result justifies it:

1. Obtain and read Carrel's two 1931 primary papers, “The New Cytology” and “Physiological Time”; check the quotations and context currently available only through secondary literature.

3. Read only the relevant Steiner 1905 organism/growth passages for immediate historical context; do not begin the deferred systematic spiritualist survey.

5. Trace the nineteenth-century primary writers behind the dimensional-menagerie account (Sylvester, Helmholtz, Clifford, Rodwell, Pearson); establish which biological analogies each actually uses.
6. Complete the German/Austrian and other-language press/repository overview, including the unconventional, speculative, personal-science and “weird/wacky” source families. Labels are discovery metadata, not evidence ratings.
7. Reconstruct the user's 4D histological diagrams from the 28 assets and direct-user turns, once image bytes are accessible; keep histological and cellular/chromosome sequences distinct.
8. Produce the compact synthesis required by §35: meaning taxonomy, historical chronology, traditions/actors, biological concepts, primary-source map, multilingual/repository coverage, and unresolved questions. Only when that overview is adequate should the Tock proceed through β, then ρ, then k.
